**Author:** Joseph D. Kirchner

**Date:** Working draft, September 2026

# Preface

This book began with one word in one verse. The second chapter of Genesis, in the version I first read it in, opened its account of the making of man with "Now no shrub of the field had yet appeared," and I read that "now" against the six days of the first chapter and asked what it was doing there: whether the second chapter continued the first or went back over it. The question became a reading, the reading became a set of essays, and the essays, written in 2024 and 2025, made claims about the Hebrew and about the versions that I had no means of testing. Those essays were drafted with the assistance of early language-model tools, and they carried what such tools carried then: summaries of what the Septuagint or the Vulgate or the King James "reads," taken on trust, some of them right and some of them wrong. This book replaces them.

What changed in 2026 is the shelf. The versions the essays had talked about were acquired as images of their pages, the Leningrad Codex, Swete's Septuagint, the Roman Vulgate of 1592, Coverdale's Bible of 1535, the Great Bible of 1540, the Geneva of 1560, the Bishops' of 1568, the King James of 1611 in facsimile, and, late in the work, four later printings of the Bishops' Bible and the copy of 1602 that the King James translators marked as they revised it, and every claim the essays had made was taken to the page and either confirmed or corrected there. The book that follows is written against the page and cites the leaf for every reading, and where the page went against the essays the book says so and says what the essays had wrong.

I do not read Hebrew, Greek or Latin. The Hebrew in this book is the Leningrad Codex as I have read its letters and points from the image; the Greek is Swete's page; the Latin is the 1592's; and the early English is what the copies print. I have set the translations of each period beside the originals and beside one another, and I have stated as undecided every reading that remains so. The readings of the Hebrew, and of the secretary hand in the translators' copy of 1602, are mine and await the review of those who read the languages and the hand. Where the book says a version "reads" something, it means the copy cited, at the leaf cited, and nothing else. The reading of the leaves was carried out with the assistance of machine tools working under my direction at every step, and the responsibility for every reading is mine.

Two companion volumes stand beside this one, and the reader should know what has been sent to each. The reading of Genesis 1–3 set out here was first made in the service of a philosophical framework I have set out elsewhere, and the framework is not argued in this book; the reading stands on the text and does not need it. And the doctrine of original sin, which the reading bears on at every step, is not answered here either; a second volume takes it up against Augustine, and this book's Conclusion says in one paragraph where that argument has to begin. What this book argues is what the sentences of Genesis 1–3 say, in the order they say it, and what the versions did with them, and nothing beyond that.

The title says what the book does. It is a reading, of three chapters of one book, and not a history of their interpretation. It is restorative because it restores the text's own order against the versions that rearranged it, and its own words against the versions that dispersed them, and because it finds the text, so restored, to say something other than what has been read into it. And it is of the Hebrew and its versions because the versions are not, in this book, obstacles between the reader and the text; they are witnesses, each of them faithful somewhere and unfaithful somewhere else, and the reader who knows where is nearer the Hebrew than the reader who has only one of them.

Joseph D. Kirchner
September 2026

# Introduction

In 1568 the Bible of the Church of England, the Bishops' Bible, set at the head of the second chapter of Genesis a summary of its contents, and two of its eleven lines read: "4 A repeatyng of thynges foretolde in the first," and "7 He rehearseth agayne the fashionyng of man."[^ni1] The lines stood in the folios the church printed in 1574 and 1578 and in the edition of 1575. By 1602 the Bishops' own printers had replaced them with a list of the chapter's contents that said nothing of the first chapter, and the King James translators, working from a copy of that printing, headed the chapter in 1611 "The maner of the creation."[^ni2] No English Bible since has told its reader, at the head of Genesis 2, that the second chapter goes over the first again. This book argues that it does; that the Hebrew says so in the shape of its sentences; and that the versions of the text, from the Greek of the third century before Christ to the English of the present, can be read by where they kept that shape and where they let it go.

### A. The Reading

The book's reading can be stated in two pages, and it is stated here once. The chapters that follow do not restate it; they take it verse by verse and set beside each verse what the witnesses did.

Genesis 1:1–2:3 tells the creation as six days and a rest. From its second verse to its end the Hebrew runs on the verb of sequence, "and God said … and there was … and God saw," and the Leningrad Codex, the oldest complete manuscript of the Hebrew Bible, leaves an open space at the end of each day. The first verse is a heading. The second, "and the earth was without form and void," is not an act but a state, and the Hebrew marks it as one by putting the noun and not the verb after its connective. The days that follow are told in one order, and the sixth ends with a man and a woman made in the image of God and the whole judged very good. The seventh is rest. Nothing in these verses says how the man and the woman were made, or what the image is, or what "very good" contained.

At 2:4 the codex leaves a gap across a line and a half, and the text begins again with a heading, "these are the generations of the heavens and of the earth." What follows is the second telling. It does not go on from the seventh day; it goes back to the sixth, and tells the making of the man and the woman in the detail the first telling left out. It opens, as the first telling did, on a state: no plant of the field yet, no rain yet, no man to till the ground, and a mist going up from the earth. Then, in a chain of verbs of sequence, the LORD God forms the man of dust from the ground and breathes into him; plants a garden; makes the trees grow; sets the man in the garden; and commands him, alone and in the singular, not to eat of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. The text turns aside for five verses to describe the rivers, in clauses that describe and do not narrate, and then continues: the LORD God judges that the man's being alone is not good, forms the beasts and the birds, and brings them to the man, who names them and finds among them no help. He builds the woman from the man's side and brings her to him; the man names her and speaks a law, that a man shall cleave to his wife; and the two are naked and not ashamed. Every act in this chain is told as following the one before. The garden is planted after the man is formed; the trees grow after; the animals are formed after; the woman is built after them all. The woman is not present when the commandment is given and is never said to hear it.

The third chapter opens without a break in the codex and with a scene-clause in the text: "now the serpent was more subtle than any beast of the field." The serpent speaks to the woman, in the plural, of a commandment she did not hear, and tells her what will happen if she eats: her eyes will be opened, and she will be as God, or as gods, knowing good and evil. The book reads the serpent's words as the first account of the knowledge of good and evil that either human being hears from anyone, and as true in every particular the text allows the reader to test. The woman sees, takes and eats, and gives to her husband with her, and he eats. Their eyes are opened; they know they are naked; they hide. God calls the man first and examines him on the commandment; the man blames the woman and, through her, God; God examines the woman on the act, and she blames the serpent. The serpent is not examined. The sentences fall in the order the examination set: the serpent, "because thou hast done this"; the woman, with no "because"; the man, "because thou hast hearkened." The serpent is cursed in his person and sentenced to eat dust; the woman is sentenced to pain and to a husband who will rule; the man's ground, not the man, is cursed, and he is sentenced to labour and to return to the dust he was made of. The man names the woman; God clothes them; and God says, of the man, "behold, the man is become as one of us, to know good and evil," and sends him out to till the ground he was taken from, with the way to the tree of life guarded and not destroyed. The next verse of Genesis opens a new scene.

The reading is this. The first telling and the second are one narrative told twice, the second being the sixth day in detail, and the rest of 2:1–3 follows in time everything that 2:4–3:24 narrates. The order of the second telling is the order of the events: the man first, then the garden and its trees, then the animals, then the woman. The commandment was given to the man alone; the woman heard of it from the serpent, whose account was true; the man was present at the eating and ate with knowledge the woman never had. The image of God announced at 1:26 is the likeness God declares achieved at 3:22, and its content is the knowledge of good and evil; the sending out is not the undoing of the creation but the beginning of the life the creation was for, on the ground the man was made from. And the woman's subjection, which the tradition has read into 3:16, is not in the Hebrew of that verse; it was written into the text by the Latin, into the printed text in the translators' italic by the Geneva, and into the margin by the King James.

### B. The Four Findings

Four findings at the page carry the reading, and each is the book's own, made in the copies the Method describes.

The first is the shape of the connective. Biblical Hebrew joins its clauses with one letter, and the letter does two things depending on the word it is attached to: before a verb it continues the sequence; before a noun it steps out of the sequence to set a scene or turn against what came before. Seven verses between 1:1 and 4:1 open in the second way, and the second telling's acts open in the first. The Septuagint marked the second shape with δέ at six of the seven; the Vulgate with *autem*, *sed* and *vero* at five; the English Bibles of 1535 to 1568 between them at all but two; the King James at two, and printed "And" at the other five, where its predecessors had marked them.[^ni3] The versions divide at exactly the places the rule marks, and the order of the second telling, which every act's verb of sequence states, is the thing the rule protects.

The second is the heading of 1568, with which this Introduction opened: a Bible of the English church stated in its own summary that the second chapter repeats the first and rehearses again the fashioning of man, and the statement disappeared from the English Bible not by the King James translators' decision but before them, in the Bishops' own printings, so that the translators of 1611 never saw it in the copy they revised.[^ni4]

The third is a word in the Greek. At 2:9 and 2:19, where the trees grow and the animals are formed after the man, the Septuagint adds ἔτι, "further," which the Hebrew does not have: the oldest translation of Genesis read the order of the second telling as the order of the events and wrote a word to make sure its readers did too. The Vulgate, centuries later, moved the planting of the garden before the man with a pluperfect and the forming of the animals into the background with an ablative absolute, and every modern English version that reads "Now the LORD God had planted" stands with Jerome and against the Greek and the Hebrew.[^ni5]

The fourth is a word in the Hebrew. The man is made of dust (2:7), the serpent is sentenced to eat dust (3:14), and the man is sentenced to return to dust (3:19), and the word is one word, עָפָר, at all three places. The Greek collapsed it into the word for earth; the Latin dissolved it into three words, mud, earth and dust; the first English Bible followed the Latin; and the Great Bible of 1540 was the first version in any language on this book's shelf to put one word at all three places, so that an English reader since 1540 has been able to see what a Greek or Latin reader could not, that the serpent is sentenced to eat what the man is made of.[^ni6]

### C. What the Book Does Not Argue

The book argues what the sentences of Genesis 1–3 say, in the order they say it, and what the versions did with them. It does not argue the philosophical system the author has set out elsewhere, though the reading was first made in its service; the system is not needed to make the reading and is not invoked here. It does not argue the doctrine of original sin or answer Augustine; a companion volume does, and the reader who wants to know what follows from this reading for the doctrine is sent there, with one paragraph in the Conclusion to say where the door is. It does not argue the age of the earth, the length of the days, or the relation of the narrative to natural history. It does not argue the authorship or date of the text, or the question of its sources, and it does not need to: the reading is of the text as the codex has it and as the versions received it, whoever wrote it and however it came together. And it does not decide the readings that the Hebrew leaves open, the number of the serpent's "as gods," the meaning of the woman's "desire," the sense of the serpent's "subtle"; where the witnesses divide and the Hebrew does not decide, the book records the division and reads on.

### D. The Order of the Parts

The Method comes first, in three sections cited by letter, because everything after it depends on a rule about the Hebrew clause and on a discipline about the page, and both are stated once. Part 1 reads the first telling, 1:1–2:3, as the frame that supplies the second telling with its order and its words. Part 2 reads the second telling from its heading to the woman, 2:4–25, one chain of acts in one order, and shows the Method's rule working at the two verses where the versions divided. Part 3 reads the third chapter, 3:1–24, as the knowledge: the serpent's account of it, the eating, the examination, the sentences, and God's declaration that the likeness is achieved. Part 4 reads the versions as wholes, the Greek, the Latin, the English before 1611, and the King James and after, in the vocabulary the Method fixed, and it comes last because a version can be judged only after the text it carried has been read. The Conclusion states what a reader who follows the shape of each clause finds, and hands the doctrine to the companion volume. The Appendix is tables: the witnesses and their leaves, the connective at every clause the rule marks in every witness, the three words for the ground at their verses, the terms and their history, and where the material of the author's earlier essays now stands.

Every chapter that reads a span of verses closes with a lettered section on the witnesses at that span, and every claim about a version cites the leaf of the copy read. The reader who wishes to check the book against its sources will find the leaf in the note, and the Method states how far each witness was examined and what the author can and cannot read.

# Method

### A. The Page Before the Version

Every reading in this book was made at a page, and the book states here once what that means, so that the chapters may cite the page and not restate the rule.

The Hebrew text is the Leningrad Codex, the oldest complete manuscript of the Hebrew Bible, read at the image of its leaves; Genesis 1:1–4:12 stands on four of them.[^ma1] The book prints the Hebrew with the points the codex carries, and the reading of the consonants and the points is the author's from the image. No printed Hebrew Bible stands between the reader and the manuscript in this book: where a printed edition is mentioned it is mentioned as a printed edition, and the codex is the text.

Every version is read at the page of a copy, or at the image of that page, and is quoted as that copy prints it. The Septuagint is quoted from Swete's Cambridge edition, whose Genesis is the text of Codex Alexandrinus, and the book says so wherever it says "the Septuagint"; the Vulgate from the Roman edition of 1592, the first printing of the Clementine text; Coverdale's Bible from a copy of 1535, the Great Bible from a copy of 1540, the Geneva from a copy of 1560, the Bishops' from a copy of 1568, the King James from the Oxford facsimile of the first printing of 1611.[^ma2] The early English is given with its spelling, its abbreviations and its marks of supplement as they stand on the leaf, "vnto," "ȳ," "(he made)," "[shalbe]," because the marks are part of what the translators said; the long s alone is modernised. Where a letter or a word on the leaf could not be read with confidence the book says so at the place.

Translations are set beside their originals and never beside one another alone. A claim that "the Vulgate reads" or "the Geneva reads" is a claim about the leaf cited, and the leaf is cited by the unit the copy gives it: the codex by page, the Greek and Latin by printed page, Coverdale and the Great Bible by opening, the Geneva and the Bishops' by leaf, the 1611 by the facsimile's page, the Bodleian's annotated Bishops' Bible by canvas. Nothing in the book rests on a later edition's summary of what an earlier version says, and where the book's predecessor essays rested on such summaries, or on a modern version quoted as if it were the text, the book has gone to the page and reports what it found, including where the finding is against the essays.

The English text of record is the King James of 1611, quoted as printed, and the English Bibles before it are read beside it in the order of their age. The Septuagint and the Vulgate are the two ancient witnesses. The versions of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries are witnesses to the modern reader's text and to nothing else; the book quotes none of them as its own text, and chapter 4.4 states how the thirteen it consulted were read.

[^ma1]: [Leningrad Codex (St Petersburg, National Library of Russia, Firkovich B 19 A, AD 1008), pp. 7–10 of the page images on the shelf: 1:1–1:26 (p. 7), 1:26–2:19 (p. 8), 2:19–3:18 (p. 9), 3:18–4:12 (p. 10)](cite:ma1/1); three columns to the page, read from the right. The page numbers are those of the image set and not folio numbers.
[^ma2]: [The copies, with the leaves that carry Genesis 1–3, are tabulated in § C below. Swete's edition prints the Vaticanus text where that codex survives and supplies its lost first forty-six chapters of Genesis from Alexandrinus, with the Cotton Genesis and the Bodleian Genesis in the apparatus](cite:ma2/1); the book's "Septuagint" is therefore a fifth-century manuscript's text in a 1901 edition, and chapter 4.1 § A states what follows from that.

### B. The Connective and the Clause

Every claim this book makes about the order of events in Genesis 2 rests on one feature of Hebrew narrative, and the feature is stated here once so that the chapters may cite it rather than restate it.

Biblical Hebrew joins its clauses with a single letter, vav (וְ), prefixed to the first word of the clause. "And" is the ordinary English for it. The King James Version prints "and" more than two hundred times in Genesis 1–3 and opens sixty-three of the eighty verses with it.[^mb1] But the letter does not do one thing, and the reader is not left to guess which thing it is doing. What it does is decided by the word it is attached to.

When the vav is prefixed to a finite verb, the clause continues the narrative. Grammarians call the form the vav-consecutive, or *wayyiqtol*; it is the backbone of Hebrew storytelling, and each clause built on it reports the next event after the one before: "and God said … and there was light … and God saw … and God divided." The English that renders the form is "and" or "then." The two are interchangeable, and the choice between them is a matter of English rhythm, not of Hebrew grammar. In Genesis 1 every verse after the second opens this way. In Genesis 2 the acts of the second telling open this way: the LORD God formed the man (2:7, וַיִּצֶר), planted a garden (2:8, וַיִּטַּע), made the trees grow (2:9, וַיַּצְמַח), formed the beasts (2:19, וַיִּצֶר), caused a deep sleep to fall (2:21, וַיַּפֵּל), and built the woman (2:22, וַיִּבֶן). Each is told as following the one before it.

When the vav is prefixed to a noun or a pronoun instead, so that the verb is pushed back from the head of the clause, the clause does something else. It steps out of the sequence. It sets a scene, states a standing condition, or turns against what came before, and the English that renders it is "now" or "but," not "and." Genesis 1:2 is the first instance in the Bible, וְהָאָרֶץ הָיְתָה, "now the earth was"; Genesis 3:1 is the best known, וְהַנָּחָשׁ הָיָה, "now the serpent was." Seven verses between 1:1 and 4:1, the span this book reads, open this way with a verb following the noun: 1:2, 2:5, 2:6, 2:10, 3:1, 3:17 and 4:1. Four more clauses, in the description of the rivers, open with vav and a noun and have no verb at all: וּזֲהַב הָאָרֶץ (2:12), וְשֵׁם־הַנָּהָר (2:13), וְשֵׁם הַנָּהָר (2:14) and, closing the same verse, וְהַנָּהָר הָרְבִיעִי. They describe rather than narrate, and the book keeps them apart from the seven. The same shape occurs inside a verse, where it pairs or contrasts two clauses: "and the darkness he called night" (1:5), "and the gathering of the waters he called seas" (1:10), "but for Adam there was not found a help" (2:20).[^mb2] A third case completes the rule. A verse that opens with no vav at all is not continuing a clause but beginning a section, and Genesis 1–3 has three such openings: 1:1, 2:4 (אֵלֶּה תוֹלְדוֹת, "these are the generations") and 3:16 (אֶל־הָאִשָּׁה אָמַר, "unto the woman he said"). The Leningrad Codex leaves a visible gap in the column before the second and the third.[^mb3]

The rule is therefore a rule of syntax, not of vocabulary. The word does not change; its place in the clause does. A translator who reads the word alone must choose between "and" and "now" by taste. A translator who reads the clause has the choice made for him. The two ancient versions read the clause. The Septuagint renders the verb-first clauses with καί and the noun-first verse-openers with δέ, the Greek particle of a new scene, at six of the seven (2:5 is the exception), and it reserves δέ for that use: the in-verse pairs of 1:5 and 1:10 it renders with καί, and the contrast of 2:20 with τε.[^mb4] The Vulgate answers the Greek with *autem*, *sed* and *vero* at five of the seven (2:5 and 2:10 are its exceptions) and at the mid-verse clause of 2:20, and varies its sequence clauses freely with *-que*, *igitur*, *ergo* and *quoque*.[^mb5] At the four verbless clauses of the rivers the two versions divided the marking between them: the Septuagint has δέ at the gold (2:12) and at the fourth river and καί at the second and third; the Vulgate has *Et* at the gold and the second river and *vero* and *autem* at the third and fourth.[^mb7] The first English Bibles kept the range. The King James of 1611 marked two of the seven, "But" at 2:6 and "Now" at 3:1, and printed "And" at the other five; at 2:20 it has "but for Adam," as the Geneva had before it.[^mb6] The chapters below record what each version did at each clause; this section records only the rule by which their choices can be judged.

Two consequences follow, and the book relies on both. First, a "now" placed at a verb-first clause is not a translation of the connective but an interpretation of the story, and where it is joined to a pluperfect, "Now the LORD God had planted" (2:8), "Now the LORD God had formed" (2:19), it lifts an event out of the sequence in which the Hebrew set it. Second, the absence of a connective is itself a datum. Where the Hebrew opens a sentence without vav, the text is beginning again, and a version that supplies "and" there has closed a seam the text left open.

Nothing in this rule is peculiar to this book; it is the grammar of the language as any grammar of it states. What is the book's own is the application: reading the two tellings of Genesis 1–3 clause by clause under it, and finding that the versions divide at exactly the places the rule marks.

Coverdale (1535): "But" at 2:6 and 3:1, "Morouer" at 4:1. Great Bible (1540): "The earth was" (1:2), "But the serpent" (3:1). Geneva (1560): "But" at 2:6, "Now" at 3:1, "Also" at 3:17, "Afterwarde" at 4:1, "but for Adám" (2:20). Bishops' (1568): "But" at 2:6. The verse-by-verse table is Appendix B.](cite:mb6/2)
[^mb7]: [Swete, vol. I, p. 3 (2:12 τὸ δὲ χρυσίον; 2:13 καὶ ὄνομα τῷ ποταμῷ τῷ δευτέρῳ; 2:14 καὶ ὁ ποταμὸς ὁ τρίτος … ὁ δὲ ποταμὸς ὁ τέταρτος)](cite:mb7/1); [Vulgate 1592, p. 2 (*Et aurum terræ illius*; *Et nomen fluuij secundi*; *Nomen vero fluminis tertij*; *Fluuius autem quartus*).](cite:mb7/2)

### C. Degree of Examination

Not every witness in this book was examined to the same depth, and the book states the depth at which each was read so that no claim carries more weight than its source will bear. Four degrees are distinguished.

The first is the image of a manuscript. Only the Hebrew was read so: the Leningrad Codex at its own leaves, with the scribe's gaps, his columns and his points before the reader.

The second is the image of a printed page of the version itself. The Vulgate of 1592, Coverdale, the Great Bible, the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 were read so, leaf by leaf, for every verse of Genesis 1–3 the book cites, and the Bishops' Bibles of 1574, 1575, 1577 and 1578, on microfilm, at the argument to the second chapter only.[^mc1] The Bodleian's copy of the Bishops' Bible of 1602, annotated by the King James translators, was read at the image of its Genesis leaves; the printed text is of this second degree, and the manuscript annotations are of the first, read in a hand the author has not been trained in, and the book marks every reading of them as the author's own.

The third is the image of a modern edition standing for an ancient text. The Septuagint was read so, in Swete's edition of 1901, whose Genesis is the text of Codex Alexandrinus with the readings of two other early manuscripts in its apparatus; no codex image of the Greek Genesis 1–3 was available, and the book's Greek claims are claims about Swete's page.

The fourth is edition text without a page: the thirteen public-domain English versions of the nineteenth, twentieth and twenty-first centuries, read as verse-keyed text files. They are cited as editions and as witnesses to the modern reader's text only; nothing in the book's reading rests on them.

Outside these four stand the versions the book names but did not read: the New International Version, the English Standard Version and the New American Standard Bible, which are in copyright and are cited only as the book's predecessor essays quoted them, and only to say what the essays quoted.

| Vulgate | *Biblia Sacra Vulgatæ editionis* (Rome: Typographia Apostolica Vaticana, 1592) | printed pp. 1 (1:1–26), 2 (1:26–3:4), 3 (3:5–4) | Printed page |

# Part 1 — The First Telling: Genesis 1:1–2:3

## 1.1 The Six Days as the Frame (1:1–25)

The first telling is the frame within which the second is set, and the book reads it first for two reasons. It establishes the order of the works, the order the second telling will revisit; and it shows the Hebrew connective in its plainest use, so that the reader can hear the difference when the second telling begins to use it otherwise.

> **Genesis 1:1–5 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **1** בְּרֵאשִׁית בָּרָא אֱלֹהִים אֵת הַשָּׁמַיִם וְאֵת הָאָרֶץ׃
> **2** וְהָאָרֶץ הָיְתָה תֹהוּ וָבֹהוּ וְחֹשֶׁךְ עַל־פְּנֵי תְהוֹם וְרוּחַ אֱלֹהִים מְרַחֶפֶת עַל־פְּנֵי הַמָּיִם׃
> **3** וַיֹּאמֶר אֱלֹהִים יְהִי אוֹר וַיְהִי־אוֹר׃
> **4** וַיַּרְא אֱלֹהִים אֶת־הָאוֹר כִּי־טוֹב וַיַּבְדֵּל אֱלֹהִים בֵּין הָאוֹר וּבֵין הַחֹשֶׁךְ׃
> **5** וַיִּקְרָא אֱלֹהִים לָאוֹר יוֹם וְלַחֹשֶׁךְ קָרָא לָיְלָה וַיְהִי־עֶרֶב וַיְהִי־בֹקֶר יוֹם אֶחָד׃

> **Genesis 1:1–5 (1611)**
> **1** In the beginning God created the Heauen, and the Earth.
> **2** And the earth was without forme, and voyd, and darkenesse was vpon the face of the deepe: and the Spirit of God mooued vpon the face of the waters.
> **3** And God said, Let there be light: and there was light.
> **4** And God saw the light, that it was good: and God diuided the light from the darkenesse.
> **5** And God called the light, Day, and the darknesse he called Night: and the euening and the morning were the first day.[^nb11a]

### A. The Heading and the Scene (1:1–2)

The Bible opens without a connective. בְּרֵאשִׁית בָּרָא אֱלֹהִים, "in the beginning God created," is a heading of the same kind as the אֵלֶּה תוֹלְדוֹת of 2:4, a sentence that begins a section rather than continuing one, and the book treats the two as the two headings of the two tellings.[^nb11b] What the first heading announces is the whole: the heavens and the earth. What the second will announce is the same whole, in the other order, "the earth and the heavens," told again.

The second verse is the first scene-clause in the Bible, and the book has used it in the Method as the type of its kind. וְהָאָרֶץ הָיְתָה, "and the earth was": the vav is prefixed to the noun, the verb follows, and the clause does not report an act but describes a state, the earth unformed and empty, dark, with the רוּחַ אֱלֹהִים, the Spirit or wind or breath of God, moving on the waters. The Septuagint marked it, ἡ δὲ γῆ ἦν ἀόρατος καὶ ἀκατασκεύαστος, "now the earth was unseen and unfurnished"; the Vulgate marked it, *Terra autem erat inanis & vacua*; the Great Bible of 1540 marked it by dropping the connective altogether, "The earth was voyde and emptye," which is the English way of setting a scene when the language has no particle to do it.[^nb11c] Coverdale ran it on from the first verse with a small "and," "and ȳ earth was voyde and emptie"; the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 print "And the earth was," and the 1611's "And" is the first of the five places in Genesis 1–4 where the King James translators chose "And" at a noun-first clause their predecessors or their sources had marked.[^nb11d] Every version but Coverdale's has "Spirit" for רוּחַ, and the book has shown at 3:8 what became of the same word when it stood in a phrase the translators could not make a Spirit of.[^nb11e]

### B. The Days as Sequence (1:3–25)

From the third verse to the end of the chapter the Hebrew is the vav of sequence without exception at the head of a verse. וַיֹּאמֶר, "and he said"; וַיַּרְא, "and he saw"; וַיַּבְדֵּל, "and he divided"; וַיִּקְרָא, "and he called"; וַיַּעַשׂ, "and he made"; וַיִּבְרָא, "and he created"; וַיְבָרֶךְ, "and he blessed." Each act is the next after the one before, and the day is closed by a formula that is itself two sequence verbs, וַיְהִי־עֶרֶב וַיְהִי־בֹקֶר, "and there was evening and there was morning." Within a verse the Hebrew does turn: at 1:5 וְלַחֹשֶׁךְ קָרָא לָיְלָה, "and the darkness he called night," and at 1:10 וּלְמִקְוֵה הַמַּיִם קָרָא יַמִּים, "and the gathering of the waters he called seas," a noun is fronted to pair the second naming with the first, and the Septuagint, which reserves its δέ for a clause that opens a scene, keeps καί at both.[^nb11f] The first telling has one scene, at 1:2, and after it everything is event.

The Leningrad Codex marks the days as the text does not. At the end of each day's formula the scribe leaves the rest of the line empty and begins the next day on a fresh line: after 1:5, 1:8, 1:13, 1:19 and 1:23, five open spaces that divide the first six days from one another, and none within a day.[^nb11g] The printed Bibles found their own ways to show the divisions. The Geneva and the Bishops' number the days in the margin; the 1611 marks the opening of a day with a pilcrow at 1:6, 1:9, 1:14 and 1:24, though not at 1:20; the Vulgate of 1592 runs the chapter as one paragraph with the verse numbers in the margin.[^nb11h] The codex's gaps are the oldest of these devices and the only one in the text's own hand.

The versions did not level the chapter's connective as far as the English reader might suppose, and the differences among them are worth setting out once, because Part 4 will return to them. The Septuagint has καί at every verse but 1:2. The Vulgate varies the seven speeches of God with seven different openings, *Dixitque* (1:3), *Dixit quoque* (1:6, 1:24), *Dixit vero* (1:9), *Et ait* (1:11, 1:26), *Dixit autem* (1:14), *Dixit etiam* (1:20), and its other acts with the enclitic *-que*, *Appellauitque*, *Vocauitque*, *Fecitque*, *Creauitque*, *Benedixitque*, so that no two consecutive Latin verses begin alike.[^nb11i] Coverdale closes each day with "Then of the euenynge and mornynge was made the first daye" and opens the acts that follow a speech with "Then God made," "Then God deuyded"; the Great Bible closes each day with "The euenynge also and the mornyng."[^nb11j] The Geneva has the widest range in English, "Then God said" (1:3, 1:11), "Againe God said" (1:6), "God said againe" (1:9), "Then God made" (1:7), "God then made" (1:16), "Afterwarde God said" (1:20), "Moreouer God said" (1:24), "Furthermore God said" (1:26), and closes each day "So the euening and the morning were."[^nb11k] The Bishops' has "And" at every verse from 1:3 to 1:24 and drops the connective at 1:25 and 1:26, "God made," "God saide"; the 1611 has "And" at every verse from 1:3 to 1:26 without a break.[^nb11l]

None of this is a fault in any version, and the book does not call it one. In the first telling the Hebrew's connective is the vav of sequence at every verse, and "And" renders it exactly; the Geneva's "Againe" and "Afterwarde" and the Vulgate's *quoque* and *etiam* are English and Latin variety laid over a Hebrew that has none. The King James's "And … and … and" in Genesis 1 is the Hebrew's "and … and … and," and the reader who has learned from this book that the 1611 levelled the connective should learn here where it did not need to. The levelling that matters is at the noun-first clauses, and the first telling has only one of them.

### C. The Third Day, and the Second Telling's Starting Point (1:9–13)

> **Genesis 1:11–12 (1611)**
> **11** And God said, Let the earth bring foorth grasse, the herbe yeelding seed, and the fruit tree, yeelding fruit after his kinde, whose seed is in it selfe, vpon the earth: and it was so.
> **12** And the earth brought foorth grasse, and herbe yeelding seed after his kinde, and the tree yeelding fruit, whose seed was in it selfe, after his kinde: and God saw that it was good.[^nb11m]

The third day has two works. The first is the gathering of the waters and the appearing of the dry land (1:9–10); the second is the bringing forth of vegetation, grass, herb and fruit tree (1:11–12). Between them the day is not closed; the formula of evening and morning comes only at 1:13. The second telling's scene, at 2:5, is an earth with dry land and water and no shrub or herb, and the book has argued in chapter 2.1 that this places the second telling's opening between the two works of the third day. The man is formed on the dry land of 1:9–10, and the trees that grow around him at 2:8–9 are the vegetation of 1:11–12, told from where he stands. Nothing in the first telling contradicts this; the first telling reports that the earth brought forth vegetation on the third day and does not say at what hour of it, or whether anything else was on the earth when it did.

The word of the third day that the second telling will use is זֶרַע, seed. מַזְרִיעַ זֶרַע, "seeding seed," and אֲשֶׁר זַרְעוֹ־בוֹ, "whose seed is in it," name the power of the vegetation to reproduce itself, and the word will return at 3:15 as the seed of the woman and the seed of the serpent. Every version kept it: σπέρμα, *semen*, "sede," "seed," with the 1611 confessing the Hebrew's doubling in its margin at 1:29, "Hebr. seeding seed."[^nb11n] The first telling gives the plants the power to continue; the first telling's blessing on the living creatures at 1:22 and on the man and woman at 1:28 will give it to them in a verb, "be fruitful and multiply"; and the second telling will make the woman's seed the bearer of the enmity with the serpent.

### D. The Fourth Day, and a Verb the Hebrew Does Not Have (1:14–19)

The lights of the fourth day are made to divide the day from the night and to be for signs and seasons, and the verse that reports their making has a phrase without a verb. וַיַּעַשׂ אֱלֹהִים אֶת־שְׁנֵי הַמְּאֹרֹת הַגְּדֹלִים … וְאֵת הַכּוֹכָבִים: "and God made the two great lights … and the stars." The stars hang at the end of the verse as a third object with no verb of their own, and the translators had to decide whether to leave them so or to supply one. The Septuagint left them, καὶ τοὺς ἀστέρας; the Vulgate, *& stellas*.[^nb11o] The English supplied a verb, and three of the English Bibles told the reader they had done so: Coverdale and the Great Bible print "and (he made) starres also," the verb in parentheses, and the Bishops' "and [he made] starres also," the verb in the brackets the Bishops' used for its supplements; the Geneva has "he made also ȳ starres" and the 1611 "he made the starres also," both without a mark.[^nb11p] The book notes the verse because the predecessor essay made something of it, reading "the stars also" as a subordination of the stars; the Hebrew has no verb to subordinate with, and the versions that marked their supplement were the more faithful.

### E. The Fifth and Sixth Days: Living Souls, and the Creeping Things (1:20–25)

> **Genesis 1:24–25 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **24** וַיֹּאמֶר אֱלֹהִים תּוֹצֵא הָאָרֶץ נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה לְמִינָהּ בְּהֵמָה וָרֶמֶשׂ וְחַיְתוֹ־אֶרֶץ לְמִינָהּ וַיְהִי־כֵן׃
> **25** וַיַּעַשׂ אֱלֹהִים אֶת־חַיַּת הָאָרֶץ לְמִינָהּ וְאֶת־הַבְּהֵמָה לְמִינָהּ וְאֵת כָּל־רֶמֶשׂ הָאֲדָמָה לְמִינֵהוּ וַיַּרְא אֱלֹהִים כִּי־טוֹב׃

> **Genesis 1:24–25 (1611)**
> **24** And God said, Let the earth bring forth the liuing creature after his kinde, cattell, and creeping thing, and beast of the earth after his kinde: and it was so.
> **25** And God made the beast of the earth after his kinde, and cattell after their kinde, and euery thing that creepeth vpon the earth, after his kinde: and God saw that it was good.[^nb11q]

Two words of the fifth and sixth days are words the second telling will use of the man and the serpent, and the book records them here so that the reader has them when they return.

The first is נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה, "living soul." It is what the waters bring forth on the fifth day (1:20), what the earth brings forth on the sixth (1:24), what every creature that moves is at 1:30, what the man becomes at 2:7 and what every creature the man names is at 2:19. The phrase is one phrase in the Hebrew at all five places. The Septuagint has ψυχὴ ζῶσα at all five and the Vulgate *anima viuens*; Coverdale has "creatures that moue and haue life" at 1:20 but "lyuynge soules" at 1:24 and "a lyuynge soule" at 2:7.[^nb11r] The Great Bible of 1540 gave the animals "mouynge creature that hath lyfe" (1:20) and "lyuing creature" (1:24), and kept "a lyuing soule" for the man, and every English Bible after it has done the same, so that the reader of the King James meets "the liuing creature" here and "a liuing soule" at 2:7 and cannot see that they are the same words.[^nb11s] The Geneva put the Hebrew in its margin at 1:20, "Ebr. the soule of life"; the 1611 at 1:20, "Heb. soule," and at 1:30, "Hebr. a liuing soule."[^nb11t] Chapter 2.2 has drawn the consequence; here the book records only that the division between creature and soul, which the English reader takes from these verses, is English and dates to 1540.

The second word is רֶמֶשׂ, the creeping thing, with its verb רָמַשׂ, to creep or move about. It is one of the three classes the earth brings forth on the sixth day, בְּהֵמָה וָרֶמֶשׂ וְחַיְתוֹ־אֶרֶץ, "cattle and creeping thing and beast of the earth," and at 1:26 and 1:28 the man is given dominion over it, הָרֶמֶשׂ הָרֹמֵשׂ, "the creeping thing that creepeth." The serpent of chapter 3 is one of these, and the book has shown in chapter 3.3 that his sentence does not use the word: he is sentenced to go on his belly and eat dust, and the word that binds his sentence to the rest of the narrative is dust, not creeping. The versions render the class variously and none of them wrongly: the Septuagint ἑρπετά, the Vulgate *reptilia* and *reptile terræ*, Coverdale and the Great Bible "wormes," the Geneva "that which crepeth," the Bishops' "worme," the 1611 "creeping thing" and "euery thing that creepeth."[^nb11u] The 1611 adds two margins that show its translators watching the root: at 1:20, where the text has "mouing creature," the margin has "Or, creeping," and at 1:28, where the text has "euery liuing thing that mooueth," the margin has "Heb. creepeth."[^nb11v]

### F. What the Frame Establishes

Six days, told in one order, with one scene-clause at the head and a verb of sequence at every verse after it: light, the firmament, the dry land and then the vegetation, the lights, the creatures of the water and the air, the creatures of the land. The codex divides the days with open spaces and the versions with margins and pilcrows, and no version disturbs the order. The words the frame supplies to the second telling are three: seed, which the plants have and the woman will have; living soul, which the animals and the man are alike; and creeping thing, the class the serpent belongs to and the word his sentence does not use. The next chapter reads the sixth day's last work and the seventh day's rest, which are the first telling's account of what the second telling will spend twenty-three verses on.

## 1.2 The Image and the Mandate (1:26–31)

> **Genesis 1:26–28 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **26** וַיֹּאמֶר אֱלֹהִים נַעֲשֶׂה אָדָם בְּצַלְמֵנוּ כִּדְמוּתֵנוּ וְיִרְדּוּ בִדְגַת הַיָּם וּבְעוֹף הַשָּׁמַיִם וּבַבְּהֵמָה וּבְכָל־הָאָרֶץ וּבְכָל־הָרֶמֶשׂ הָרֹמֵשׂ עַל־הָאָרֶץ׃
> **27** וַיִּבְרָא אֱלֹהִים אֶת־הָאָדָם בְּצַלְמוֹ בְּצֶלֶם אֱלֹהִים בָּרָא אֹתוֹ זָכָר וּנְקֵבָה בָּרָא אֹתָם׃
> **28** וַיְבָרֶךְ אֹתָם אֱלֹהִים וַיֹּאמֶר לָהֶם אֱלֹהִים פְּרוּ וּרְבוּ וּמִלְאוּ אֶת־הָאָרֶץ וְכִבְשֻׁהָ וּרְדוּ בִּדְגַת הַיָּם וּבְעוֹף הַשָּׁמַיִם וּבְכָל־חַיָּה הָרֹמֶשֶׂת עַל־הָאָרֶץ׃

> **Genesis 1:26–28 (1611)**
> **26** And God said, Let vs make man in our Image, after our likenesse: and let them haue dominion ouer the fish of the sea, and ouer the foule of the aire, and ouer the cattell, and ouer all the earth, and ouer euery creeping thing that creepeth vpon the earth.
> **27** So God created man in his owne Image, in the Image of God created hee him; male and female created hee them.
> **28** And God blessed them, and God said vnto them, Be fruitfull, and multiply, and replenish the earth, and subdue it, and haue dominion ouer the fish of the sea, and ouer the foule of the aire, and ouer euery liuing thing that mooueth vpon the earth.[^nb12a]

### A. "Let Us Make" (1:26)

The last work of the sixth day is announced in a way no other work was. Every other work was commanded, "let there be," "let the earth bring forth"; this one is deliberated, נַעֲשֶׂה אָדָם, "let us make man," in the first person and in the plural. The plural is the plural of the word for God, אֱלֹהִים, which has governed a singular verb in every verse of the chapter until now; here, speaking of the making of man, God speaks as "us," and the pattern of the image is "our image, after our likeness," בְּצַלְמֵנוּ כִּדְמוּתֵנוּ. The book has met this "us" twice already, at 3:5, where the serpent promised the woman she would be כֵּאלֹהִים, and at 3:22, where God said the man had become כְּאַחַד מִמֶּנּוּ, "as one of us." The three verses are one sentence spoken three times, by God as intention, by the serpent as promise, and by God as fact.

The versions kept the plural, and the two that had a word for it added it elsewhere. The Septuagint has ποιήσωμεν ἄνθρωπον κατ᾿ εἰκόνα ἡμετέραν καὶ καθ᾿ ὁμοίωσιν, the Vulgate *Faciamus hominem ad imaginem, & similitudinem nostram*; both, as chapter 2.3 recorded, carried the same plural into 2:18, where the Hebrew has "I will make," so that in Greek and Latin the making of the man and the making of his help are announced in the same voice.[^nb12b] The English Bibles have "let vs make" without exception.[^nb12c] What the plural means the versions did not say in their texts, and the Reformation Bibles said in their margins, in two ways. The Geneva reads it as deliberation: God "cōmanded the water and the earth to bring forthe other creatures: but of man he saith, Let vs make: signifying ȳ God taketh counsel with his wisdome & vertue, purposing to make an excellent worke aboue all the rest of his creacion."[^nb12d] The Bishops' reads it as the Trinity: "One God, and three persons. Coloss. ii. b."[^nb12e] The book does not decide between them, as it did not decide the number of the serpent's כֵּאלֹהִים at 3:5. It observes only that the plural is in the text at 1:26, that God uses it again of himself at 3:22, and that whatever "us" means in the one place it means in the other.

The image is stated twice in the Hebrew of 1:27, בְּצַלְמוֹ בְּצֶלֶם אֱלֹהִים, "in his image, in the image of God," and once in the Greek, κατ᾿ εἰκόνα θεοῦ ἐποίησεν αὐτόν, which drops the first of the two.[^nb12f] The Vulgate restored the doubling, *ad imaginem suam: ad imaginem Dei creauit illum*, and the English from 1540 follows the Latin.[^nb12g] Coverdale has no "image" at all: "let vs make man in oure similitude after oure licknesse … And God created man after his licknesse: after ȳ licknesse of God created he him," two words for the Hebrew's two, צֶלֶם and דְּמוּת, but "likeness" for both.[^nb12h] The Geneva's note on the verse says what the image is, by way of Ephesians 4:24: "man was created after God in righteousnes & true holines, meaning by these two wordes all perfection, as wisdome, trueth, innocencie, power, &c."; the Bishops' says that man "by sinne he afterwarde corrupted" it, and that "We ought to labour to repayre this image so oft repeated."[^nb12i] The reading this book carries is a different one, and it is the text's: the image is announced here, and the text does not say here what it consists in; it says at 3:22, in God's own sentence, that the man has become like us *to know good and evil*. The Geneva's gloss puts the content of the image before the eating; the text puts it after.

### B. Male and Female, and the Blessing (1:27–28)

זָכָר וּנְקֵבָה בָּרָא אֹתָם, "male and female created he them." The first telling states in three words what the second telling will spend from 2:7 to 2:22 narrating: the man formed, the woman built. It states it as one act on one day, and the reading this book has argued from the second telling is that the first telling's one act is the second telling's many, compressed. The first telling does not say that the male and the female were made at the same moment; it says that on the sixth day they were made. Where the second telling places the woman's making, the first telling's sixth day is reached.

Then the blessing, and it is spoken to them: וַיְבָרֶךְ אֹתָם אֱלֹהִים וַיֹּאמֶר לָהֶם, "and God blessed them, and God said unto them," the pronouns plural, the addressees two. פְּרוּ וּרְבוּ, "be fruitful and multiply," is the blessing the creatures of the water and air received at 1:22; here it is given to the man and the woman with three verbs added, וּמִלְאוּ אֶת־הָאָרֶץ וְכִבְשֻׁהָ וּרְדוּ, "and fill the earth, and subdue it, and have dominion." The blessing cannot be spoken to one; it is spoken to the pair, and in the second telling's order the pair exists only from 2:22. Nothing in the second telling reports the blessing being spoken, and the book does not supply the moment; it observes that the first telling places it on the sixth day, after the making of male and female, which is where the second telling's narrative arrives at 2:25.

The two verbs of the mandate have been read as harshly as any two words in the chapter, and the versions show where the harshness came from. וְכִבְשֻׁהָ is "subdue it," from כָּבַשׁ, a verb of treading down; וּרְדוּ is "have dominion," from רָדָה, a verb of ruling. The Septuagint is the strong version: κατακυριεύσατε αὐτῆς καὶ ἄρχετε, "lord it over it and rule," where at 1:26 it had the milder ἀρχέτωσαν, "let them rule."[^nb12j] The Vulgate is milder than its reputation: at 1:26 *præsit*, "let him preside over," and at 1:28 *subiicite eam, & dominamini*.[^nb12k] The English "subdue it, and haue dominion" is not an inheritance from either. It is Coverdale's wording of 1535, "subdue it, & haue domynion," and the Great Bible, the Bishops' and the 1611 kept it; the Geneva has "subdue it, and rule ouer."[^nb12l] Every edition since has "subdue." The English reader who hears in the mandate a licence to exploit hears Coverdale's English for a Hebrew whose second verb is the verb of a king's rule and whose first is the verb the Hebrew Bible uses for bringing land under cultivation; the book records the words and leaves the theology of them to the companion volume.

### C. The Food, and "Very Good" (1:29–31)

> **Genesis 1:29–31 (1611)**
> **29** And God said, Behold, I haue giuen you euery herbe bearing seede, which is vpon the face of all the earth, and euery tree, in the which is the fruit of a tree yeelding seed, to you it shall be for meat:
> **30** And to euery beast of the earth, and to euery foule of the aire, and to euery thing that creepeth vpon the earth, wherein there is life, I haue giuen euery greene herbe for meat: and it was so.
> **31** And God saw euery thing that hee had made: and behold, it was very good. And the euening and the morning were the sixth day.[^nb12m]

The food of the man and the woman is the seed-bearing herb and the fruit of the tree, and the food of the animals is the green herb. Two things in these verses bear on the second telling. The first is that the man and woman are given every tree that bears fruit with seed, without exception, in the first telling; the second telling will except one, and the exception is the whole of its plot. The second is the phrase at 1:30 that the 1611 renders "wherein there is life" and glosses in its margin "Hebr. a liuing soule": it is נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה once more, of every beast and bird and creeping thing, and the 1611's margin is the last of its three confessions that the animals and the man are called by one name.[^nb12n]

Then the judgement on the whole. וַיַּרְא אֱלֹהִים אֶת־כָּל־אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה וְהִנֵּה־טוֹב מְאֹד, "and God saw everything that he had made, and behold, very good." Six times the chapter has said "good"; here, of the whole and on the sixth day, it says "very good" and adds הִנֵּה, "behold." The word will return once more in these chapters, at 3:22, הֵן הָאָדָם הָיָה כְּאַחַד מִמֶּנּוּ, "behold, the man has become as one of us." The two "beholds" are God's two judgements on the work of the sixth day, the first on the whole as made and the second on the man as completed, and the reading this book carries is that they are one judgement. The versions kept the word: ἰδοὺ καλὰ λίαν, *& erant valde bona*, "lo, they were exceadinge good" (Coverdale), "beholde, it was exceading good" (the Great), "lo, it was very good" (the Geneva), "beholde, it was exceedyng good" (the Bishops'), "behold, it was very good" (the 1611).[^nb12o]

### D. The Sixth Day and the Second Telling

The first telling gives the sixth day six verses and the making of man three of them. It says that God deliberated, that he made man in his image, male and female, that he blessed them and gave them the earth and its food, and that he saw it all and it was very good. It does not say how the man was made, or the woman, or in what order, or what passed between their making and the evening; it does not mention a garden, or a tree, or a commandment, or a serpent. The second telling supplies all of that, and the reading this book has argued from the second telling's own clauses is that it supplies it as the content of the sixth day and of the days before it, told again from the ground. The first telling's "let us make man in our image" is the intention; its "male and female created he them" is the summary; its "very good" is the verdict. The second telling's "the man has become as one of us, to know good and evil" is the same verdict in the words of the intention, and it is spoken when the second telling has finished telling how the intention was carried out. The next chapter reads the rest that follows both.

## 1.3 The Seventh Day and the Seam (2:1–3)

> **Genesis 2:1–3 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **1** וַיְכֻלּוּ הַשָּׁמַיִם וְהָאָרֶץ וְכָל־צְבָאָם׃
> **2** וַיְכַל אֱלֹהִים בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי מְלַאכְתּוֹ אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה וַיִּשְׁבֹּת בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי מִכָּל־מְלַאכְתּוֹ אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה׃
> **3** וַיְבָרֶךְ אֱלֹהִים אֶת־יוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי וַיְקַדֵּשׁ אֹתוֹ כִּי בוֹ שָׁבַת מִכָּל־מְלַאכְתּוֹ אֲשֶׁר־בָּרָא אֱלֹהִים לַעֲשׂוֹת׃

> **Genesis 2:1–3 (1611)**
> **1** Thus the heauens and the earth were finished, and all the hoste of them.
> **2** And on the seuenth day God ended his worke, which hee had made: And he rested on the seuenth day from all his worke, which he had made.
> **3** And God blessed the seuenth day, and sanctified it: because that in it he had rested from all his worke, which God created and made.[^nb13a]

### A. The Rest (2:1–3)

The first telling closes with three verbs of sequence and a seventh day that has no evening and morning. וַיְכֻלּוּ, "and they were finished"; וַיְכַל, "and he finished"; וַיִּשְׁבֹּת, "and he rested"; וַיְבָרֶךְ, "and he blessed"; וַיְקַדֵּשׁ, "and he sanctified." The day is named three times, בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי, and the work is named four times, מְלַאכְתּוֹ, "his work," each time as the work already made, אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה. The chapter division of the printed Bibles falls between 1:31 and 2:1, but the text does not divide there: the seventh day is the last of the seven, and the codex, which leaves an open space after each of the first six days, leaves one after the sixth at 1:31 and a larger one after the seventh at 2:3.[^nb13b]

The Hebrew of 2:2 says that God finished his work on the seventh day, וַיְכַל אֱלֹהִים בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי, and rested on the seventh day. The oldest version would not have it so. The Septuagint reads καὶ συνετέλεσεν ὁ θεὸς ἐν τῇ ἡμέρᾳ τῇ ἕκτῃ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ, "and God finished his works on the *sixth* day," and rested on the seventh; the translators, or the text before them, moved the finishing back a day so that nothing should be done on the day of rest.[^nb13c] The Vulgate has the Hebrew's day, *Compleuitque Deus die septimo opus suum*, and every English Bible "in the seuenth daye God ended his worke."[^nb13d] The book records the Greek reading as the first harmonisation in these chapters, older than any in the second telling, and of the same kind: a difficulty in the order of the text smoothed by changing a word. The Hebrew's difficulty is only apparent. To finish a work on a day is to have it complete when the day begins; the seventh day is the day on which the work stands finished, and the rest is the finishing.

The last words of the verse the 1611 renders "which God created and made," and its margin gives the Hebrew, "Heb. created to make," אֲשֶׁר־בָּרָא אֱלֹהִים לַעֲשׂוֹת, an infinitive the translators could not carry into English and did not pretend to.[^nb13e] The Great Bible and the Bishops' have "which God ordeyned to make," Coverdale and the Geneva "created and made," the Septuagint ὧν ἤρξατο ὁ θεὸς ποιῆσαι, "which God began to make," and the Vulgate *quod creauit Deus vt faceret*, "which God created that he might make."[^nb13f] The Greek's "began" is the odd one; the Hebrew says the work was created to be made, and the Latin is closest.

The versions differ at the openings of the three verses in the ways Part 1 has taught the reader to expect. The Septuagint has καί at each. The Vulgate opens *Igitur perfecti sunt*, "therefore were finished," and continues *Compleuitque*, *Et benedixit*. Coverdale has "Thus was heauē and earth fynished … and thus in the seuenth daye God ended his worke"; the Great Bible "The heauēs also and the erth were fynished … And in the seuenth daye … In the seuenth daye also, he rested"; the Geneva "Thus the heauens … For in the seuenth day God ended … So God blessed"; the Bishops' "The heauens also & the earth were finisshed … And in the seuenth day … And the seuēth day he rested … And God blessed"; the 1611 "Thus the heauens … And on the seuenth day … And he rested … And God blessed."[^nb13g] The 1611's "Thus" is the Geneva's and Coverdale's; the Great Bible's "also" and the Vulgate's *igitur* are the same summarising turn in another word. None of them alters the order, and none needed to.

The Reformation notes read the rest as the tradition had. The Geneva: God "had now finished his creacion, but his prouidence stil watcheth ouer his creatures, and gouerneth thē," and the day is "Appointed … to be kept holy, that man might therein consider ȳ excellencie of his workes & Gods goodnes towards him."[^nb13h] The Bishops': "The seuenth daye consecrated to the seruice of God, for man in his perfect state."[^nb13i] The reading this book carries is compatible with both and adds one thing. The seventh day is the rest after the work, and the work, as the first telling has just said, ended with a man and a woman made in the image of God and judged very good. The second telling, on this book's reading, tells the making of that man and woman down to the moment God declared the image achieved. The rest of 2:1–3 therefore follows, in the order of the events, the whole of what 2:4–3:24 narrates; the second telling does not come after the seventh day but within the six, and the text's placing of the rest before the second telling is the placing of a summary before its detail.

### B. The Seam (2:3–4)

Between the last word of 2:3 and the first word of 2:4 the codex leaves its widest open space in these chapters, and the second telling begins without a connective: אֵלֶּה תוֹלְדוֹת, "these are the generations."[^nb13j] Chapter 2.1 has read the heading; this chapter records what the English Bibles printed above it.

Three of the English Bibles supplied each chapter with a summary of its contents, and at the second chapter of Genesis the three summaries are the tradition's own statement of how the two tellings relate. The Great Bible of 1540 summarised the chapter as "The halowynge of the Sabbath daye. The foure floudes of Paradise. The settyng in of man in Paradise. The tre of knowledge is forbidden him; how Adam named all creatures. The creacyon of Eua. The institucyon of maryage," a list of contents that says nothing about the relation of the chapter to the one before.[^nb13k] The Geneva of 1560 summarised it "God resteth the seuenth day, and sanctifieth it. 15 He setteth man in the garden. 22 He createth the womā. 29 Mariage is ordeined," also a list.[^nb13l] The Bishops' of 1568 wrote: "2 The day of rest is blessed. 4 A repeatyng of thynges foretolde in the first. 6 The cloude from the earth as a well to water it. 7 He rehearseth agayne the fashionyng of man. 8 Man in the paradise of pleasure. 10 The wood of lyfe, the wood of knowledge. 11. 13. 14 The ryuers names. 16 God forbyddeth man the tree of knowledge of good and euyll. 19 Adam geueth names vnto all lyuyng thynges. 22 Woman is created. 23 The institution of holy mariage."[^nb13m] Two of its eleven lines say what the other summaries do not: that verse 4 begins a repeating of things foretold in the first chapter, and that verse 7 rehearses again the fashioning of man.

The heading did not survive the Bishops' Bible's own printings. The folios of 1574 and 1578 and the edition of 1575 keep it word for word; the edition of 1577 replaced it with the Great Bible's list, "The halowyng of the Sabboth day … Adam nameth all creatures … Heua is created … And marriage is ordeyned"; and the folio of 1602, the copy the King James translators were given to revise, prints that list and not the heading.[^nb13o] The King James translators kept the form of the summaries and wrote their own. The 1611 reads: "1 The first Sabbath. 4 The maner of the creation. 8 The planting of the garden of Eden, 10 and the riuer thereof. 17 The tree of knowledge onely forbidden. 19. 20 The naming of the creatures. 21 The making of woman, and institution of Mariage."[^nb13n] The Bishops' "A repeatyng of thynges foretolde in the first" had become, in the Bishops' own later printings, a bare list of contents, and became in the 1611 "The maner of the creation"; the Bishops' "He rehearseth agayne the fashionyng of man" has no counterpart in either. What was dropped was not a translation but a reading, and the reading dropped was that Genesis 2 repeats Genesis 1. The Elizabethan church's lectern Bible printed that reading at the head of the chapter in its folios from 1568 to at least 1578; by 1602 its own printers had let it go, the King James translators never saw it in the text they revised, and the English reader has not seen it since.

The book does not claim that the Bishops' translators held the reading this book argues, or that "repeatyng" meant to them what "one narrative told twice" means here. It claims what the leaf shows: that a Bible of the English church stated in its own heading that the second chapter goes back over the first, that the heading was let go by the Bishops' own printers before 1602 and not restored by the revisers of 1611, and that the dropping and the 1611's "maner of the creation" were each a choice about how the reader should approach the seam. The Hebrew of the seam is a gap and a heading. The Bishops' told the reader what kind of heading it was. The 1611 told the reader it was "the maner of the creation," which is true and says nothing.

### C. What Part 1 Establishes

The first telling is a frame of six days and a rest, told as sequence from its second verse, closed by the codex with an open space at each day, and ended with a man and a woman made in the image of God and judged very good. It supplies the second telling with its order and with three words, seed, living soul and creeping thing, and it leaves to the second telling everything about how the last work was done. The seam between the two is a gap in the manuscript and a heading in the text, and the one English Bible that told its readers what the heading meant let the telling go in its own later printings, so that the King James never had it to keep. Parts 2 and 3 have read what follows the seam. Part 4 now reads the versions as wholes.

# Part 2 — The Second Telling: Genesis 2:4–25

## 2.1 The Heading and the Scene (2:4–6)

The second telling does not begin with "and." In the Leningrad Codex the seventh day closes at 2:3, the scribe leaves the rest of the line empty and an open space below it, the widest break in these three chapters, and the text resumes with two words that have no connective before them: אֵלֶּה תוֹלְדוֹת, "these are the generations."[^nb21a] The gap and the opening are of a piece. What follows is not the next event after the seventh day; it is a new beginning, headed as such.

> **Genesis 2:4–6 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **4** אֵלֶּה תוֹלְדוֹת הַשָּׁמַיִם וְהָאָרֶץ בְּהִבָּרְאָם בְּיוֹם עֲשׂוֹת יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים אֶרֶץ וְשָׁמָיִם׃
> **5** וְכֹל שִׂיחַ הַשָּׂדֶה טֶרֶם יִהְיֶה בָאָרֶץ וְכָל־עֵשֶׂב הַשָּׂדֶה טֶרֶם יִצְמָח כִּי לֹא הִמְטִיר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים עַל־הָאָרֶץ וְאָדָם אַיִן לַעֲבֹד אֶת־הָאֲדָמָה׃
> **6** וְאֵד יַעֲלֶה מִן־הָאָרֶץ וְהִשְׁקָה אֶת־כָּל־פְּנֵי־הָאֲדָמָה׃

> **Genesis 2:4–6 (1611)**
> **4** These are the generations of the heauens, & of the earth, when they were created; in the day that the LORD God made the earth, and the heauens,
> **5** And euery plant of the field, before it was in the earth, and euery herbe of the field, before it grew: for the LORD God had not caused it to raine vpon the earth, and there was not a man to till the ground.
> **6** But there went vp a mist from the earth, and watered the whole face of the ground.[^nb21b]

### A. The Heading

"These are the generations of" is a formula, and Genesis uses it as a heading. It stands at the head of the line of Adam (5:1), of Noah (6:9), of the sons of Noah (10:1), of Shem (11:10), of Terah (11:27), of Ishmael (25:12), of Isaac (25:19), of Esau (36:1, 36:9) and of Jacob (37:2), eleven times in the book, and 2:4 is the first.[^nb21c] At every other occurrence the reader understands that a section has ended and another begun, and that the new section will go back over ground the old one has touched: the line of Adam in chapter 5 repeats his creation from chapter 1, the generations of Noah in 6:9 repeat his introduction from 5:32 and 6:8. The formula at 2:4 asks to be read the same way. It announces a second account of the heavens and the earth, and the account that follows goes back over the ground of the first.

The Bishops' Bible of 1568 read it so, and said so in the chapter's own heading: "4 A repeatyng of thynges foretolde in the first," and, three lines on, "7 He rehearseth agayne the fashionyng of man."[^nb21d] The Bishops' own printings had dropped both lines by 1602, and the King James translators, revising the folio of 1602, wrote "4 The maner of the creation," which asserts nothing about the relation of the chapters. Chapter 1.3 has told that story; here it is enough to note that the heading the Bishops' printed is what the Hebrew formula says.

The ancient versions did not disturb the heading. The Septuagint has Αὕτη ἡ βίβλος γενέσεως οὐρανοῦ καὶ γῆς, "this is the book of the generation of heaven and earth," a rendering that supplies the word "book" and by doing so makes the heading more of a heading, not less.[^nb21e] The Vulgate has *Istæ sunt generationes cæli & terræ*, and every English Bible from Coverdale to the 1611 "These are the generations."[^nb21f] No version adds a connective. On this verse the whole tradition agrees with the codex: 2:4 begins.

What the Latin and the early English printings did not reproduce is the gap. The 1592 Vulgate runs 2:3 into 2:4 in one paragraph, and so do Coverdale and the Great Bible; the Geneva and the Bishops' number the verse and let it stand; the 1611 marks it with a pilcrow.[^nb21g] The seam the scribe of the codex left open across a line and a half became, in print, a verse number. The reader of a printed Bible has to be told there is a seam here. The reader of the manuscript can see it.

### B. The Scene

The first clause of the second telling is not an event. It is a condition. וְכֹל שִׂיחַ הַשָּׂדֶה טֶרֶם יִהְיֶה בָאָרֶץ: the vav is prefixed to a noun, "and every shrub of the field," and the verb is displaced to the middle of the clause. By the rule of the Method (§ B) this is a clause that steps out of any sequence to set a scene, and the scene it sets is a bare earth. No shrub of the field was yet in the earth; no herb of the field had yet sprung up; the LORD God had not yet caused rain; and there was no man to work the ground. Four negatives, and the last of them, וְאָדָם אַיִן, "and man there was none," is the one the second telling exists to reverse. The next verb of the narrative, at 2:7, forms him.

The reading this book carries from the first telling to the second depends on taking 2:5 at its word. If the earth has no shrub and no herb when the second telling opens, the second telling opens before the third day's second work, the bringing forth of grass, herb and fruit tree (1:11–12). It does not open after the sixth day. The man who is formed in the next verse is formed on an earth that has dry land and water and nothing growing, and the trees he will see planted and sprouting (2:8–9) are the vegetation the first telling assigned to the third day, now told from the ground where he stands. Chapter 2.2 takes up the sequence; this chapter establishes only where the sequence starts, and 2:5 says where.

The versions did not mark the turn here, and that has to be recorded against the pattern they otherwise keep. The Septuagint, which reserves δέ for a noun-first clause that opens a scene and uses it at six of the seven such verses between 1:1 and 4:1, has καὶ πᾶν χλωρὸν ἀγροῦ at 2:5, a plain "and."[^nb21h] The Vulgate has *Et omne virgultum agri*.[^nb21i] Coverdale and the Great Bible run the verse into the heading without a break: "in the tyme whan the LORDE God made heauen and earth: before there was eny twygg vpon earth," and "in the daye, when the Lorde God made ȳ earth and the heauēs and euery plant of the felde, before it was in the earth."[^nb21j] The Geneva was the first to begin the verse with "And euerie plant," and the Bishops' and the 1611 followed it.[^nb21k] Not one witness before the twentieth century opened 2:5 with a word that tells the reader a scene is being set. The modern versions' "Now no shrub had yet appeared" is, at this one verse, the first translation to render the shape of the clause, and the book says so because the book elsewhere faults the moderns for supplying "now" where the Hebrew has a verb. Here the Hebrew has a noun, and "now" is right.

The second scene-clause, one verse on, every version marked. וְאֵד יַעֲלֶה מִן־הָאָרֶץ: "and a mist went up from the earth," again a noun after the vav, again a standing condition rather than an event, and this time the sense forces the contrast, because the verse before has just said it had not rained. The Septuagint has πηγὴ δὲ ἀνέβαινεν, "but a spring went up," the Vulgate *Sed fons ascendebat*, Coverdale "But there arose a myst," the Geneva "But a myst went vp," the Bishops' and the 1611 "But there went vp a mist"; only the Great Bible has "And."[^nb21l] The 1611 adds in its margin "Or, a mist which went vp from &c.," an alternative that would fold the clause back into 2:5 as a relative, and did not adopt it.[^nb21m] The two verses together show the translators' habit exactly. Where the sense of the passage demanded a "but," they read the clause and wrote one. Where the sense did not force the issue, at 2:5, they wrote "and" and let the scene-setting go unmarked. The Hebrew marks both alike.

The word the mist is made of, אֵד, occurs once more in the Bible, and its sense is uncertain; the Greek translators took it for a spring, the Latin for a fountain, the English for a mist, and the 1611's margin shows the translators aware that the clause could be construed two ways.[^nb21n] Nothing in this book turns on it. What turns on 2:6 is only its shape: a second condition laid over the first, water without rain, before the first act.

### C. What the Three Verses Establish

A heading, and two conditions. The heading says a new account begins and that it concerns the heavens and the earth already made. The first condition says the earth of this account has nothing growing on it and no man. The second says it is watered from below. Then, at 2:7, the first verb: וַיִּיצֶר, "and the LORD God formed." Everything the second telling narrates is narrated as following that verb, and everything the versions did to rearrange the order of the second telling they did to the verbs after it, not to the scene before it. The scene they left alone, and the scene is where the man is placed: on the third day's earth, before the third day's plants.
