## 2.2 The Man Formed, Then the Garden Planted (2:7–17)

The scene set, the narrative begins, and it begins with the man.

> **Genesis 2:7–9 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **7** וַיִּיצֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים אֶת־הָאָדָם עָפָר מִן־הָאֲדָמָה וַיִּפַּח בְּאַפָּיו נִשְׁמַת חַיִּים וַיְהִי הָאָדָם לְנֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה׃
> **8** וַיִּטַּע יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים גַּן־בְּעֵדֶן מִקֶּדֶם וַיָּשֶׂם שָׁם אֶת־הָאָדָם אֲשֶׁר יָצָר׃
> **9** וַיַּצְמַח יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים מִן־הָאֲדָמָה כָּל־עֵץ נֶחְמָד לְמַרְאֶה וְטוֹב לְמַאֲכָל וְעֵץ הַחַיִּים בְּתוֹךְ הַגָּן וְעֵץ הַדַּעַת טוֹב וָרָע׃

> **Genesis 2:7–9 (1611)**
> **7** And the LORD God formed man of the dust of the ground, & breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and man became a liuing soule.
> **8** And the LORD God planted a garden Eastward in Eden; and there he put the man whom he had formed.
> **9** And out of the ground made the LORD God to grow euery tree that is pleasant to the sight, and good for food: the tree of life also in the midst of the garden, and the tree of knowledge of good and euill.[^nb22a]

Three verses, three verbs, each in the form the Method describes: וַיִּיצֶר, "and he formed"; וַיִּטַּע, "and he planted"; וַיַּצְמַח, "and he made to grow." Each is prefixed with the vav of sequence, and each reports the next act after the one before. The man is formed; then a garden is planted and he is put in it; then the trees grow out of the ground. Read as the Hebrew has it, the man is on the earth before there is a tree on it, which is what 2:5 had said of the earth he was formed on. The three chapters of this Part turn on that order, and on what the versions did with it.

### A. The Man Formed (2:7)

The man is made of two things, and the verse names both in words that the rest of the narrative will use again. He is עָפָר מִן־הָאֲדָמָה, "dust of the ground," and he becomes נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה, "a living soul," by the breath of God in his nostrils.

The dust is the word to watch. עָפָר occurs three times in Genesis 1–3, here, in the serpent's sentence (3:14, "dust shalt thou eat"), and in the man's (3:19, "dust thou art, and unto dust shalt thou return"), and the three occurrences are a single thread that binds the making to the two sentences. Chapter 3.3 follows it. What matters here is that the ancient versions did not keep the thread whole. The Septuagint has χοῦν ἀπὸ τῆς γῆς, "dust from the earth," at 2:7, but at 3:14 and 3:19 it has γῆ, "earth," the same word it uses for the ground the dust is taken from, so that the man, the serpent's food and the man's end are all "earth" in Greek and the dust disappears into the ground.[^nb22b] The Vulgate goes the other way: *de limo terræ*, "of the mud of the earth," at 2:7, *terram* at 3:14, *puluis* at 3:19, three Latin words for one Hebrew one.[^nb22c] The English recovered the thread. Coverdale has "of the moulde of the earth" here and "earth" at 3:14 and 3:19, following the Latin and Luther; but the Great Bible of 1540 has "euen dust from of the grounde," and "dust" at both sentences, and the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 keep it, the 1611 adding in its margin "Heb. dust of the ground" to show the phrase is the Hebrew's own.[^nb22d] An English reader since 1540 has been able to see what a Greek or Latin reader could not: that the serpent is sentenced to eat what the man is made of.

The soul is the word to be careful with. נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה, "a living soul," is not a phrase reserved for the man. It is the phrase the first telling used for the creatures of the sea (1:20), the land animals (1:24) and every living thing (1:30), and the second telling will use it again for every creature the man names (2:19). The Hebrew gives the animals and the man one name for what they are. So does the Greek, ψυχὴ ζῶσα at every place, and the Latin, *anima viuens*, and so does Coverdale, "lyuynge soules" for the beasts and "a lyuynge soule" for the man.[^nb22e] The Great Bible of 1540 divided the phrase: "lyuing creature" for the animals at 1:24, "a lyuing soule" for the man here, and every English Bible after it has kept the division in the text and put the Hebrew in the margin. The Geneva has "the soule of life" at 1:20 in its margin, the 1611 "Heb. soule" at 1:20 and "Heb. a liuing soule" at 1:30.[^nb22f] The distinction between a creature and a soul, which the English reader takes from this verse, was made in English in 1540 and is not in the verse.

The versions agree on the verb. The Septuagint has καὶ ἔπλασεν, the Vulgate *Formauit igitur*, and the English "shope" (Coverdale, the Great, the Bishops'), "made" (the Geneva) or "formed" (the 1611), every one of them a past act in sequence.[^nb22g] The Geneva and the Great Bible mark the verse as a fresh start with "also" ("The Lord God also made the mā," "The Lorde God also shope man"), which is English for a Latin *igitur* and does not change the order.[^nb22h] No witness places the forming of the man anywhere but here, after the scene of 2:5–6 and before the planting of 2:8.

### B. The Garden Planted and the Trees Grown (2:8–9)

Here the versions divide, and the division is the subject of this Part.

The Hebrew is sequence: וַיִּטַּע, "and he planted," וַיַּצְמַח, "and he made to grow." The Septuagint is sequence, and more than sequence. At 2:8 it has Καὶ ἐφύτευσεν Κύριος ὁ θεὸς παράδεισον, "and the Lord God planted a paradise," and at 2:9 καὶ ἐξανέτειλεν ὁ θεὸς ἔτι ἐκ τῆς γῆς πᾶν ξύλον, "and God made to spring up *further* out of the earth every tree."[^nb22i] The Hebrew has no "further." The Greek translators added it, and an addition is a reading: they understood the trees to be grown after the man was formed, as a further act, and they said so in a word the Hebrew did not need because its verb-form had already said it. The oldest translation of Genesis, made in Alexandria in the third century before Christ for readers whose language was Greek, took the order of these verses to be the order of the events.

The Vulgate did not. At 2:8 Jerome wrote *Plantauerat autem Dominus Deus Paradisum voluptatis a principio*, "but the Lord God had planted a paradise of pleasure from the beginning."[^nb22j] Two things are done in that clause that the Hebrew does not do. The verb is put into the pluperfect, *plantauerat*, "had planted," which places the planting before the forming of the man in the verse above; and *a principio*, "from the beginning," is written for the Hebrew מִקֶּדֶם, which is a direction, "eastward," and not a time.[^nb22k] The garden is thereby moved back to the beginning of things and the man is set down in a garden that was there before him. This is the first harmonisation of the second telling to the first in any version, and it was made by the translator who, at the connective, is otherwise the Hebrew's most careful reader in these chapters. It stood in the Bible of the Western Church for eleven hundred years, and it is the ancestor of every modern "Now the LORD God had planted."

The English did not follow Jerome here. Coverdale has "The LORDE God also planted a gardē of pleasure in Eden, towarde ȳ east," the Great Bible "And ȳ Lorde God planted a garden eastwarde from Edē," the Bishops' "And the Lord God planted a garden eastwarde in Eden," the 1611 "And the LORD God planted a garden Eastward in Eden."[^nb22l] All four are simple past, in sequence, and all four have "eastward" for מִקֶּדֶם where the Latin had "from the beginning." The Geneva has the same at 2:8, "And the Lord God plāted a garden Eastwarde in Eden," but at 2:9 it does something of its own: it prints the verse in parentheses, "(For out of the grounde made the Lord God to growe euerie tre pleasant to the sight, and good for meat …)."[^nb22m] A parenthesis with "For" turns a narrated act into an explanation of the one before it; the trees are no longer the next thing that happened but the reason the garden was a garden. It is a gentler harmonisation than Jerome's, and the 1611 did not keep it: "And out of the ground made the LORD God to grow euery tree," in sequence, as the Hebrew has it.

So at 2:8–9 the witnesses stand thus. The Hebrew, the Septuagint, Coverdale, the Great Bible, the Bishops' and the 1611 tell the planting and the growing as acts after the forming of the man. The Vulgate moves the planting to before him, and the Geneva makes the growing a parenthesis. The New International Version, which this book's predecessor quoted as its text for this passage, reads "Now the LORD God had planted a garden in the east, in Eden," and stands with Jerome.[^nb22n] The Septuagint stands with the Hebrew, and adds a word to make sure the reader knows it.

### C. The Rivers (2:10–14)

Between the trees and the commandment the narrative pauses to describe the garden's water, and it marks the pause in the way the Method describes: וְנָהָר יֹצֵא מֵעֵדֶן, "and a river went out of Eden," a noun after the vav, a standing description rather than an event.[^nb22o] The Septuagint marks it, ποταμὸς δὲ ἐκπορεύεται, "now a river goes out"; the Vulgate does not, *Et fluuius egrediebatur*; and every English Bible has "And."[^nb22p] The five verses that follow name the four heads of the river and the lands they compass, and they are the one passage in the second telling where the narrator speaks from outside the story, in a present tense, about a geography his readers might know. They are not part of the sequence and no version treats them as if they were. The sequence resumes at 2:15.

### D. The Man Placed and Commanded (2:15–17)

> **Genesis 2:15–17 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **15** וַיִּקַּח יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים אֶת־הָאָדָם וַיַּנִּחֵהוּ בְגַן־עֵדֶן לְעָבְדָהּ וּלְשָׁמְרָהּ׃
> **16** וַיְצַו יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים עַל־הָאָדָם לֵאמֹר מִכֹּל עֵץ־הַגָּן אָכֹל תֹּאכֵל׃
> **17** וּמֵעֵץ הַדַּעַת טוֹב וָרָע לֹא תֹאכַל מִמֶּנּוּ כִּי בְּיוֹם אֲכָלְךָ מִמֶּנּוּ מוֹת תָּמוּת׃

> **Genesis 2:15–17 (1611)**
> **15** And the LORD God tooke the man, and put him into the garden of Eden, to dresse it, and to keepe it.
> **16** And the LORD God commanded the man, saying, Of euery tree of the garden thou mayest freely eate.
> **17** But of the tree of the knowledge of good and euill, thou shalt not eate of it: for in the day that thou eatest thereof, thou shalt surely die.[^nb22q]

Two more verbs of sequence, וַיִּקַּח and וַיְצַו, "and he took," "and he commanded," and the commandment is given. It is given to the man. The woman has not been made; she will not be made until 2:22; and the pronouns of 2:16–17 are singular throughout, "thou mayest eat," "thou shalt not eat," "thou shalt die." When the serpent puts the commandment to the woman in 3:1–5 he will put it in the plural, "ye shall not eat," and she will answer in the plural, and the book returns to that in chapter 3.1. Here it records only what the Hebrew says: the commandment was spoken to one person, on a day when there was one. The Septuagint did not keep that. Its 2:16 is singular, βρώσει φάγῃ, "thou shalt eat"; its 2:17 turns plural, οὐ φάγεσθε … ᾗ δ᾿ ἂν ἡμέρᾳ φάγησθε … θανάτῳ ἀποθανεῖσθε, "ye shall not eat … in the day ye eat … ye shall die," so that the prohibition is spoken, in Greek, to more than the one man who is there to hear it.[^nb22w] The Vulgate and every English Bible keep the Hebrew's singular. The Greek translator has let the serpent's plural of 3:1 reach back into the commandment; the Hebrew keeps the two apart, and the difference is the ground of chapter 3.1.

The clause that carries the prohibition is the fourth noun-first clause of the chapter, and every version marked it. וּמֵעֵץ הַדַּעַת, "and of the tree of knowledge," sets the forbidden tree against all the others, and the Septuagint has ἀπὸ δὲ τοῦ ξύλου, the Vulgate *De ligno autem*, and every English Bible from Coverdale to the 1611 "But."[^nb22r] Where the contrast is in the sense, the translators read the shape.

The Hebrew of the permission and the penalty doubles its verbs, and the doubling has a history. אָכֹל תֹּאכֵל, "eating thou shalt eat," and מוֹת תָּמוּת, "dying thou shalt die," are the idiom by which Hebrew makes a verb emphatic, and from the Greek of the third century before Christ to the English of 1568 the versions carried the idiom across. The Septuagint has βρώσει φάγῃ and θανάτῳ ἀποθανεῖσθε; the Vulgate *comede* for the first but *morte morieris*, "by death thou shalt die," for the second; and every English Bible from 1535 to 1568 has "thou shalt dye the death."[^nb22s] The King James of 1611 was the first to move the idiom out of the text. It reads "thou mayest freely eate" and "thou shalt surely die," and confesses the Hebrew in two marginal notes, "Hebr. eating thou shalt eate" and "Hebr. dying thou shalt die."[^nb22t] The translators knew what the Hebrew said and chose the smoother English; the margin is their record of the choice.

The tree's name was read two ways in antiquity. The Hebrew is עֵץ הַדַּעַת טוֹב וָרָע, "the tree of the knowledge of good and evil," and the Vulgate and every English Bible render it so. The Septuagint at 2:9 has τὸ ξύλον τοῦ εἰδέναι γνωστὸν καλοῦ καὶ πονηροῦ, "the tree of knowing what is knowable of good and evil," a doubled rendering that strains after something more than "knowledge," and at 2:17 τοῦ ξύλου τοῦ γινώσκειν καλὸν καὶ πονηρόν, "the tree of knowing good and evil," with the verb rather than the noun.[^nb22u] The Geneva's notes read the knowledge as experience: at 2:9, "That is, of miserable experience, which came by disobeying God," and at 2:17, on the death threatened, "By this death he meaneth the separacion of man frō God, who is our life and chief felicitie."[^nb22v] The reading this book gives the tree in Part 3 is that the knowledge of good and evil is what the first telling's image of God requires and the second telling's man does not yet have; the Geneva's "miserable experience" is a sixteenth-century statement that the knowledge is gained by doing, not by being told, and the book takes it as a witness.

### E. The Order So Far

From 2:7 to 2:17 the Hebrew is a chain of sequence verbs with two descriptive pauses (2:10–14 and the tree's name in 2:9), and every link in the chain is the next event: the man formed, the garden planted, the trees grown, the river described, the man placed, the man commanded. The chain is broken in one ancient version at one link, the Vulgate's *plantauerat* at 2:8, and bent in one early English version at another, the Geneva's parenthesis at 2:9. The Septuagint not only keeps the chain but adds ἔτι at 2:9 to say that the trees came after. The King James keeps it whole. The modern versions that print "Now the LORD God had planted" have chosen Jerome's link over the Hebrew's, and the reader who follows them is placed in a garden that the text says was planted around a man already there.

## 2.3 The Animals Formed, Then Named (2:18–20)

> **Genesis 2:18–20 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **18** וַיֹּאמֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים לֹא־טוֹב הֱיוֹת הָאָדָם לְבַדּוֹ אֶעֱשֶׂה־לּוֹ עֵזֶר כְּנֶגְדּוֹ׃
> **19** וַיִּצֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים מִן־הָאֲדָמָה כָּל־חַיַּת הַשָּׂדֶה וְאֵת כָּל־עוֹף הַשָּׁמַיִם וַיָּבֵא אֶל־הָאָדָם לִרְאוֹת מַה־יִּקְרָא־לוֹ וְכֹל אֲשֶׁר יִקְרָא־לוֹ הָאָדָם נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה הוּא שְׁמוֹ׃
> **20** וַיִּקְרָא הָאָדָם שֵׁמוֹת לְכָל־הַבְּהֵמָה וּלְעוֹף הַשָּׁמַיִם וּלְכֹל חַיַּת הַשָּׂדֶה וּלְאָדָם לֹא־מָצָא עֵזֶר כְּנֶגְדּוֹ׃

> **Genesis 2:18–20 (1611)**
> **18** And the LORD God said, It is not good that the man should be alone: I will make him an helpe meet for him.
> **19** And out of the ground the LORD God formed euery beast of the field, and euery foule of the aire, and brought them vnto Adam, to see what he would call them: and whatsoeuer Adam called euery liuing creature, that was the name thereof.
> **20** And Adam gaue names to all cattell, and to the foule of the aire, and to euery beast of the fielde: but for Adam there was not found an helpe meet for him.[^nb23a]

### A. The Judgement and the Intention (2:18)

The commandment given, the narrative moves at once to the man's condition: וַיֹּאמֶר, "and he said," a verb of sequence, and the first thing God says about the man he has made is that his state is not good. לֹא־טוֹב הֱיוֹת הָאָדָם לְבַדּוֹ, "not good is the being of the man by himself." The first telling had pronounced every stage of its work good and the whole of it very good; the second telling, having formed one man, says of him alone what the first never said of anything. The judgement is not on the man but on his solitude, and the remedy is announced in the same breath: אֶעֱשֶׂה־לּוֹ עֵזֶר כְּנֶגְדּוֹ, "I will make for him a help as over against him."

The phrase is two words and the tradition has made a great deal of the second. עֵזֶר is "help" or "helper," a word the Hebrew Bible uses of God more often than of anyone else; כְּנֶגְדּוֹ is a preposition and a pronoun, "like what is in front of him," "corresponding to him," "his counterpart." Nothing in either word ranks the help below the helped. The versions did not rank it either. The Septuagint has βοηθὸν κατ᾿ αὐτόν, "a helper according to him," and at 2:20 βοηθὸς ὅμοιος αὐτῷ, "a helper like him"; the Vulgate *adiutorium simile sibi*, "a help like himself," and *adiutor similis eius*; Coverdale "an helpe, to beare him cōpany"; the Great Bible "an helpe, which maye be present wt him"; the Bishops' "an helpe lyke vnto hym."[^nb23b] Five renderings across three languages and a thousand years, and every one of them reads the phrase as likeness or company.

"Help meet" is the Geneva's, coined in 1560: "I wil make him an helpe mete for him," with the margin "Ebr. before him."[^nb23c] "Meet" is the adjective, "suitable," and the phrase says a help suitable for him, which is the Hebrew. The 1611 took it over, "an helpe meet for him," with its own margin "Hebr. as before him," and from the 1611 it passed into English speech, where the adjective was heard as part of a noun and "helpmeet," and then "helpmate," came to mean a wife, and a subordinate one.[^nb23d] The subordination is in the later English word, not in the Geneva's phrase and not in any version of the verse. No witness before the twentieth century, and no witness in it, renders 2:18 so as to place the woman under the man. Where the tradition did write her subjection into the text it was at 3:16, and chapter 3.3 shows how. Here, at the verse the argument is usually hung on, there is nothing to hang it on.

Two of the versions hear a plural in God's intention. The Hebrew is singular, אֶעֱשֶׂה, "I will make." The Septuagint has ποιήσωμεν, "let us make," and the Vulgate *faciamus*, the same plural both had used at 1:26 for "let us make man in our image."[^nb23e] The translators have carried the first telling's plural into the second telling's promise, and in doing so have made 2:18 echo 1:26 in a way the Hebrew does not. Every English Bible has "I will make." The book records the variant and draws nothing from it except this: that the ancient translators heard the two tellings as one account and let the one shape the other, here as at 2:8.

### B. The Animals Formed, After the Man (2:19)

The verse begins with the verb the whole Part has been watching: וַיִּצֶר, "and he formed," the same verb, in the same form, that formed the man at 2:7. The Hebrew tells the forming of the beasts and birds as the next act after God's word of 2:18, and the purpose clause that follows says why: they are brought to the man "to see what he would call them." The animals are formed and brought so that the man may name them, and the naming is the search for the help; the verse after says the search failed. Read in sequence, the beasts of the field and the birds of the air are formed on the day the man is told he is alone, after him, for his sake.

That is the order the Septuagint read, and once more it said so with a word of its own: καὶ ἔπλασεν ὁ θεὸς ἔτι ἐκ τῆς γῆς πάντα τὰ θηρία τοῦ ἀγροῦ, "and God formed *further* out of the earth all the beasts of the field."[^nb23f] The ἔτι is the same adverb the Greek added at 2:9 for the trees. Twice in the second telling the oldest translation inserts "further" where the Hebrew has only the verb of sequence, and both times at the verse where the order of the second telling differs from the order of the first. The translators saw the difference and, rather than smooth it, underlined it.

The Vulgate smoothed it. *Formatis igitur, Dominus Deus, de humo cunctis animantibus terræ, & vniuersis volatilibus cæli, adduxit ea ad Adam*: "the Lord God, therefore, all the living things of the earth and all the birds of heaven having been formed of the soil, brought them to Adam."[^nb23g] The construction is an ablative absolute, and its effect is the effect of the pluperfect at 2:8: the forming is made a completed background to the bringing, an event already over when the verse's main verb begins. Jerome does not say when the animals were formed, but he removes their forming from the sequence of the second telling and leaves the reader free to place it on the sixth day of the first. It is the second of his two harmonisations in this chapter, and it made the same journey as the first.

The English divided along the line of their sources. Coverdale, working from the Latin and from Luther, has the pluperfect: "And whan God the LORDE had made of the earth all maner beastes of the felde, & all maner foules vnder the heauē, he brought them vnto man."[^nb23h] The Great Bible of 1540 restored the sequence: "And so out of the grounde shope the Lorde God euery beast of the felde, and euery foule of the ayre, and brought it vnto man."[^nb23i] The Geneva kept it: "So the Lord God formed of the earth euerie beast of the field, and euerie foule of the heauen, & broght them vnto the mā."[^nb23j] The Bishops' of 1568 went back to Coverdale's tense, "And so out of the grounde the Lorde God had shapen euery beast of the field," and this was the text the King James translators were instructed to revise.[^nb23k] They revised it. The 1611 reads "And out of ȳ ground the LORD God formed euery beast of the field, and euery foule of the aire, and brought them vnto Adam," a simple past, in sequence, with the Bishops' "had" removed.[^nb23l] The New International Version, three and a half centuries later, put it back: "Now the LORD God had formed out of the ground all the wild animals."

The order of the witnesses at 2:19 is therefore this. With the Hebrew: the Septuagint (with ἔτι), the Great Bible, the Geneva, the 1611. Against it, by pluperfect or its equivalent: the Vulgate, Coverdale, the Bishops', and the modern versions that follow the NIV. The King James translators, at this verse, chose the Hebrew over their base text, and the choice is visible in the one word they struck.

### C. The Naming, and the Help Not Found (2:19b–20)

The man names what is brought to him, and the Hebrew says the naming was final: וְכֹל אֲשֶׁר יִקְרָא־לוֹ הָאָדָם נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה הוּא שְׁמוֹ, "and whatever the man called it, a living soul, that was its name." The phrase נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה, "living soul," is here applied to every creature the man names; it is the phrase the man himself was called at 2:7, and chapter 2.2 has shown what the English did with it. The 1611 has "euery liuing creature" here and "a liuing soule" there, and the reader of the English cannot see that the man named the animals by the name he bore.[^nb23m]

It is the man's first act. He has been formed, placed, commanded; now he speaks, and what he speaks becomes the names of things. The verb is וַיִּקְרָא, "and he called," the verb the first telling used of God naming the day and the night, the heaven, the earth and the seas (1:5, 1:8, 1:10).[^nb23n] What God did in the first telling the man does in the second, and the text does not qualify it: "that was its name." The book returns to the man's speech at 2:23–24, where he speaks a second time and what he says becomes a law.

Then the clause the search was for. וּלְאָדָם לֹא־מָצָא עֵזֶר כְּנֶגְדּוֹ: "but for the man he found not a help as over against him." The vav is prefixed to a noun, the clause turns against the sequence, and every version but one marked the turn: the Septuagint τῷ τε Ἀδάμ, the Vulgate *Adæ vero*, Coverdale "But vnto man," the Geneva "but for Adám," the Bishops' "but for man," the 1611 "but for Adam"; the Great Bible alone has "And for man."[^nb23o] The naming ends where it began, with the man alone. The versions differ on who did not find. The Hebrew verb is third person, "he found not," and the subject is left to the reader, God or the man; the Septuagint made it passive, οὐχ εὑρέθη, "there was not found"; the Vulgate *non inueniebatur*, "was not being found"; the Geneva and the Bishops' "founde he not"; the 1611 "there was not found."[^nb23p] Nothing turns on it. What the verse says in every version is that the animals were tried and were not the help, and that the woman, when she is made in the next verse, is made because they were not.

### D. Where the Man Is Named

A last matter of the versions belongs here, because it begins in this passage. The Hebrew of Genesis 2 calls the man הָאָדָם, "the man," with the definite article, throughout; it is a description, not yet a name. The versions begin to write "Adam" at different points. The Septuagint has τῷ Ἀδάμ from 2:16 and Ἀδάμ at 2:19, 2:20 and after; the Vulgate *Adam* from 2:19; Coverdale keeps "man" through the chapter; the Great Bible has "Adam" at 2:18 and 2:21 and "man" between; the Geneva keeps "the man" but has "Adám" at 2:20 in the clause "but for Adám"; the Bishops' "man" to 2:20 and "Adam" at 2:21; the 1611 "the man" at 2:15–18, "Adam" at 2:19–21 and 2:23, and "the man" again at 2:22 and 2:25, with the margin at 2:15 "Or, Adam" and at 2:19 "Or, the man," which is the translators telling the reader that the Hebrew is the same word in both places and that the choice between "the man" and "Adam" is theirs.[^nb23q] The book follows the 1611's margin rather than its text, and calls him the man until the text names him.

## 2.4 The Woman Made, and the Man Speaks (2:21–25)

> **Genesis 2:21–25 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **21** וַיַּפֵּל יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים תַּרְדֵּמָה עַל־הָאָדָם וַיִּישָׁן וַיִּקַּח אַחַת מִצַּלְעֹתָיו וַיִּסְגֹּר בָּשָׂר תַּחְתֶּנָּה׃
> **22** וַיִּבֶן יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים אֶת־הַצֵּלָע אֲשֶׁר־לָקַח מִן־הָאָדָם לְאִשָּׁה וַיְבִאֶהָ אֶל־הָאָדָם׃
> **23** וַיֹּאמֶר הָאָדָם זֹאת הַפַּעַם עֶצֶם מֵעֲצָמַי וּבָשָׂר מִבְּשָׂרִי לְזֹאת יִקָּרֵא אִשָּׁה כִּי מֵאִישׁ לֻקֳחָה־זֹּאת׃
> **24** עַל־כֵּן יַעֲזָב־אִישׁ אֶת־אָבִיו וְאֶת־אִמּוֹ וְדָבַק בְּאִשְׁתּוֹ וְהָיוּ לְבָשָׂר אֶחָד׃
> **25** וַיִּהְיוּ שְׁנֵיהֶם עֲרוּמִּים הָאָדָם וְאִשְׁתּוֹ וְלֹא יִתְבֹּשָׁשׁוּ׃

> **Genesis 2:21–25 (1611)**
> **21** And the LORD God caused a deepe sleepe to fall vpon Adam, and hee slept; and he tooke one of his ribs, and closed vp the flesh in stead thereof.
> **22** And the rib which the LORD God had taken from man, made hee a woman, & brought her vnto the man.
> **23** And Adam said, This is now bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh: she shalbe called Woman, because shee was taken out of man.
> **24** Therefore shall a man leaue his father and his mother, and shall cleaue vnto his wife: and they shalbe one flesh.
> **25** And they were both naked, the man & his wife, and were not ashamed.[^nb24a]

### A. The Woman Made (2:21–22)

Six verbs of sequence carry the making of the woman, and there is no scene-clause among them: וַיַּפֵּל, "and he caused to fall"; וַיִּישָׁן, "and he slept"; וַיִּקַּח, "and he took"; וַיִּסְגֹּר, "and he closed"; וַיִּבֶן, "and he built"; וַיְבִאֶהָ, "and he brought her." The passage is the most continuous stretch of narrative in the second telling, one act after another with nothing set aside as background, and no version treats it otherwise. The Septuagint has καί at each, the Vulgate its variety of sequence particles, *Immisit ergo … cumque … tulit … repleuit … Et ædificauit … adduxit*, and the English "And … and … and."[^nb24b] The Geneva opens the passage with "Therefore," which reads the sleep as the consequence of the help not found, and the Great Bible with "also," but neither disturbs the order.[^nb24c]

The verb of the woman's making is not the verb of the man's. He was formed, יָצַר, the potter's word; she is built, בָּנָה, the word for a house. The versions kept the difference and the English margins record it: the Septuagint ᾠκοδόμησεν, the Vulgate *ædificauit*, and in English "made" in the text of every Bible from Coverdale to the 1611 with "Hebre. buylded" in the Bishops' margin and "Hebr. builded" in the 1611's.[^nb24d] The book notes the word and makes no more of it than the text does. She is built from a part of the man, brought to him, and nothing is said of breath: the man received the breath of life at 2:7 and became a living soul; the woman is taken from a living soul and is not said to receive anything, because she is made of what already lives.

This is the making of the second human being, and the reading this book carries places it on the sixth day. The ground of the placing is not in these verses, which name no day; it is in the first telling, which says that on the sixth day God made mankind male and female (1:27) and blessed them and told them to multiply (1:28). Before 2:22 there is no female, and the blessing of 1:28 cannot have been spoken. After it there is, and the second telling has arrived at the point the first telling reached at the close of its sixth day. Everything between 2:7 and 2:22, the garden, the trees, the commandment, the animals and their naming, lies within the days the first telling passed over in the single verb "made." Part 3 will show that the day does not end with 2:25.

### B. The Man Speaks (2:23)

The man's second speech is the first human sentence in the Bible addressed to no one. At 2:19–20 he named the animals; the text reports the naming but not the words. Here the words are given, and they are a recognition and a naming in one: זֹאת הַפַּעַם, "this one, this time," or "this at last," after the animals that were not the help; עֶצֶם מֵעֲצָמַי וּבָשָׂר מִבְּשָׂרִי, "bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh"; and then the name, לְזֹאת יִקָּרֵא אִשָּׁה כִּי מֵאִישׁ לֻקֳחָה־זֹּאת, "this shall be called woman, because from man was this taken."

The name is a play on words, אִשָּׁה from אִישׁ, and the versions divide on whether to carry it. The Septuagint could not: Γυνή, "woman," and ἀνήρ, "man," share no letters in Greek, and the Greek reader is given the explanation without the thing it explains, αὕτη κληθήσεται Γυνή, ὅτι ἐκ τοῦ ἀνδρὸς αὐτῆς ἐλήμφθη αὕτη.[^nb24e] Jerome carried it. *Hæc vocabitur Virago, quoniam de viro sumpta est*: he chose for "woman" a Latin word, *virago*, formed on *vir*, "man," so that the Latin reader hears in the name the word it is taken from, as the Hebrew reader does.[^nb24f] It is the one place in these chapters where the Vulgate does something the Hebrew does and the Greek could not, and it shows a translator who heard the Hebrew as sound and not only as sense. The English, by an accident of the language, half-carries it: "woman" contains "man," and every English Bible has "she shalbe called woman, because she was taken out of man," so that the English reader hears part of what the Hebrew reader hears without any translator's contrivance.[^nb24g] The Geneva alone points it out, in a margin that offers an alternative and explains the Hebrew: "Or, Mannes, because she cōmeth of man: for in Ebr. Ish is man, and Ishah the woman."[^nb24h]

### C. The Man's Law (2:24)

The next verse is a law, and the question the book must answer is who speaks it.

עַל־כֵּן יַעֲזָב־אִישׁ אֶת־אָבִיו וְאֶת־אִמּוֹ וְדָבַק בְּאִשְׁתּוֹ וְהָיוּ לְבָשָׂר אֶחָד: "therefore shall a man leave his father and his mother, and cleave to his wife, and they shall be one flesh." The verse opens with עַל־כֵּן, "therefore," drawing a consequence from what has just been said, and it is the only sentence in Genesis 1–3 cast as a general rule about people who do not yet exist: there is no father or mother in the world, and the man who speaks has neither. The text gives no new speaker. The verb of speech at 2:23 is וַיֹּאמֶר הָאָדָם, "and the man said," and nothing between 2:23 and 2:24 closes his speech or opens another. The tradition has nonetheless read 2:24 as the narrator's comment, and two of the Gospels and one of the Epistles quote it as a word of God about marriage.[^nb24i] The 1611 marks the verse with an asterisk and lists the New Testament places in its margin, and the Bishops' has the same references.[^nb24j]

This book reads 2:24 as the text presents it, as the continuation of the man's speech. Nothing in the Hebrew separates the two verses; the "therefore" answers the man's own "because"; and the sentence is a rule that a man who has just recognised his wife might draw, not a rule that a narrator standing outside the story needs to state here rather than anywhere else. Read so, the man's second speech has two parts, a recognition of the woman as his own flesh and a law about what a man owes his wife, and the law is his, spoken by him, before God has said anything to either of them about the other. The consequence of that reading comes at 3:12, where the same man, asked what he has done, speaks of "the woman whom thou gauest to be with me" and does not hold fast to her. The chapter on that verse (3.2) sets the two speeches side by side. Here it is enough to have shown that the text allows, and by its silence about any change of speaker invites, the reading that the man made the law he would break.

The versions leave the question where the Hebrew leaves it. None supplies a speaker. The Septuagint has ἕνεκεν τούτου, the Vulgate *Quamobrem*, Coverdale, the Great and the Bishops' "For this cause," the Geneva and the 1611 "Therefore," all of them the man's "therefore" or the narrator's, as the reader decides.[^nb24k]

Two words of the verse have histories of their own, and the book records them. The verb וְדָבַק, "and he shall cleave," is the verb of adhering, sticking to, and the Vulgate has *adhærebit*, the Septuagint προσκολληθήσεται, both exact. The English divided: Coverdale, the Geneva and the 1611 "cleue / cleaue," the Great Bible and the Bishops' "shalbe ioyned with his wyfe"; the modern versions have "be united to."[^nb24l] "Cleave" and "adhere" keep the Hebrew's picture of one thing holding fast to another; "joined" and "united" state the result and lose the holding. The second word is one the Hebrew does not have. וְהָיוּ לְבָשָׂר אֶחָד is "and they shall be one flesh"; the Septuagint has καὶ ἔσονται οἱ δύο εἰς σάρκα μίαν, "and the two shall be one flesh," and the Vulgate *erunt duo in carne una*.[^nb24m] "The two" is the Greek's, and it is the form in which the Gospels and Paul quote the verse. Coverdale has "they two shalbe one flesh," from the Latin; the Great Bible dropped the number, "they shall become one flesh," and the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 follow the Hebrew's "they."[^nb24n] The English reader who knows the verse from the New Testament knows it in the Greek form; the English reader who knows it from Genesis knows it in the Hebrew.

### D. Naked, and Not Ashamed (2:25)

The second telling closes on the state of the two before the serpent. וַיִּהְיוּ שְׁנֵיהֶם עֲרוּמִּים, "and they were both naked," הָאָדָם וְאִשְׁתּוֹ, "the man and his wife," וְלֹא יִתְבֹּשָׁשׁוּ, "and they were not ashamed."

The word for naked is the word to hold in mind across the chapter division. עֲרוּמִּים at 2:25 and עָרוּם, the serpent's "subtle," at 3:1 are written with the same letters in the codex, ע־ר־ו־ם, and the two verses stand next to each other with no break between them; the codex has no gap here, though it has one before 2:4, 3:16, 3:17 and 3:22.[^nb24o] The last word of the second telling and the first description of the serpent are a pun, and the pun is the seam between the man and woman's innocence and the serpent's cunning. Chapter 3.1 reads the second half of it; this chapter notes only that the first half is placed here, at the end of the sixth day's making, as the condition in which the two are found when the serpent speaks. No version carries the pun, because no other language has one word for naked and for shrewd; the Septuagint has γυμνοί here and φρονιμώτατος there, the Vulgate *nudus* and *callidior*, the English "naked" and "sotyller / subtil / suttiller / subtill."[^nb24p]

The versions do one small thing to the verse. The Septuagint adds "the two" once more, καὶ ἦσαν οἱ δύο γυμνοί, where the Hebrew has שְׁנֵיהֶם, "the two of them," so that for once the Greek's addition is the Hebrew's own word.[^nb24q] The Reformation Bibles read the verse through the Fall that follows it. The Geneva's note says "For before sinne entred, all things were honest and comely," and the Bishops' "The shame of nakednes is a token of our nature corrupted."[^nb24r] Both notes are true to the story's sequence: the shame is not yet, and the reader is told so before it comes.

### E. The Second Telling Closed

From 2:4 to 2:25 the second telling has run in one order: a heading; a scene, the earth bare and unmanned; the man formed; the garden planted and the trees grown; the river; the man placed and commanded; the animals formed and named; the woman built and brought; the man's recognition and his law; the two naked and unashamed. Every act is a verb of sequence and the Hebrew marks no act as prior to the one before it. The Septuagint reads the order so and twice adds a word to say so. The Vulgate moves the garden before the man and the animals into the background. Coverdale and the Bishops' follow the Latin at 2:19; the Great Bible, the Geneva and the 1611 follow the Hebrew at every verb. The man has been alone with God, has been commanded, has named the creation, and has spoken a law; the woman has been made and named and has heard none of it. That is the state of things when the third chapter opens without a break, and it is the state Part 3 reads the Fall against.

# Part 3 — The Knowledge: Genesis 3:1–24

## 3.1 The Serpent (3:1–5)

> **Genesis 3:1–5 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **1** וְהַנָּחָשׁ הָיָה עָרוּם מִכֹּל חַיַּת הַשָּׂדֶה אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים וַיֹּאמֶר אֶל־הָאִשָּׁה אַף כִּי־אָמַר אֱלֹהִים לֹא תֹאכְלוּ מִכֹּל עֵץ הַגָּן׃
> **2** וַתֹּאמֶר הָאִשָּׁה אֶל־הַנָּחָשׁ מִפְּרִי עֵץ־הַגָּן נֹאכֵל׃
> **3** וּמִפְּרִי הָעֵץ אֲשֶׁר בְּתוֹךְ־הַגָּן אָמַר אֱלֹהִים לֹא תֹאכְלוּ מִמֶּנּוּ וְלֹא תִגְּעוּ בּוֹ פֶּן־תְּמֻתוּן׃
> **4** וַיֹּאמֶר הַנָּחָשׁ אֶל־הָאִשָּׁה לֹא־מוֹת תְּמֻתוּן׃
> **5** כִּי יֹדֵעַ אֱלֹהִים כִּי בְּיוֹם אֲכָלְכֶם מִמֶּנּוּ וְנִפְקְחוּ עֵינֵיכֶם וִהְיִיתֶם כֵּאלֹהִים יֹדְעֵי טוֹב וָרָע׃

> **Genesis 3:1–5 (1611)**
> **1** Now the serpent was more subtill then any beast of the field, which the LORD God had made, and he said vnto the woman, Yea, hath God said, Ye shall not eate of euery tree of the garden?
> **2** And the woman said vnto the serpent, We may eate of the fruite of the trees of the garden:
> **3** But of the fruit of the tree, which is in the midst of the garden, God hath said, Ye shall not eate of it, neither shall ye touch it, lest ye die.
> **4** And the Serpent said vnto the woman, Ye shall not surely die:
> **5** For God doeth know, that in the day ye eate thereof, then your eyes shalbee opened: and yee shall bee as Gods, knowing good and euill.[^nb31a]

### A. The Scene, and the Pun (3:1a)

The third chapter opens as the second did not, with a scene-clause, and as the second did, without a break. וְהַנָּחָשׁ הָיָה עָרוּם: the vav is prefixed to the noun, "and the serpent was subtle," the verb stands second, and by the Method's rule the clause steps out of the sequence to introduce a character and his quality before he acts. The codex runs straight on from 2:25 with no open space, so that the eye passes from עֲרוּמִּים, the two naked, to עָרוּם, the serpent subtle, in consecutive lines, and the letters of the two words are the same.[^nb31b] The last state of the man and woman and the first quality of the serpent are one word in two senses. What the pun says the story will show: the one who is ערום in the sense of shrewd will bring the two who are ערום in the sense of bare to know it.

Every version marked the scene-clause, and the marking is the clearest case in the three chapters of the versions agreeing with the Hebrew's shape. The Septuagint has Ὁ δὲ ὄφις ἦν φρονιμώτατος, the Vulgate *Sed & serpens erat callidior*, Coverdale "But the serpent was sotyller," the Great Bible "But the serpent was sotyller," the Bishops' "And the serpent was suttiller," the Geneva "Now the serpent was more subtil," and the 1611 "Now the serpent was more subtill."[^nb31c] Six of seven mark the turn, and the 1611's "Now" is the Geneva's, the first "Now the serpent" in English. The King James translators, who levelled the scene-clauses of 1:2 and 4:1 to "And," kept this one, because their predecessor had made it the most familiar opening in the book after "In the beginning."

The pun no version could carry, because no other language has one word for naked and for shrewd; what the versions did with the second sense is the matter of record. The Septuagint's serpent is φρονιμώτατος πάντων τῶν θηρίων, "the most prudent of all the beasts," and φρόνιμος is a word of praise in Greek, the word for the wise steward and the prudent virgins of the Gospels.[^nb31d] The Greek serpent is not cunning; he is wise. The Vulgate's is *callidior*, "cleverer," a word that can be praise or blame; the Reformation English is "sotyller," "suttiller," "more subtil," "more subtill," a word that in the sixteenth century meant fine, penetrating, skilful before it meant sly.[^nb31e] "Crafty," which is what the modern reader expects, is the choice of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The older versions left the serpent's quality open, as the Hebrew does: עָרוּם is the word Proverbs uses for the prudent man who foresees evil and hides himself, and it is the word here for the creature who foresees what the fruit will do.[^nb31f] The book keeps the Hebrew's ambivalence and the Geneva's and the King James's "subtle." The serpent is not yet judged in the verse that introduces him.

The clause ends by placing the serpent inside the creation: אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים, "which the LORD God had made." He is one of the beasts of the field, formed at 2:19 and named by the man, and he is the most ערום of them. The versions all keep the relative clause. The Reformation Bibles supplied in their margins what the text does not say, that the serpent was an instrument: the Geneva, "God suffered Satan to make the serpent his instrument and to speake in him," and again, "As Satan cā change him selfe into an Angel of light, so did he abuse the wisdome of the serpēt to deceaue man"; the Bishops', in a note whose first words are cut in the copy read, that the devil first would make the woman doubt God's word.[^nb31g] The text says a beast spoke. The notes say who spoke through him. The book reads the text and records the notes.

### B. The Question Put in the Plural (3:1b–3)

The serpent speaks to the woman, and the first thing to observe is the number of his verb. לֹא תֹאכְלוּ, "ye shall not eat": the commandment of 2:16–17, which was given to the man in the singular on a day when the woman did not exist, is quoted back to the woman in the plural, as a commandment to both. The woman answers in the same number, נֹאכֵל, "we may eat," and לֹא תֹאכְלוּ … וְלֹא תִגְּעוּ … פֶּן־תְּמֻתוּן, "ye shall not eat, neither shall ye touch, lest ye die." The Hebrew of 2:17 and the Hebrew of 3:3 are not the same commandment. One was spoken to a man alone; the other is a commandment to two, reported by one of them, with a clause the original did not have, "neither shall ye touch it," and without the original's doubled verb, מוֹת תָּמוּת, in place of which she has פֶּן־תְּמֻתוּן, "lest ye die."

Whether the woman heard the commandment from God, or from the man, or in a form the text does not report, the text does not say. What it says is that she was not there when it was given, that she quotes it in a form that differs from the form given, and that the serpent's first move was to put it to her in the plural she uses. Chapter 2.2 noted that the Septuagint had already made 2:17 plural, so that in Greek the commandment and its quotation agree; the Hebrew keeps them apart, and the difference is what lets the reader see that the woman is repeating a report.[^nb31h] The English versions keep the Hebrew's numbers exactly, "thou" at 2:16–17 and "ye" from 3:1, and the reader of the 1611 can see the shift as plainly as the reader of the codex.[^nb31i]

The serpent's opening words are hard, and the versions show it. אַף כִּי־אָמַר אֱלֹהִים is two particles and a verb, "indeed that God said," or "even though God said," or, as a question, "is it so that God said?" The Septuagint has Τί ὅτι εἶπεν ὁ θεός, "why is it that God said"; the Vulgate *Cur præcepit vobis Deus*, "why has God commanded you"; Coverdale "Yee, hath God sayde in dede"; the Great Bible "yee, hath the Lorde God sayde"; the Geneva "Yea, hathe God in dede said"; the Bishops' "yea, hath God saide"; the 1611 "Yea, hath God said," with the margin "Heb. Yea, because, &c."[^nb31j] The Greek and Latin heard a question about God's reason; the English heard a question about the fact, tinged with incredulity, and the 1611's margin admits that the Hebrew is neither and is barely a sentence. The serpent's first utterance is a half-question that leaves the woman to finish it, and every translator has had to finish it for her.

### C. The Denial and the Promise (3:4–5)

The serpent's second speech has two parts. The first denies the penalty: לֹא־מוֹת תְּמֻתוּן, "not dying shall ye die," the doubled idiom of 2:17 returned with a negative in front of it, and again in the plural.[^nb31k] The versions carried the idiom here as they did there: the Septuagint Οὐ θανάτῳ ἀποθανεῖσθε, the Vulgate *Nequaquam morte moriemini*, the English "Tush, ye shall not dye the death" (Coverdale), "ye shall not dye the death" (the Great, the Geneva, the Bishops'), and the 1611 "Ye shall not surely die," which is the 1611's "surely die" of 2:17 negated.[^nb31l] Coverdale's "Tush" is his own; no other witness gives the serpent an interjection.

The second part is the promise, and it contains the verse of Genesis 1–3 on which every version had to decide what the Hebrew word for God means. כִּי יֹדֵעַ אֱלֹהִים כִּי בְּיוֹם אֲכָלְכֶם מִמֶּנּוּ וְנִפְקְחוּ עֵינֵיכֶם וִהְיִיתֶם כֵּאלֹהִים יֹדְעֵי טוֹב וָרָע: "for God knows that in the day ye eat of it your eyes shall be opened, and ye shall be as *elohim*, knowing good and evil." The word אֱלֹהִים is the word the first telling used for God from its first verse, a plural noun with a singular verb; here it stands after a preposition with no verb to fix its number, and the participle that follows, יֹדְעֵי, "knowing," is plural. The serpent's sentence can be read "as God, who knows good and evil," or "as gods, knowing good and evil," and the Hebrew does not choose.

The versions did, and they did not agree. The Septuagint has ἔσεσθε ὡς θεοί, "ye shall be as gods," plural; the Vulgate *eritis sicut dij*, plural.[^nb31m] Coverdale has "ye shall be as God," singular.[^nb31n] The Great Bible has "ye shalbe euē as goddes," the Geneva "ye shalbe as gods," the Bishops' "ye shalbe euē as gods," and the 1611 "yee shall bee as Gods," with a capital, all plural.[^nb31o] The literal and revised versions of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries have "as God" or "like God," singular again. The word that the whole tradition renders as one God at 1:1 was rendered as many gods at 3:5 by the Greek, the Latin and the English from 1540 to 1611, and by no one since. The Geneva's note reads the plural as the serpent's slander of God's motive: "As thogh he shulde say, God doeth not forbid you to eat of the frute, saue that he knoweth that if you shulde eat thereof, you shulde be like to him."[^nb31p] The book does not decide the number. It observes that the word is the same word, that the versions could not agree what it meant when the serpent used it, and that the serpent's promise, whichever way it is read, is the promise of 1:26 in another mouth: to be like God, or like the gods, knowing good and evil. The book returns to that at 3:22, where God says the promise came true.

### D. What the Serpent Knew

The serpent's promise is accurate in every particular. The eyes will be opened (3:7); the two will know good and evil (3:22, in God's own words); and they will not die on the day they eat. The tradition has called him a liar, and the Geneva's note on 3:4 calls the denial "Satans chiefest subtiltie, to cause vs not to feare Gods threatenings"; but the text gives him one falsehood at most, the denial of death, and even that the sequel does not straightforwardly refute, since the man lives nine hundred and thirty years (5:5). What the serpent does is tell the woman what the man was told and she was not, in the plural she uses, and add to it what God will confirm. The reading this book carries is that the serpent's speech is the first account of the knowledge of good and evil that either human being hears from anyone, that it is given to the one who was not present when the commandment was given, and that it is true. Chapter 3.2 reads the eating in that light.

## 3.2 The Eating and the Opening of the Eyes (3:6–13)

> **Genesis 3:6–8 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **6** וַתֵּרֶא הָאִשָּׁה כִּי טוֹב הָעֵץ לְמַאֲכָל וְכִי תַאֲוָה־הוּא לָעֵינַיִם וְנֶחְמָד הָעֵץ לְהַשְׂכִּיל וַתִּקַּח מִפִּרְיוֹ וַתֹּאכַל וַתִּתֵּן גַּם־לְאִישָׁהּ עִמָּהּ וַיֹּאכַל׃
> **7** וַתִּפָּקַחְנָה עֵינֵי שְׁנֵיהֶם וַיֵּדְעוּ כִּי עֵירֻמִּם הֵם וַיִּתְפְּרוּ עֲלֵה תְאֵנָה וַיַּעֲשׂוּ לָהֶם חֲגֹרֹת׃
> **8** וַיִּשְׁמְעוּ אֶת־קוֹל יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים מִתְהַלֵּךְ בַּגָּן לְרוּחַ הַיּוֹם וַיִּתְחַבֵּא הָאָדָם וְאִשְׁתּוֹ מִפְּנֵי יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים בְּתוֹךְ עֵץ הַגָּן׃

> **Genesis 3:6–8 (1611)**
> **6** And when the woman saw, that the tree was good for food, and that it was pleasant to the eyes, and a tree to be desired to make one wise, she tooke of the fruit thereof, and did eate, and gaue also vnto her husband with her, and hee did eate.
> **7** And the eyes of them both were opened, & they knew that they were naked, and they sewed figge leaues together, and made themselues aprons.
> **8** And they heard the voyce of the LORD God, walking in the garden in the coole of the day: and Adam and his wife hid themselues from the presence of the LORD God, amongst the trees of the garden.[^nb32a]

### A. The Eating (3:6)

The verse is five verbs of sequence, all in the feminine until the last: she saw, she took, she ate, she gave, and he ate. וַתֵּרֶא … וַתִּקַּח … וַתֹּאכַל וַתִּתֵּן … וַיֹּאכַל. The woman acts through four verbs and the man through one, and the one is the same verb as hers. The text does not say that the man was persuaded, or that he hesitated, or that he asked; it says that she gave to him and he ate. What it does say about him is where he was: גַּם־לְאִישָׁהּ עִמָּהּ, "also to her husband *with her*." The man who received the commandment, and who alone had heard it from God, is beside the woman at the tree when she is told what the fruit will do, and the text records his presence in a single word.

That word has a history in the versions, and the history matters because the reading of this book rests on it. The Septuagint keeps it: καὶ ἔδωκεν καὶ τῷ ἀνδρὶ αὐτῆς μετ᾿ αὐτῆς, καὶ ἔφαγον, "and she gave also to her husband with her, and they ate."[^nb32b] The Vulgate drops it: *deditque viro suo, qui comedit*, "and she gave to her husband, who ate."[^nb32c] The Latin reader is told that the woman ate and then gave, and may suppose the man elsewhere; for eleven centuries the Bible of the West had the man absent from the tree, or at least unplaced. Coverdale, working from Luther and the Latin, drops it too: "and gaue vnto hir husbande also therof, and he ate."[^nb32d] The Great Bible of 1540 is the first English Bible to put the man back beside the woman, and it does so with emphasis: "and gaue vnto her husband beynge with her, which dyd eate also."[^nb32e] The Bishops' keeps "beyng with her," the Geneva and the 1611 "with her."[^nb32f] The man's presence at the eating is in the Hebrew and the Greek, was lost in the Latin and in the first English Bible, and has stood in every English Bible since 1540.

The versions also divide on how the verse opens. The Hebrew וַתֵּרֶא is a sequence verb, "and she saw"; the Septuagint has καὶ ἴδεν ἡ γυνή, the Vulgate *Vidit igitur mulier*, Coverdale "And the woman sawe," the Great and the Bishops' "And so the woman," the Geneva "So the woman."[^nb32g] The 1611 alone has "And when the woman saw … she tooke," making the seeing a subordinate clause and the taking the main event; it is a smoothing of the English and does not change the order.[^nb32h] Two glosses of the sixteenth century read the man's motive, which the text does not give. The Geneva's note on the verse says he ate "Not so muche to please his wife, as moued by ambicion at her persuasion," and the 1611 records in its margin that the tree "pleasant to the eyes" is in the Hebrew "a desire" to them.[^nb32i] The book records the glosses and returns to the text, which says only that he was with her and that he ate.

### B. The Eyes Opened (3:7)

What the serpent promised happens in the serpent's words. He had said וְנִפְקְחוּ עֵינֵיכֶם, "and your eyes shall be opened" (3:5); the narrator says וַתִּפָּקַחְנָה עֵינֵי שְׁנֵיהֶם, "and the eyes of them both were opened." He had said they would know; they know, וַיֵּדְעוּ. What they know is that they are naked, and the codex writes the word differently from the way it wrote it at 2:25. There the two were עֲרוּמִּים; here they are עֵירֻמִּם, with a yod after the first letter and the vav gone, and the same spelling holds at 3:10 and 3:11, עֵירֹם.[^nb32j] The consonants have changed with the knowledge. Before the eating the word for their nakedness was the word that made the pun with the serpent's subtlety; after it the pun is over and the word is written as it will be written for the rest of the Bible. Whether the scribe of the codex meant the change as more than orthography the book cannot say; that he made it, and made it at the verse where the two first know they are bare, is on the page.

The versions have no way to show the change of spelling, and none of them tries. What they do with the verse is otherwise uniform: the Septuagint καὶ διηνοίχθησαν οἱ ὀφθαλμοὶ τῶν δύο, καὶ ἔγνωσαν ὅτι γυμνοὶ ἦσαν, the Vulgate *Et aperti sunt oculi amborum: cumque cognouissent se esse nudos*, and the English "the eyes of them both were opened, and they knewe that they were naked," with "Then" in Coverdale, the Geneva and the Bishops' and "And" in the Great and the 1611.[^nb32k] The garments they make, חֲגֹרֹת, are girdles, things bound about the loins; the Greek has περιζώματα and the Latin *perizomata*, the Greek word borrowed; the English divided between "apurns / aperns / apernes / aprons" (Coverdale, the Great, the Bishops', the 1611) and the Geneva's "breeches," which the Geneva's margin explains as "Ebr. things to girde about thē to hide their priuities," and the 1611's margin as "Or, things to gird about."[^nb32l]

### C. The Voice and the Wind (3:8)

They hear a voice, or a sound: קוֹל is both, and it is the voice of the LORD God מִתְהַלֵּךְ בַּגָּן לְרוּחַ הַיּוֹם, "walking in the garden in the *ruach* of the day." The word רוּחַ is the word of 1:2, where it was the רוּחַ אֱלֹהִים, the Spirit or wind or breath of God, moving on the face of the waters, and every version rendered it there as Spirit. Here the same word stands in a phrase that no version has rendered literally, because "the wind of the day" is not English, or Greek, or Latin, and the translators had to decide what the phrase meant before they could write it.

The Septuagint decided it meant a time: τὸ δειλινόν, "in the afternoon," a reading in which the wind has become the hour when the wind blows.[^nb32m] The Vulgate kept the wind and added the hour: *ad auram post meridiem*, "at the breeze after midday."[^nb32n] The English, from Coverdale to the 1611 without exception, has "in the coole of the day," a paraphrase in which neither wind nor spirit survives.[^nb32o] Two of the English Bibles confess in the margin what they would not put in the text. The Geneva prints "coole" and notes "Or, winde"; the 1611 prints "coole" and notes "Heb. wind."[^nb32p] The reader of the King James text meets the Spirit of God at 1:2 and the cool of the day at 3:8 and has no way of knowing that they are one Hebrew word; the reader of the King James margin is told, at 3:8, that the word is wind. The book records the three readings, spirit, wind and afternoon, as the tradition's own account of the range of רוּחַ, and returns to the range in Part 4.

The two hide, וַיִּתְחַבֵּא, "and the man and his wife hid themselves," מִפְּנֵי, "from the face of," the LORD God. The versions keep it: ἀπὸ προσώπου, *a facie*, "from the presence" in every English Bible. The Geneva's marginal reading of the hiding is that "They began to fele their miserie, but they soght not to God for remedie."[^nb32q] The 1611's chapter argument had summarised the chapter's second half in one word: "9 God arraigneth them."[^nb32r]

### D. The Examination (3:9–13)

> **Genesis 3:9–13 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **9** וַיִּקְרָא יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים אֶל־הָאָדָם וַיֹּאמֶר לוֹ אַיֶּכָּה׃
> **10** וַיֹּאמֶר אֶת־קֹלְךָ שָׁמַעְתִּי בַּגָּן וָאִירָא כִּי־עֵירֹם אָנֹכִי וָאֵחָבֵא׃
> **11** וַיֹּאמֶר מִי הִגִּיד לְךָ כִּי עֵירֹם אָתָּה הֲמִן־הָעֵץ אֲשֶׁר צִוִּיתִיךָ לְבִלְתִּי אֲכָל־מִמֶּנּוּ אָכָלְתָּ׃
> **12** וַיֹּאמֶר הָאָדָם הָאִשָּׁה אֲשֶׁר נָתַתָּה עִמָּדִי הִוא נָתְנָה־לִּי מִן־הָעֵץ וָאֹכֵל׃
> **13** וַיֹּאמֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים לָאִשָּׁה מַה־זֹּאת עָשִׂית וַתֹּאמֶר הָאִשָּׁה הַנָּחָשׁ הִשִּׁיאַנִי וָאֹכֵל׃

> **Genesis 3:9–13 (1611)**
> **9** And the LORD God called vnto Adam, and said vnto him, Where art thou?
> **10** And he said, I heard thy voice in the garden: and I was afraid, because I was naked, and I hid my selfe.
> **11** And he said, Who told thee, that thou wast naked? Hast thou eaten of the tree, whereof I commanded thee, that thou shouldest not eate?
> **12** And the man said, The woman whom thou gauest to be with mee, shee gaue me of the tree, and I did eate.
> **13** And the LORD God said vnto the woman, What is this that thou hast done? And the woman said, The Serpent beguiled me, and I did eate.[^nb32s]

God calls the man. Not the two: וַיִּקְרָא … אֶל־הָאָדָם, "he called to the man," and the question is singular, אַיֶּכָּה, "where art thou?" The examination that follows is addressed to the man through three verses, and only at 3:13 does God turn to the woman. The order is the order of the commandment. The one who was commanded is the one who is asked; the one who was not present when the commandment was given is asked second, and asked a different question. To the man: "Who told thee that thou wast naked? Hast thou eaten of the tree whereof I commanded thee?" The commandment is quoted back to him in the singular in which it was given, צִוִּיתִיךָ, "I commanded *thee*." To the woman: מַה־זֹּאת עָשִׂית, "what is this thou hast done?" The man is asked about a commandment; the woman is asked about an act. The Septuagint, which had made the commandment plural at 2:17, keeps God's question to the man singular here, ἐνετειλάμην σοι, and adds the man's name to the call, Ἀδάμ, ποῦ εἶ; "Adam, where art thou?"[^nb32t]

The man's answer is the verse this chapter has been reading toward. הָאִשָּׁה אֲשֶׁר נָתַתָּה עִמָּדִי הִוא נָתְנָה־לִּי מִן־הָעֵץ וָאֹכֵל: "the woman whom thou gavest with me, she gave me of the tree, and I ate." Set it beside the man's two earlier speeches. At 2:23 he said זֹאת הַפַּעַם עֶצֶם מֵעֲצָמַי, "this at last is bone of my bones"; at 2:24, on the reading this book has argued, he said that a man shall cleave to his wife and the two shall be one flesh. At 3:12 he says "the woman," not "my wife" and not "bone of my bones"; he says "whom thou gavest," placing her origin in God's act and not in his own recognition; and he says הִוא, "she," with the emphatic pronoun the Hebrew does not need, "*she* gave me." The word עִמָּדִי, "with me," is the word of 3:6, עִמָּהּ, "with her," turned round: there she gave to her husband with her; here he speaks of the woman given to be with him. The man who spoke the law of cleaving does not cleave. He does not hold fast to her; he holds her out. The versions keep every element of the sentence, and two of them make the "with me" a description of what the woman was for: the Vulgate *Mulier, quam dedisti mihi sociam*, "the woman whom thou gavest me as a companion," and Coverdale, following it, "The woman which thou gauest me (to beare me company) gaue me of the tre, and I ate."[^nb32u] The Great Bible, the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 have "The woman which thou gauest to be with me, she gaue me of the tre," which is the Hebrew, pronoun and all.[^nb32v] The Geneva's note reads the speech as the book does: "His wickednes and lacke of true repentance appeareth in this that he burdeneth God with his faute, because he had geuen him a wife."[^nb32w]

One word of the 1611 deserves notice here. At 3:9 it has "the LORD God called vnto Adam," at 3:12 "And the man said." The Hebrew is הָאָדָם at both. The translators, who had been writing "Adam" since 2:19 and would write it again at 3:17, 3:20 and 3:21, wrote "the man" for the speaker of this one sentence; whether by design or by the accident of two hands, the effect is that in the English the excuse is spoken not by a named person but by the man.[^nb32x]

The woman's answer is shorter and blames what deceived her: הַנָּחָשׁ הִשִּׁיאַנִי וָאֹכֵל, "the serpent deceived me, and I ate." The verb is נָשָׁא, to lead astray; the Septuagint ἠπάτησέν με, the Vulgate *decepit me*, the English "disceaued me so, that I ate" (Coverdale), "begyled me, and I dyd eate" (the Great, the Bishops'), "beguiled me, and I did eat" (the Geneva, the 1611).[^nb32y] She does not say the man was with her. Neither does he. The serpent is not asked anything. He is the one party to the eating who is neither called nor questioned, and the sentences that follow begin with him.

### E. What the Examination Establishes

Three things, and each is a matter of the text's own order. The man was present at the eating, and every witness that reads the Hebrew says so; the two that do not, the Vulgate and Coverdale, are the two that lost the word. The man was examined first and on the commandment, the woman second and on the act, in the order in which the commandment had reached them. And the man's speech at 3:12 is the reverse of his speeches at 2:23–24: the one who named the woman his own flesh and spoke the law of cleaving now names her the woman God gave and lets the fault pass through her to God. The sentences of 3:14–19, which the next chapter reads, fall in the order the examination has set, and the word "because" falls where the examination has put the responsibility.
