## 3.3 The Sentences (3:14–19)

Three sentences are passed, and the text marks each in a different way. The serpent's is introduced by a verb of sequence and grounded with a "because." The woman's is introduced by nothing, after an open space in the codex, and has no "because." The man's is introduced by a noun-first clause, after a second open space, and grounded with a "because." The differences are the Hebrew's, they are on the page of the codex, and every version has kept them. This chapter reads the three sentences in order and then asks what the differences mean.

### A. The Serpent (3:14–15)

> **Genesis 3:14–15 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **14** וַיֹּאמֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים אֶל־הַנָּחָשׁ כִּי עָשִׂיתָ זֹּאת אָרוּר אַתָּה מִכָּל־הַבְּהֵמָה וּמִכֹּל חַיַּת הַשָּׂדֶה עַל־גְּחֹנְךָ תֵלֵךְ וְעָפָר תֹּאכַל כָּל־יְמֵי חַיֶּיךָ׃
> **15** וְאֵיבָה אָשִׁית בֵּינְךָ וּבֵין הָאִשָּׁה וּבֵין זַרְעֲךָ וּבֵין זַרְעָהּ הוּא יְשׁוּפְךָ רֹאשׁ וְאַתָּה תְּשׁוּפֶנּוּ עָקֵב׃

> **Genesis 3:14–15 (1611)**
> **14** And the LORD God said vnto the serpent, Because thou hast done this, thou art cursed aboue all cattell, and aboue euery beast of the field: vpon thy belly shalt thou goe, and dust shalt thou eate all the dayes of thy life.
> **15** And I will put enmitie betweene thee and the woman, and betweene thy seed and her seed: it shal bruise thy head, and thou shalt bruise his heele.[^nb33a]

The serpent, who was not questioned, is sentenced first, and the sentence opens with its ground: כִּי עָשִׂיתָ זֹּאת, "because thou hast done this." The Septuagint has Ὅτι ἐποίησας τοῦτο, the Vulgate *Quia fecisti hoc*, and every English Bible "Because thou hast done this."[^nb33b] What follows is a curse on the creature himself, אָרוּר אַתָּה, "cursed art thou," set above or apart from all the cattle and beasts of the field among which 3:1 had placed him as the most subtle. The most subtle of the beasts is made the most cursed of them.

The two clauses of the sentence say what the serpent will do and what he will eat. עַל־גְּחֹנְךָ תֵלֵךְ, "upon thy belly shalt thou go": the verb is הָלַךְ, the ordinary Hebrew word for going or walking, and the versions rendered it as such, πορεύσῃ, *gradieris*, "shalt thou go" in every English Bible.[^nb33c] The Septuagint doubled the noun, ἐπὶ τῷ στήθει σου καὶ τῇ κοιλίᾳ, "upon thy breast and belly," and the Vulgate kept the breast alone, *super pectus tuum*; the English have "belly." No version, and no Hebrew, makes the serpent creep here; the word for creeping things, רֶמֶשׂ, which the first telling used at 1:24–26 for the class the serpent belongs to, is not in the verse. The connection between the serpent's curse and the creeping things over which the man was given dominion is real, but it is not carried by this verb.

It is carried by the next word. וְעָפָר תֹּאכַל, "and dust shalt thou eat": עָפָר is the dust of 2:7, of which the man was made, and it will be the dust of 3:19, to which he will return. The serpent is sentenced to eat the substance of the man. The three verses are one thread in the Hebrew, and chapter 2.2 has shown what became of the thread in the versions: the Septuagint has γῆν, "earth," here and at 3:19, and χοῦν only at 2:7; the Vulgate *terram* here, *limo* at 2:7, *puluis* at 3:19; Coverdale "earth" here and at 3:19, "moulde" at 2:7.[^nb33d] The Great Bible of 1540 is the first version in any language on this shelf to have one word at all three places, "dust," and the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 kept it.[^nb33e] The English reader since 1540 can see that the serpent eats what the man is; the Greek and Latin reader could not.

The second verse of the sentence is the one the tradition has read most, and its variants are doctrinal in every direction. וְאֵיבָה אָשִׁית, "and enmity will I put," between the serpent and the woman and between his seed and hers; then הוּא יְשׁוּפְךָ רֹאשׁ וְאַתָּה תְּשׁוּפֶנּוּ עָקֵב, "he shall bruise thy head, and thou shalt bruise his heel." The pronoun הוּא is masculine and agrees with זֶרַע, the seed; the verb שׁוּף is used twice, once of each party, so that what the seed does to the serpent's head and what the serpent does to the seed's heel are the same act. The versions kept neither feature whole. The Septuagint has the masculine, αὐτός σου τηρήσει κεφαλήν, but its verb is τηρέω, "watch" or "lie in wait for," at both places.[^nb33f] The Vulgate has *ipsa conteret caput tuum*, "*she* shall crush thy head," a feminine that agrees with the woman and not the seed and has no warrant in the Hebrew or the Greek, and then a second verb for the serpent's part, *tu insidiaberis calcaneo eius*, "thou shalt lie in wait for her heel," so that the two acts are no longer one.[^nb33g] From *ipsa* descends the reading of the verse as a promise about the woman, and from the woman as Mary; it is the Latin's, and the Latin's alone. Coverdale and the Great Bible have the neuter, "The same shal treade downe thy head, and thou shalt treade vpon his hele," with one English verb for the two Hebrew ones; the Geneva has the masculine, "He shal breake thine head, & thou shalt bruise his heele," with two verbs; the Bishops' and the 1611 have the neuter again, "it shall treade downe thy head, and thou shalt treade vpon his heele" and "it shal bruise thy head, and thou shalt bruise his heele."[^nb33h] The 1611 is the one English version to render the Hebrew's single verb with a single verb, "bruise … bruise," and with the neuter "it" it declines to decide the sex of the seed, as the Hebrew, whose pronoun agrees with a grammatically masculine noun, also declines to make it a person. The Geneva's notes make the seed Christ and the serpent Satan; the Bishops' note reads "Victorie promised by Christe, to mans comfort."[^nb33i] The book records the notes and reads the verse as the sentence on the serpent that it is: the enmity is put between him and the woman he deceived, and it is her seed, not the man's, that is named.

### B. The Woman (3:16)

> **Genesis 3:16 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **16** אֶל־הָאִשָּׁה אָמַר הַרְבָּה אַרְבֶּה עִצְּבוֹנֵךְ וְהֵרֹנֵךְ בְּעֶצֶב תֵּלְדִי בָנִים וְאֶל־אִישֵׁךְ תְּשׁוּקָתֵךְ וְהוּא יִמְשָׁל־בָּךְ׃

> **Genesis 3:16 (1611)**
> **16** Unto the woman he said, I will greatly multiplie thy sorowe and thy conception. In sorow thou shalt bring forth children: and thy desire shall be to thy husband, and hee shall rule ouer thee.[^nb33j]

In the codex the woman's sentence stands after an open space, and it begins with no connective and no "because": אֶל־הָאִשָּׁה אָמַר, "unto the woman he said," the object first and the verb bare.[^nb33k] It is the one sentence of the three that is not tied to what precedes it and the one that is not given a ground. The serpent is sentenced because he did this; the man will be sentenced because he hearkened; the woman is told what will be. Chapter 3.2 showed that the examination had asked her not about a commandment but about an act; the sentence answers in kind. It states consequences and names no cause.

The versions kept the absence of a "because" without exception, and the book has found no version that supplies one. What they did with the absent connective varies. The Septuagint supplies καί, "and to the woman he said"; the Vulgate *Mulieri quoque dixit*, "to the woman also he said"; Coverdale "And vnto the woman he sayde"; the Great Bible and the Bishops' "But vnto the woman he sayde"; the Geneva and the 1611 nothing, "Vnto the woman he said," "Unto the woman he said," which is the Hebrew's shape exactly.[^nb33l] The King James, which levelled the scene-clauses of 1:2 and 4:1 to "And," here printed a sentence with no connective because the Hebrew had none, and the Geneva had done so before it.

The sentence has three clauses. The first, הַרְבָּה אַרְבֶּה עִצְּבוֹנֵךְ וְהֵרֹנֵךְ, doubles its verb in the idiom the versions carried at 2:17, "multiplying I will multiply thy pain and thy conception," and the second, בְּעֶצֶב תֵּלְדִי בָנִים, "in pain shalt thou bear children," names the pain again. The noun is עִצָּבוֹן, and it will be the man's noun too: at 3:17 he is told בְּעִצָּבוֹן תֹּאכְלֶנָּה, "in pain shalt thou eat of it." The woman's childbearing and the man's labour are one word in the Hebrew, and the Septuagint kept it, ἐν λύπαις at both, and so did the English, "in sorowe" at both from the Great Bible to the 1611; the Vulgate divided it, *in dolore* for her and *in laboribus* for him.[^nb33m] The two sentences are parallel in their pain, and the reader of the Latin cannot see it.

The third clause is the one the tradition has built on, and its history is the history of a single word. וְאֶל־אִישֵׁךְ תְּשׁוּקָתֵךְ וְהוּא יִמְשָׁל־בָּךְ: "and to thy husband thy תְּשׁוּקָה, and he shall rule over thee." The noun תְּשׁוּקָה occurs three times in the Bible, here, at 4:7 of sin's relation to Cain, and in the Song of Songs of the lover's to the beloved, and the versions have heard it three ways.[^nb33n] The Septuagint heard a turning: πρὸς τὸν ἄνδρα σου ἡ ἀποστροφή σου, "toward thy husband thy turning" or "thy recourse."[^nb33o] The Vulgate heard power, and wrote the clause as a sentence of subjection: *sub viri potestate eris*, "thou shalt be under thy husband's power."[^nb33p] With that rendering the woman's subordination entered the text of Western Christendom, in the place where the Hebrew has a noun whose sense is open. The English heard desire, and then hedged. Coverdale: "thy lust shal pertayne vnto ȳ hußbande, & he shal rule the," with the margin "Some reade: Thou shalt bowe downe thy self before thy husbande."[^nb33q] The Great Bible: "thy lust shall pertayne to thy husbande, and he shall haue the rule of the."[^nb33r] The Geneva: "thy desire *shal be subiect* to thine housband, and he shal rule ouer thee," the two words "shal be subiect" printed in the italic the Geneva used for what the translators supplied, and the margin sending the reader to 1 Corinthians 14:34, "let your women keep silence in the churches."[^nb33s] The Bishops': "thy desire [shalbe] to thy husbande, and he shall haue the rule of thee," the supplied verb in the Bishops' brackets.[^nb33t] The 1611: "thy desire shall be to thy husband, and hee shall rule ouer thee," with the margin "Or, subiect to thy husband."[^nb33u] The Douay, from the Latin, keeps "thou shalt be under thy husband's power."

The record is plain. The Hebrew says the woman's תְּשׁוּקָה is toward her husband and that he will rule her; it does not say she is subject to him, and the word it uses for what she has toward him is the word the Song of Songs uses for love. The Greek read it as turning. The Latin wrote subjection into the clause. The English read desire and then, in the Geneva, supplied subjection in italics as the translators' own words; the 1611 moved the subjection to the margin. The tradition's teaching that the woman was placed under the man by this verse rests on Jerome's *sub potestate*, on the Geneva's italics, and on the King James's margin, and not on the Hebrew of the verse or on any version's rendering of the Hebrew noun. What the Hebrew does say, וְהוּא יִמְשָׁל־בָּךְ, "and he shall rule over thee," is said as a consequence and not as a commandment; it stands in a sentence that has no "because," and the man is not told to rule. The same idiom recurs at 4:7, where sin's תְּשׁוּקָה is toward Cain and Cain is told he shall rule over it; the Geneva supplied "shalbe subiect" there too, and Coverdale's margin offered "Let it be subdued vnto the, & rule thou it."[^nb33v] The translators' treatment of the woman's clause was their treatment of the idiom wherever it occurred.

### C. The Man (3:17–19)

> **Genesis 3:17–19 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **17** וּלְאָדָם אָמַר כִּי־שָׁמַעְתָּ לְקוֹל אִשְׁתֶּךָ וַתֹּאכַל מִן־הָעֵץ אֲשֶׁר צִוִּיתִיךָ לֵאמֹר לֹא תֹאכַל מִמֶּנּוּ אֲרוּרָה הָאֲדָמָה בַּעֲבוּרֶךָ בְּעִצָּבוֹן תֹּאכְלֶנָּה כֹּל יְמֵי חַיֶּיךָ׃
> **18** וְקוֹץ וְדַרְדַּר תַּצְמִיחַ לָךְ וְאָכַלְתָּ אֶת־עֵשֶׂב הַשָּׂדֶה׃
> **19** בְּזֵעַת אַפֶּיךָ תֹּאכַל לֶחֶם עַד שׁוּבְךָ אֶל־הָאֲדָמָה כִּי מִמֶּנָּה לֻקָּחְתָּ כִּי־עָפָר אַתָּה וְאֶל־עָפָר תָּשׁוּב׃

> **Genesis 3:17–19 (1611)**
> **17** And vnto Adam he said, Because thou hast hearkened vnto the voyce of thy wife, and hast eaten of the tree, of which I commanded thee, saying, Thou shalt not eate of it: cursed is the ground for thy sake: in sorrow shalt thou eate of it all the dayes of thy life.
> **18** Thornes also and thistles shall it bring forth to thee: and thou shalt eate the herbe of the field.
> **19** In the sweate of thy face shalt thou eate bread, till thou returne vnto the ground: for out of it wast thou taken, for dust thou art, and vnto dust shalt thou returne.[^nb33w]

After a second open space the man's sentence begins, and it begins with a noun-first clause: וּלְאָדָם אָמַר, "and to the man he said," the object fronted with the vav, a turn against the sentence before.[^nb33x] The Septuagint marks it, τῷ δὲ Ἀδάμ; the Vulgate, *Adæ vero*; the Great Bible and the Bishops' drop the connective, "Unto Adam he sayde"; the Geneva has "Also to Adám"; Coverdale and the 1611 "And vnto Adam."[^nb33y] Then the ground: כִּי־שָׁמַעְתָּ לְקוֹל אִשְׁתֶּךָ, "because thou hast hearkened unto the voice of thy wife." The man's fault is named as the act of listening, and the commandment is quoted a third time, in the singular a third time, אֲשֶׁר צִוִּיתִיךָ, "which I commanded *thee*." The versions all have the "because": Ὅτι ἤκουσας, *Quia audisti*, "Because thou hast herkened / hearkened / obeied," and Coverdale's "For so moch as thou hast herkened," which is "because" in another dress.[^nb33z]

What is cursed is not the man. אֲרוּרָה הָאֲדָמָה, "cursed is the ground": the same participle that fell on the serpent, אָרוּר אַתָּה, falls here on the אֲדָמָה from which the אָדָם was taken, and it falls בַּעֲבוּרֶךָ, "for thy sake," "on thy account." The serpent was cursed in his person; the man's ground is cursed because of him; the woman was not cursed at all, and neither was anything on her account. The versions kept the distinction of object, though two of them altered the reason: the Septuagint has ἐπικατάρατος ἡ γῆ ἐν τοῖς ἔργοις σου, "cursed is the earth in thy works," and the Vulgate *maledicta terra in opere tuo*, so that the Greek and Latin reader is told the ground is cursed in what the man does to it rather than on his account; every English Bible has "for thy sake."[^nb33aa]

The rest of the sentence is labour and death. The man will eat the ground's produce בְּעִצָּבוֹן, in the woman's word for pain; it will bring forth thorns and thistles; he will eat bread in the sweat of his face; and then the sentence closes the thread that 2:7 opened. עַד שׁוּבְךָ אֶל־הָאֲדָמָה כִּי מִמֶּנָּה לֻקָּחְתָּ, "until thou return unto the ground, for out of it wast thou taken," and כִּי־עָפָר אַתָּה וְאֶל־עָפָר תָּשׁוּב, "for dust thou art, and unto dust shalt thou return." The ground and the dust are named together as they were at 2:7, עָפָר מִן־הָאֲדָמָה, and the man is sent back to both. This is the third occurrence of עָפָר, and the versions did with it what they did with the first two: the Septuagint γῆ εἶ καὶ εἰς γῆν ἀπελεύσῃ, earth to earth, the same word as the ground; the Vulgate *puluis es, & in puluerem reuerteris*, a third Latin word; Coverdale "earth thou art, and vnto earth shalt thou be turned agayne"; and from the Great Bible on, "dust": "thou art dust, & into dust shalt thou be turned agayne," "thou art dust, and to dust shalt thou returne," "dust thou art, and into dust shalt thou be turned agayne," "dust thou art, and vnto dust shalt thou returne."[^nb33ab] In the Hebrew and in the English since 1540 the man is made of dust, the serpent eats dust, and the man returns to dust, in one word. In the Greek the three are earth; in the Latin they are mud, earth and dust; in Coverdale mould, earth and earth.

### D. Where the Blame Falls

The three sentences differ in three ways, and the ways agree. The serpent's and the man's have a "because"; the woman's has none. The serpent's and the man's are curses, on the serpent himself and on the man's ground; the woman's curses nothing. The serpent's follows the examination by a verb of sequence and the man's by a turn; the woman's stands apart, after a gap, without a connective, as a sentence that begins again.

The reading this book draws from these differences is the reading Part 2 prepared. The serpent knew what the fruit would do and said so; he is sentenced because he did this. The man had received the commandment from God, alone, in the singular, and had heard the serpent's account of the fruit standing beside the woman; he is sentenced because he hearkened. The woman had received no commandment, was not present when it was given, quoted it in a form that was not its form, and was deceived; she is told what her life will be and is not told that it is because of anything. The Hebrew assigns the ground of the sentences to the two who had the knowledge, the serpent by his subtlety and the man by the commandment, and withholds it from the one who had neither. The versions, which disagreed about so much else in these three chapters, agree about this without exception: no version gives the woman a "because," and no version takes one from the serpent or the man.

The subordination the tradition has read into the story is real, but it is not where the tradition has placed it. It is not at 2:18, where every version has a help like him or fit for him and none has a subordinate. It is not at 2:23–24, where the man names his wife his own flesh and speaks a law of cleaving. It is at 3:16, in the third clause of the woman's sentence, and it entered the text of the Bible as *sub viri potestate eris* in the fourth century, as "shal be subiect" in italics in 1560, and as "Or, subiect to thy husband" in the margin of 1611. The Hebrew says the man will rule; it does not say the woman was made to be ruled, and it says the man will rule in a sentence that gives no reason, to a woman who was given no commandment, after a man who was given one had broken it with her beside him. That is what the text says. The versions that read the Hebrew say it too.

## 3.4 Like One of Us (3:20–24)

> **Genesis 3:20–24 (Leningrad Codex)**
> **20** וַיִּקְרָא הָאָדָם שֵׁם אִשְׁתּוֹ חַוָּה כִּי הִוא הָיְתָה אֵם כָּל־חָי׃
> **21** וַיַּעַשׂ יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים לְאָדָם וּלְאִשְׁתּוֹ כָּתְנוֹת עוֹר וַיַּלְבִּשֵׁם׃
> **22** וַיֹּאמֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים הֵן הָאָדָם הָיָה כְּאַחַד מִמֶּנּוּ לָדַעַת טוֹב וָרָע וְעַתָּה פֶּן־יִשְׁלַח יָדוֹ וְלָקַח גַּם מֵעֵץ הַחַיִּים וְאָכַל וָחַי לְעֹלָם׃
> **23** וַיְשַׁלְּחֵהוּ יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים מִגַּן־עֵדֶן לַעֲבֹד אֶת־הָאֲדָמָה אֲשֶׁר לֻקַּח מִשָּׁם׃
> **24** וַיְגָרֶשׁ אֶת־הָאָדָם וַיַּשְׁכֵּן מִקֶּדֶם לְגַן־עֵדֶן אֶת־הַכְּרֻבִים וְאֵת לַהַט הַחֶרֶב הַמִּתְהַפֶּכֶת לִשְׁמֹר אֶת־דֶּרֶךְ עֵץ הַחַיִּים׃

> **Genesis 3:20–24 (1611)**
> **20** And Adam called his wiues name Eue, because she was the mother of all liuing.
> **21** Unto Adam also, and to his wife, did the LORD God make coates of skinnes, and cloathed them.
> **22** And the LORD God said, Behold, the man is become as one of vs, to know good & euill. And now lest hee put foorth his hand, and take also of the tree of life, and eate and liue for euer:
> **23** Therefore the LORD God sent him foorth from the garden of Eden, to till the ground, from whence he was taken.
> **24** So he droue out the man: and he placed at the East of the garden of Eden, Cherubims, and a flaming sword, which turned euery way, to keepe the way of the tree of life.[^nb34a]

### A. The Name (3:20)

The sentences passed, the man speaks for the last time in these chapters, and what he does is what he did at 2:19 and 2:23: he names. וַיִּקְרָא הָאָדָם שֵׁם אִשְׁתּוֹ חַוָּה, "and the man called his wife's name Chavah," כִּי הִוא הָיְתָה אֵם כָּל־חָי, "because she was the mother of all living." The name is made from the verb to live, חָיָה, and the sentence that gives it says so; a Hebrew reader hears in חַוָּה the חָי that closes the verse. It is the third act of naming in the second telling and the first that is a proper name. The animals were named by their kinds; the woman was named אִשָּׁה, a common noun that is the feminine of the man's; now she is given a name that is hers, and the ground of it is not what she was taken from but what will come from her. The man who at 3:12 called her "the woman whom thou gavest" here calls her the mother of every living thing, on the day he has been told he will die.

The versions treated the name as they treated the pun of 2:23, and the results are instructive in the same way. The Septuagint translated it: Καὶ ἐκάλεσεν Ἀδὰμ τὸ ὄνομα τῆς γυναικὸς αὐτοῦ Ζωή, "and Adam called his wife's name Life," ὅτι αὕτη μήτηρ πάντων τῶν ζώντων, "because she is the mother of all the living."[^nb34b] The Greek reader hears in Ζωή the ζώντων that follows, as the Hebrew reader hears חָי in חַוָּה; the translator chose to carry the meaning of the name rather than its sound, and at 4:1, where the name recurs without its explanation, the same translator wrote Εὕαν, the sound.[^nb34c] The Vulgate has *Heua*, and after it Coverdale, the Great Bible and the Bishops' "Heua," the Geneva "Heuáh," and the 1611 "Eue," which supplies in its margin what its text cannot: "Heb. Chauah."[^nb34d] The 1611's margin does for the reader of English what the Septuagint did for the reader of Greek, in the only way English could do it.

The Geneva printed the verse in parentheses, "(And the man called his wiues name Heuáh, because she was the mother of all liuing)," as it had printed 2:9, treating the naming as an aside between the sentences and the clothing; the other English Bibles run it in sequence, "And Adam called."[^nb34e] The Hebrew is a verb of sequence, וַיִּקְרָא, and the naming is the next thing that happened.

### B. The Coats (3:21)

The next verb is God's, and it is an act of care. וַיַּעַשׂ יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים לְאָדָם וּלְאִשְׁתּוֹ כָּתְנוֹת עוֹר וַיַּלְבִּשֵׁם: "and the LORD God made for the man and for his wife coats of skin, and clothed them." The girdles of fig leaves they had made for themselves at 3:7 are replaced by garments God makes, and the verb of making, עָשָׂה, is the verb of the first telling's works. The two who knew they were naked are covered by the one who asked them who had told them so.

The Hebrew opens the verse with its verb, as it opens every act in these chapters. The English Bibles from 1540 did not. The Great Bible has "Unto the same Adam also & to hys wyfe dyd the Lorde God make lethren garmentes," the Geneva "Vnto Adám also and to his wife did the Lord God make coates of skinnes," the Bishops' "Unto Adam also and to his wyfe dyd the Lorde God make garmentes of skynnes," and the 1611 "Unto Adam also, and to his wife, did the LORD God make coates of skinnes."[^nb34f] The object is fronted and the verb inverted, with an "also" the Hebrew does not have; it is the English of 1540 laying an emphasis on the recipients, and the King James kept it. Coverdale, alone among the English, has the Hebrew's order: "And the LORDE God made Adam & his wyfe garmentes of skynnes."[^nb34g] The Septuagint has καὶ ἐποίησεν, the Vulgate *Fecit quoque*, both in sequence.[^nb34h] The garments are χιτῶνας δερματίνους in Greek, *tunicas pelliceas* in Latin, "garmentes of skynnes" in Coverdale and the Bishops', "lethren garmentes" in the Great Bible, "coates of skinnes" in the Geneva and the 1611.[^nb34i]

After this verse the codex leaves an open space.[^nb34j] It is the fourth such gap in the two tellings, after those before 2:4, 3:16 and 3:17, and it separates the clothing from the declaration that follows.

### C. The Declaration (3:22)

> **22** וַיֹּאמֶר יְהוָה אֱלֹהִים הֵן הָאָדָם הָיָה כְּאַחַד מִמֶּנּוּ לָדַעַת טוֹב וָרָע

After the gap, a verb of sequence and a word of attention: וַיֹּאמֶר … הֵן, "and the LORD God said, Behold." What God says is the sentence the two tellings have been moving toward, and it is worth setting down word by word. הָאָדָם, the man; הָיָה, has become; כְּאַחַד מִמֶּנּוּ, as one of us; לָדַעַת טוֹב וָרָע, to know good and evil.

Three earlier sentences are in it. The first is 1:26, נַעֲשֶׂה אָדָם בְּצַלְמֵנוּ כִּדְמוּתֵנוּ, "let us make man in our image, after our likeness": the plural of that verse, "us," "our," returns here in מִמֶּנּוּ, "of us," and the man who was to be made in the likeness of "us" is declared to have become "as one of us." The second is 2:17, where the tree was named עֵץ הַדַּעַת טוֹב וָרָע, "the tree of the knowledge of good and evil," and the man was told not to eat of it. The third is 3:5, the serpent's promise, וִהְיִיתֶם כֵּאלֹהִים יֹדְעֵי טוֹב וָרָע, "ye shall be as *elohim*, knowing good and evil." God's sentence at 3:22 takes the serpent's words and makes them his own, with one change: where the serpent said כֵּאלֹהִים, "as God" or "as gods," God says כְּאַחַד מִמֶּנּוּ, "as one of us," and the ambiguity of the serpent's word is resolved in the direction of the first telling's plural. What the serpent promised, God confirms; and what God confirms is what God intended at 1:26.

This is the sentence on which the reading of this book rests, and the book states the reading here once, plainly. The first telling said that on the sixth day God made man in his image and likeness, male and female, and that on the seventh he rested. The second telling has told how: the man formed, the woman built, the two brought to the knowledge of good and evil, and God's word that the man has now become like the ones who made him. The image announced at 1:26 is completed at 3:22, and it is completed by the knowledge. The eating of the fruit is not an event after the creation of humanity; it is the event by which the creation of humanity, as the first telling describes it, was finished. The first telling's "very good" at 1:31 and the second telling's "behold, the man has become as one of us" at 3:22 are the same judgement on the same day, and the rest of the seventh day follows both.

The versions kept the sentence, and nearly all of them kept its perfect tense. The Septuagint has Ἰδοὺ Ἀδὰμ γέγονεν ὡς εἷς ἐξ ἡμῶν, τοῦ γινώσκειν καλὸν καὶ πονηρόν, "behold, Adam has become as one of us, to know good and evil"; the Vulgate *Ecce Adam quasi vnus ex nobis factus est, sciens bonum & malum*; Coverdale "lo, Adam is become as it were one of vs, & knoweth good & euell"; the Geneva "Beholde, the man is become as one of vs, to knowe good and euil"; the Bishops' "Beholde, the man is become as one of vs, in knowyng good and euyll"; the 1611 "Behold, the man is become as one of vs, to know good & euill."[^nb34k] The Great Bible alone reads otherwise, and reads strangely: "Beholde, yonder man hath bene euen as one of vs, that he myght knowe good and euell," a past tense and a purpose clause that make the likeness a state the man once had rather than one he has reached, and turn the knowledge into the reason for the likeness rather than its content.[^nb34l] No other witness has it, the Bishops' revisers corrected it, and the book records it as the one version that did not hear the sentence as a declaration of what the man had become.

The second half of the verse is a sentence that does not finish. וְעַתָּה פֶּן־יִשְׁלַח יָדוֹ וְלָקַח גַּם מֵעֵץ הַחַיִּים וְאָכַל וָחַי לְעֹלָם: "and now, lest he put forth his hand and take also of the tree of life, and eat, and live for ever" — and there the speech ends, with the consequence the "lest" was to guard against unstated. The reader supplies it from the next verse. The versions kept the broken sentence: the Septuagint καὶ νῦν μή ποτε ἐκτείνῃ τὴν χεῖρα, the Vulgate *nunc ergo ne forte mittat manum suam*, the English "And now lest he put forth his hand."[^nb34m] The Bishops' Bible alone told the reader what was missing, in a note that shows its translators reading the Hebrew as a Hebrew sentence: "You may supplie the sentence with these wordes: let vs cast hym foorth."[^nb34n] Coverdale gave the clause its own connective, "But now lest he stretch his hande," which is the sense.[^nb34o] The tree of life is named for the second time, and the man is not said to have eaten of it; the first telling's "good" and "very good" contained no eternal life for the creatures, and the second telling's man had access to it and did not take it. The "lest" of 3:22 keeps him from taking it now.

### D. The Sending (3:23–24)

Two verbs of sequence close the narrative. וַיְשַׁלְּחֵהוּ, "and he sent him forth," and וַיְגָרֶשׁ, "and he drove out." Both objects are singular and both are the man: the LORD God sent *him* forth from the garden, and drove out *the man*. The woman is not named in either verse. She goes, and the text does not say so; what it says is that the man was sent to do what 3:19 had sentenced him to do, לַעֲבֹד אֶת־הָאֲדָמָה אֲשֶׁר לֻקַּח מִשָּׁם, "to till the ground from which he was taken." The ground of 2:7 receives him back as a labourer before it receives him back as dust. The Great Bible alone has the plural, "the Lorde God sent them furth," and no other witness follows it; the Septuagint, the Vulgate and every other English Bible have the singular the Hebrew has.[^nb34p]

The versions' connectives at the close are the last instance in these chapters of the pattern this book has traced. The Hebrew has two sequence verbs; the Septuagint καί and καί; the Vulgate *Et emisit* and *Eiecitque*; Coverdale "Then the LORDE God put him out" and "And he cast Adam out." From the Geneva on, the English reads the two verses as consequence and result: the Geneva "Therefore ȳ Lord God sent him forthe" and "Thus he cast out man"; the Bishops' "Therfore" and "And so he droue out man"; the 1611 "Therefore the LORD God sent him foorth" and "So he droue out the man."[^nb34q] The King James translators, who printed "And" at the scene-clauses of 1:2 and 4:1 where the Hebrew turns, here printed "Therefore" and "So" where the Hebrew merely continues, and they took both from the Geneva. The close of the third chapter in the English Bible is not the flat "And … and" its first chapter is; it is a chain of consequence the translators supplied, and the reader who has been told the King James levels every connective should be told this too.

The last verse sets the guard. הַכְּרֻבִים, the cherubim, and לַהַט הַחֶרֶב הַמִּתְהַפֶּכֶת, "the flame of the sword that turns itself," are placed east of the garden לִשְׁמֹר אֶת־דֶּרֶךְ עֵץ הַחַיִּים, "to guard the way of the tree of life." The way is guarded; it is not destroyed. The Septuagint added a clause the Hebrew lacks, καὶ κατῴκισεν αὐτὸν ἀπέναντι τοῦ παραδείσου τῆς τρυφῆς, "and he made him dwell over against the paradise of delight," so that in Greek the man is settled within sight of the garden; the Vulgate has *collocauit ante paradisum voluptatis Cherubim*, placing the cherubim before the garden and saying nothing of where the man dwelt; the English follow the Hebrew.[^nb34r] The sword is *flammeum gladium, atque versatilem* in the Latin, "a naked fyrie swerde" in Coverdale, "a fyery two-edged sworde" in the Great and the Bishops', "the blade of a sworde shaken" in the Geneva, "a flaming sword, which turned euery way" in the 1611, whose rendering of הַמִּתְהַפֶּכֶת is the closest.[^nb34s] With that the second telling ends, and the next verse of Genesis, וְהָאָדָם יָדַע, "now the man knew his wife," opens after a gap in the codex with a scene-clause of its own, on the other side of the garden.[^nb34t]

### E. The Two Tellings Closed

The first telling ended with God resting on the seventh day from all the work he had made. The second telling ends with God declaring that the man has become as one of us, and sending him out to the ground. The reading this book has carried through nine chapters is that these are one ending. The image announced on the sixth day is the likeness God declares at 3:22; the knowledge that the serpent promised, that the woman took, that the man took beside her, and that God confirmed, is the content of that likeness; and the sending out is not the undoing of the creation but the beginning of the life the creation was for, on the ground the man was made from, with the way to the tree of life guarded and not closed. Every step of the reading has been taken from the shape of the Hebrew clauses, and at every step the book has set down what the versions did. Part 4 now reads the versions as wholes, to see what each of them made of the narrative they were carrying.

# Part 4 — What the Versions Did with the Narrative

## 4.1 The Greek: The Turns Marked, the Order Kept, the Words Collapsed

The Septuagint is the oldest translation of Genesis and the only one this book reads that was made while Hebrew was still spoken as a mother tongue. Parts 1 to 3 have cited it verse by verse. This chapter reads it as a whole, for what its translators did with the narrative they were carrying, and it finds three things: that they marked the shape of the Hebrew clause more faithfully than any English version before the twentieth century; that they kept the order of the second telling and twice added a word to insist on it; and that they collapsed the Hebrew's vocabulary at the points where this book's reading depends on a word being one word.

A word first on what is being read. The Septuagint of Genesis 1–3 is quoted in this book from Swete's edition of 1901, which prints the text of Codex Alexandrinus, a manuscript of the fifth century, because Codex Vaticanus, Swete's usual base, has lost its first forty-six chapters of Genesis; Swete's apparatus records the readings of two other early witnesses, and the book has noted them where they differ.[^nb41a] What the book calls "the Septuagint" is therefore an edition of one fifth-century manuscript of a translation made in the third century before Christ, and its claims are claims about that text. The author does not read Greek; the readings are the author's from the printed page, checked against the Hebrew and the later versions, and await professional review.

### A. The Turns Marked

The Method stated the rule and Part 2 showed it working: where the Hebrew puts a noun after the vav and so opens a scene, the Septuagint puts δέ, the Greek particle of a new scene, and where the Hebrew puts a verb, καί. At six of the seven noun-first verse-openers between 1:1 and 4:1 the Greek has δέ: ἡ δὲ γῆ (1:2), πηγὴ δέ (2:6), ποταμὸς δέ (2:10), ὁ δὲ ὄφις (3:1), τῷ δὲ Ἀδάμ (3:17), Ἀδὰμ δέ (4:1); at the mid-verse contrast of 2:20 it has τε; and at the in-verse pairs of 1:5 and 1:10, where the noun is fronted to match a second naming to a first, it keeps καί, so that δέ is reserved for the clause that begins something.[^nb41b] The one noun-first verse-opener it did not mark is 2:5, καὶ πᾶν χλωρὸν ἀγροῦ, and the book has recorded that every other version failed there too.[^nb41c]

The discipline is the more striking because Greek did not need it. Greek narrative can run on καί as Hebrew runs on vav, and the Septuagint's Genesis does so at every sequence verb; the translators had a resource the Hebrew lacked, a particle that says "here the story turns," and they spent it exactly where the Hebrew turns. The modern English versions mark these turns too, and chapter 4.4 will say so; the point here is that the Greek of the third century before Christ marked them first, and that what the moderns returned to was the Greek's practice.

### B. The Order Kept, and Underlined

At the two verses where the order of the second telling differs from the order of the first, the Septuagint added a word. καὶ ἐξανέτειλεν ὁ θεὸς ἔτι ἐκ τῆς γῆς πᾶν ξύλον (2:9), "and God made to spring up *further* every tree"; καὶ ἔπλασεν ὁ θεὸς ἔτι ἐκ τῆς γῆς πάντα τὰ θηρία (2:19), "and God formed *further* all the beasts."[^nb41d] The Hebrew has וַיַּצְמַח and וַיִּצֶר, sequence verbs, and nothing more. The Greek's ἔτι is an interpretation, and it is the interpretation this book carries: that the trees grew and the beasts were formed after the man was formed, as further acts. The translators saw that a reader might take the second telling's trees and beasts for the first telling's, already made on the third and sixth days, and they wrote a word to prevent it. It is the oldest witness to the reading, and the only ancient one.

Elsewhere the Greek's additions run the other way, toward harmony. At 2:2 it has God finish his works on the sixth day, ἐν τῇ ἡμέρᾳ τῇ ἕκτῃ, where the Hebrew has the seventh; the finishing is moved off the day of rest.[^nb41e] At 2:17 the commandment given to one man turns plural in the Greek, οὐ φάγεσθε … φάγησθε … ἀποθανεῖσθε, so that the serpent's plural at 3:1 has something to answer.[^nb41f] At 2:18 God's "I will make" becomes ποιήσωμεν, "let us make," the plural of 1:26 carried forward.[^nb41g] At 3:16 the Greek supplies a καί where the Hebrew begins the woman's sentence with nothing.[^nb41h] At 3:24 it adds a clause, καὶ κατῴκισεν αὐτὸν ἀπέναντι τοῦ παραδείσου, settling the man within sight of the garden.[^nb41i] None of these is large, and none changes the order of events; together they show a translator who smoothed the surface of the second telling toward the first while leaving its sequence, and marking its sequence, intact. The Greek is not a harmoniser of the order. It is a harmoniser of the details around the order.

### C. The Words Collapsed

The Septuagint's losses are losses of vocabulary, and they fall where this book's reading needs a single Hebrew word to be visible as single. Three matter.

The first is the ground. The Hebrew has three words, אֶרֶץ for the earth, אֲדָמָה for the ground, עָפָר for the dust, and the second telling's thread runs on the third: the man is dust from the ground (2:7), the serpent eats dust (3:14), the man returns to dust (3:19). The Greek has γῆ for the first two everywhere and for the third at 3:14 and 3:19, with χοῦς only at 2:7; by 3:19 the three Hebrew words are one Greek word, and the dust the serpent eats is not the dust the man is made of but "earth."[^nb41j] The Latin dissolved the same thread into three words; the Greek collapsed it into one; the English from 1540 kept it.

The second is the image. At 1:27 the Hebrew has the word twice, בְּצַלְמוֹ בְּצֶלֶם אֱלֹהִים, and the Greek once, κατ᾿ εἰκόνα θεοῦ ἐποίησεν αὐτόν.[^nb41k] The emphasis of the Hebrew, which repeats the image as if to make sure, is not in the Greek, and the Vulgate restored it from the Hebrew.

The third is the pun. Twice the Hebrew makes a word do two things, אִישׁ and אִשָּׁה at 2:23, עֲרוּמִּים and עָרוּם at 2:25 and 3:1, and the Greek, which has no such pairs, gives Γυνή and ἀνήρ, γυμνοί and φρονιμώτατος, and the play is gone.[^nb41l] Jerome recovered the first with *virago*; no one recovered the second. The Greek also made a choice at the second that the book has noted: its serpent is φρονιμώτατος, "most prudent," a word of praise, and the ambivalence of the Hebrew עָרוּם is resolved toward wisdom.[^nb41m]

Against these the Greek has one recovery of its own kind, at 3:20. Where every later version transliterated the woman's name, the Greek translated it, Ζωή, "Life," so that the etymology the verse gives, "because she was the mother of all living," is an etymology in Greek as it is in Hebrew.[^nb41n] The translators who could not carry אִישׁ and אִשָּׁה carried חַוָּה and חָי, by giving up the sound for the sense.

### D. The Readings That Are the Greek's Own

Several renderings in the Septuagint are neither marks of the clause nor collapses of the vocabulary but readings of the story, and the book lists them because Part 2 and Part 3 have used them. τὸ δειλινόν at 3:8, "in the afternoon," for the Hebrew's "wind of the day," turns the רוּחַ into a time.[^nb41o] ἡ ἀποστροφή σου at 3:16, "thy turning," gives תְּשׁוּקָה the sense of recourse rather than desire or subjection.[^nb41p] τηρήσει and τηρήσεις at 3:15, "shall watch for," give the seed and the serpent a single verb, as the Hebrew does, but a verb of lying in wait rather than of bruising.[^nb41q] τοῦ εἰδέναι γνωστὸν καλοῦ καὶ πονηροῦ at 2:9, "of knowing what is knowable of good and evil," strains after more than "knowledge" in naming the tree.[^nb41r] ἐν τοῖς ἔργοις σου at 3:17, "in thy works," for the Hebrew's "for thy sake," makes the ground's curse a curse in the man's labour rather than on his account.[^nb41s] Τί ὅτι εἶπεν ὁ θεός at 3:1 makes the serpent's half-question a question about God's reason.[^nb41t] And ὡς θεοί at 3:5, "as gods," decides the number of the serpent's כֵּאלֹהִים toward the plural, a decision the Latin and the English of 1540 to 1611 followed and the English since has reversed.[^nb41u]

### E. What the Greek Is to the Reading

The Septuagint is this book's oldest ally on the one point where the book most needs one, the order of the second telling, and its first opponent on the point where the book's reading of the sentences depends on a word, the dust. It marked the Hebrew's turns better than any English Bible before the twentieth century, and it smoothed the Hebrew's seams, at 2:2 and 2:17 and 3:16, in ways no English Bible did. It is a translation made by men who understood the Hebrew clause and did not always trust the Hebrew text; the book takes from it what it understood and records what it did not trust.

## 4.2 The Latin: The Turns Kept, the Order Harmonised, the Pun Carried

The Vulgate is the second of the two ancient versions and the one the West read longest. Jerome made his Latin Genesis from the Hebrew at the close of the fourth century, with the Greek open beside him, and his text was the Bible of the Latin Church for eleven hundred years before any of the English Bibles in this book was printed.[^nb42a] The text read here is the edition of 1592, printed at Rome by the Vatican press as the Church's text, in which Genesis 1–3 stands on three printed pages; the readings are those pages' readings, and the author, who does not read Latin, has taken them from the printed line, checked them against the Hebrew and the Greek, and holds them for professional review.[^nb42b]

Parts 1 to 3 cited the Latin at every verse. Read as a whole it shows three things. It kept the Hebrew's turns as the Greek had, and dressed the Hebrew's sequence in a Latin variety the Hebrew does not have. It moved the order of the second telling at two verses, the first version to do so, while keeping the details the Greek had smoothed. And it carried into Latin the one pun the Greek could not carry, and wrote into two verses of the sentences two readings that no earlier text contains and that the tradition built on more than on anything in the Hebrew.

### A. The Turns Kept, and the Sequence Varied

At the noun-first clauses Jerome read the Hebrew as the Greek had read it. *Terra autem erat inanis* (1:2); *Sed fons ascendebat* (2:6); *Sed & serpens erat callidior* (3:1); *Adæ vero dixit* (3:17); *Adam vero cognouit* (4:1): five of the seven verse-openers carry a Latin particle of turning, *autem*, *sed* or *vero*, and the mid-verse contrast of 2:20 has *Adæ vero* as well.[^nb42c] Where the contrast lies in the sense he marked it too, *De ligno autem scientiæ* at 2:17, and in the verbless clauses of the rivers he set *vero* and *autem* at the third and fourth.[^nb42d] The two openers he left unmarked are 2:5, *Et omne virgultum agri*, which no version marked, and 2:10, *Et fluuius egrediebatur*, which the Greek had.[^nb42e]

At the sequence clauses he did what the Hebrew does not do. Where the Hebrew has one vav before one verb, the Latin has an enclitic *-que* (*Dixitque*, *Fecitque*, *Creauitque*, *Benedixitque*, *Produxitque*, *Eiecitque*), or *igitur*, "therefore" (*Igitur perfecti sunt*, 2:1; *Formauit igitur*, 2:7; *Formatis igitur*, 2:19; *Vidit igitur mulier*, 3:6), or *ergo* (*Immisit ergo*, 2:21; *nunc ergo*, 3:22), or *quoque* and *etiam*, "also" (*Dixit quoque*, *Dixit etiam*, *Mulieri quoque dixit*, 3:16; *Fecit quoque*, 3:21), so that the speeches of God in the first chapter scarcely open twice alike and no two consecutive Latin verses begin alike.[^nb42f] This is Latin style laid over a Hebrew that has none, and the book has said so in Part 1. It is worth separating from the marking of the turns, because the two look alike on the page and are not alike in kind. At the noun-first clauses the Latin answers the Hebrew's syntax. At the sequence clauses it answers its own ear, and where the word chosen is *igitur* it imports a reasoning the Hebrew leaves unstated: the man is formed "therefore," because the earth had no tiller (2:7); the woman sees "therefore," because the serpent has spoken (3:6). The English Bibles of 1540 and 1560 heard these and answered them, "The Lorde God also shope man," "And so the woman," "So the woman," and the reader of English who meets an "also" or a "so" at these verses is reading the Latin's connective, not the Hebrew's.[^nb42g]

The Latin reader, then, was given the Hebrew's turns and something more, and nothing was levelled. Whatever the English reader has been given at the connective since 1611, it was not the practice of either ancient version.

### B. The Order Harmonised

The Vulgate is the first text in any language to move an event of the second telling out of its place. At 2:8 it reads *Plantauerat autem Dominus Deus Paradisum voluptatis a principio: in quo posuit hominem quem formauerat*, "but the Lord God had planted a paradise of pleasure from the beginning, in which he set the man whom he had formed."[^nb42h] The Hebrew has וַיִּטַּע, a sequence verb, "and he planted," and מִקֶּדֶם, "eastward," a direction. The Latin has a pluperfect, "had planted," and *a principio*, "from the beginning," a time. In one clause the garden is moved before the man and back to the start of creation, and the man of 2:7 is set down in a place that was waiting for him. The verse that follows keeps the sequence, *Produxitque Dominus Deus de humo omne lignum*, "and the Lord God brought forth from the soil every tree," but it brings the trees forth in a garden that, in Latin, already existed; there is no *etiam* here to answer the Greek's ἔτι, and the Greek's insistence that the trees came after the man has no Latin echo.[^nb42i]

At 2:19 the same thing is done by a different construction. *Formatis igitur, Dominus Deus, de humo cunctis animantibus terræ, & vniuersis volatilibus cæli, adduxit ea ad Adam*: "the Lord God, therefore, all the living things of the earth and all the birds of heaven having been formed of the soil, brought them to Adam."[^nb42j] The ablative absolute makes the forming a finished background to the bringing. Jerome does not say when the animals were formed; he removes their forming from the chain of the second telling and leaves the reader free to set it on the sixth day of the first. The two harmonisations are of one kind, and they are the two verses at which every modern English version that reads "had planted" and "had formed" departs from the Hebrew's order. The reading is Jerome's before it is anyone's.

What is striking is what the Latin did not do. The Greek had moved the finishing of the work to the sixth day at 2:2; the Latin has the Hebrew's day, *Compleuitque Deus die septimo opus suum*. The Greek had put the commandment of 2:17 in the plural; the Latin has the singular, *ne comedas … morte morieris*. The Greek had added a clause at 3:24 settling the man opposite the garden; the Latin has none.[^nb42k] Where the Latin did smooth a detail, it smoothed it as the Greek had: *faciamus* for "I will make" at 2:18; *eritis sicut dij* at 3:5; *maledicta terra in opere tuo* at 3:17; and at 3:6 it dropped the word that places the man at the tree, *deditque viro suo, qui comedit*, "and she gave to her husband, who ate," so that the Latin reader, unlike the Greek, may suppose the man elsewhere.[^nb42l] The pattern is the reverse of the Greek's. The Greek harmonised the details and kept the order; the Latin kept the details and harmonised the order. Of the two, the Latin's is the harmonisation that lasted, because the West read it for eleven centuries and the first English Bible was made in its shadow: Coverdale's pluperfect at 2:19 and his "and he ate" at 3:6 are the Latin's readings in English, and the Great Bible of 1540 is the version that took them out.[^nb42m]

### C. The Pun Carried, and the Words

At 2:23 the Latin did what no other version did. *Hæc vocabitur Virago, quoniam de viro sumpta est*: for the woman's name Jerome chose *virago*, a Latin word built on *vir*, so that the Latin reader hears in the name the word it is taken from, as the Hebrew reader hears אִישׁ in אִשָּׁה.[^nb42n] The Greek had given up the sound for the sense, Γυνή and ἀνήρ; the English would half-carry it by the accident that "woman" contains "man." Only the Latin carried it on purpose. It is the mark of a translator who heard the Hebrew as sound, and it should be set beside the pluperfect of 2:8 as the other face of the same man: the reader who heard the pun did not trust the order.

The Latin's other choices at the words divide between recoveries and losses. It restored the doubled image of 1:27 that the Greek had halved, *ad imaginem suam: ad imaginem Dei creauit illum*, and the English from 1540 has the doubling from the Latin.[^nb42o] It kept "living soul" as one phrase for the creatures and the man, *anima viuens* at all five places, where the English of 1540 would divide it.[^nb42p] Its serpent is *callidior*, "cleverer," a word that can be praise or blame, and it leaves the serpent's quality open where the Greek had decided it toward wisdom.[^nb42q] Its mandate is milder than the Greek's, *præsit* at 1:26 and *subiicite eam, & dominamini* at 1:28, against the Greek's κατακυριεύσατε.[^nb42r] At 3:8 it kept the wind that the Greek had turned into an hour, and added the hour beside it, *ad auram post meridiem*, "at the breeze after midday."[^nb42s]

Against these, the Latin dissolved the thread the Greek had collapsed. The Hebrew's עָפָר is one word at 2:7, 3:14 and 3:19; the Latin has *de limo terræ*, "of the mud of the earth," then *terram comedes*, "earth shalt thou eat," then *puluis es, & in puluerem reuerteris*, "dust thou art, and to dust thou shalt return," three words for one, so that the Latin reader cannot see that the serpent is sentenced to eat what the man is made of, or that the man's end is his beginning.[^nb42t] And at 3:20 it transliterated the woman's name, *Heua*, where the Greek had translated it; the Latin reader is given the sound and told, in the same verse, a meaning he cannot hear in it.[^nb42u] Every English Bible transliterated as the Latin had, and the 1611 supplied in its margin, "Heb. Chauah," the only correction English could make.

### D. The Two Readings That Are the Latin's Alone

Two verses of the sentences read in Latin as they read in no earlier text, and the tradition's teaching on both descends from the Latin.

The first is 3:15. *Ipsa conteret caput tuum, & tu insidiaberis calcaneo eius*: "*she* shall crush thy head, and thou shalt lie in wait for her heel."[^nb42v] The Hebrew pronoun is הוּא, masculine, agreeing with the seed; the Greek has αὐτός, masculine. The Latin's *ipsa* is feminine and agrees with the woman, and it has no warrant in either text before it. The Hebrew uses one verb, שׁוּף, for what the seed does to the serpent and what the serpent does to the seed; the Latin uses two, *conteret* and *insidiaberis*, and the two acts are no longer one act. From *ipsa* descends the reading of the verse as a promise about the woman, and from the woman as Mary. No English Bible took it: Coverdale, the Great Bible, the Bishops' and the 1611 have "it," the Geneva "he," and the 1611 alone kept one verb for the two acts, "bruise … bruise."[^nb42w]

The second is 3:16. *In dolore paries filios, & sub viri potestate eris, & ipse dominabitur tui*: "in pain shalt thou bear children, and thou shalt be under thy husband's power, and he shall have dominion over thee."[^nb42x] The Hebrew noun is תְּשׁוּקָה, the word the Song of Songs uses for the lover's longing; the Greek heard ἀποστροφή, a turning toward. The Latin heard power, and with *sub viri potestate eris* the subjection of the woman entered the text of Western Christendom at a place where the Hebrew has a noun whose sense is open and a clause that states a consequence, not a command. The English did not follow the Latin's text, but they felt it: the Geneva supplied "shal be subiect" in the italic of the translators' own words, the 1611 moved it to the margin, "Or, subiect to thy husband," and the Douay, translated from the Latin, kept "under thy husband's power" whole.[^nb42y]

A third reading is smaller and belongs with these. At 3:1 the serpent's opening, which in Hebrew is two particles and a verb that may be read as a half-question, becomes in Latin a plain question about God's reason, *Cur præcepit vobis Deus*, "why has God commanded you"; the Greek's Τί ὅτι had gone the same way, and the English "Yea, hath God said" did not.[^nb42z]

### E. What the Latin Is to the Reading

The Vulgate is the ancient version the book must reckon with most, because it is the one whose readings reached the English. At the connective Jerome is the most careful reader of the Hebrew's shape among the versions, and the book takes his turns, his *virago*, his doubled image, his one word for the soul and his seventh day. At the order he is the first to rearrange it, and the book follows his pluperfect and his ablative absolute to Coverdale and to the modern "had planted." At the sentences he wrote two readings into the text that the Hebrew and the Greek do not contain, and the book follows those too: *ipsa* to the Marian reading, *sub potestate* to the Geneva's italics and the King James's margin and the Douay's text. What the Latin understood, the book uses. What the Latin added, the book names as the Latin's, and reads the Hebrew without it.

## 4.3 The English Before 1611: Range, Recovery, and the Heading of 1568

Between the first complete English Bible and the King James lie seventy-six years and four Bibles, and this book has read Genesis 1–3 in all four: Coverdale's of 1535, made from the Latin and from Luther by a man who did not read Hebrew; the Great Bible of 1540, Coverdale's own revision of his text against the Hebrew, set up in every parish church by royal order; the Geneva Bible of 1560, made by English exiles with the Hebrew before them and printed with notes in the margin; and the Bishops' Bible of 1568, the revision the Elizabethan church made of the Great Bible to replace the Geneva on the lectern, which it never replaced in the home.[^nb43a] The four are read here in the copies on the shelf and cited by their leaves. They are English, and the author reads them; the black letter and the abbreviations have been read from the image, and a doubtful letter has been said to be doubtful.[^nb43b]

The chapter finds three things in the four. They kept a range at the connective that neither the Latin nor the King James kept, marking the Hebrew's turns where the sense forced them and varying the sequence in English as the Latin had in Latin. They recovered, chiefly in 1540, three things the Latin had lost: the order of the second telling at 2:19, the man's presence at the tree, and the one word for dust. And one of them, in 1568, printed at the head of the second chapter a statement of what the chapter is that no Bible before it had printed and that its own later printings, and every Bible after it, let go.

### A. The Range

The first thing an English reader of 1535 or 1560 met at the connective was variety. Coverdale closes each day of the first chapter "Then of the euenynge and mornynge was made the first daye," opens the acts that follow a speech with "Then God made," "Then God deuyded," and sums the work at 2:1 with "Thus was heauen and earth fynished."[^nb43c] The Great Bible closes each day "The euenynge also and the mornyng" and opens the second telling's acts with "also" and "And so."[^nb43d] The Geneva has the widest range in English: "Then God said," "Againe God said," "God said againe," "Afterwarde God said," "Moreouer God said," "Furthermore God said," and "So the euening and the morning were," in the first chapter alone; and in the second and third "Therefore" for the sleep of 2:21, "So" for the woman's seeing at 3:6, "Also" for the man's sentence, "Therefore" and "Thus" for the sending and the driving out.[^nb43e] The Bishops' is the plainest of the four, "And" at every verse of the first chapter to 1:24, and then, having dropped the connective at 1:25 and 1:26, "And" through most of the second and third.[^nb43f] None of this variety is the Hebrew's. In the first telling the Hebrew has the vav of sequence at every verse after the second, and "And" renders it exactly; the Geneva's "Againe" and "Afterwarde" are English laid over a Hebrew that has none, as the Vulgate's *quoque* and *etiam* were Latin, and the book has said in Part 1 that they are not faults.[^nb43g]

The second thing the reader met, if he read closely, was that the same Bibles marked the Hebrew's turns where the sense made the turn audible. At 2:6, where the verse before has said it had not rained, Coverdale, the Geneva and the Bishops' have "But there arose a myst," "But a myst went vp," "But there went vp a mist," and only the Great Bible has "And."[^nb43h] At 2:17, where the forbidden tree is set against all the others, every one of the four has "But."[^nb43i] At 2:20, where the man's search ends without a help, Coverdale has "But vnto man," the Geneva "but for Adám," the Bishops' "but for man," and again only the Great Bible "And."[^nb43j] At 3:1 Coverdale and the Great Bible have "But the serpent," the Geneva "Now the serpent," the first "Now the serpent" in English and the one the King James would keep; the Bishops' alone has "And."[^nb43k] Where the sense did not force the issue the four did what the ancient versions had done at 2:5 and wrote "and": at 1:2 only the Great Bible marked the scene, by dropping the connective altogether, "The earth was voyde and emptye," which is the English way of setting a scene when the language has no particle for it; at 2:5 Coverdale and the Great Bible run the verse into the heading without a break and the Geneva and the Bishops' begin it "And euerie plant"; at 2:10 all four have "And."[^nb43l] At 3:17 the Great Bible and the Bishops' drop the connective, "Unto Adam he sayde," the Geneva has "Also to Adám," Coverdale "And vnto Adam"; at 4:1 Coverdale has "Morouer" and the Geneva "Afterwarde."[^nb43m]

Set beside the ancient versions, the English record at the seven verse-openers is this. The Greek marked six, the Latin five. Of the four English Bibles the Geneva marked four (2:6, 3:1, 3:17, 4:1), Coverdale three (2:6, 3:1, 4:1), the Great Bible two (1:2, 3:1), the Bishops' one (2:6), and each of them marked the mid-verse turn of 2:20 except the Great; the bare "Unto Adam he sayde" of the Great and the Bishops' at 3:17 is counted as neither, since it follows a "But" at 3:16 that the Hebrew does not have.[^nb43n] The English translators of these years were not reading the shape of the clause as the Greek translators had; they were reading the sense of the passage and writing "but" or "now" where the sense turned. But because the sense turns where the clause turns, they marked, between them, every one of the seven but 2:5 and 2:10, and they marked them in a range of English, "But," "Now," "Also," "Morouer," "Afterwarde," that told the reader something was beginning. The book will show in the next chapter what the King James did with that range.

### B. The Recovery

The Latin had lost three things in the second telling, and the English of 1540 recovered all three. The book has recorded each at its verse; here they are set together because they were recovered together, in one revision, and because the man who made the revision had lost them himself five years before.

The first is the order at 2:19. Coverdale, working from the Latin and from Luther, had the pluperfect: "And whan God the LORDE had made of the earth all maner beastes of the felde … he brought them vnto man," which is Jerome's ablative absolute in English, and places the forming of the animals before the second telling begins.[^nb43o] The Great Bible of 1540 reads "And so out of the grounde shope the Lorde God euery beast of the felde, and euery foule of the ayre, and brought it vnto man," a simple past in sequence, and the Geneva kept it, "So the Lord God formed of the earth euerie beast."[^nb43p] The Bishops' of 1568 went back to Coverdale's tense, "the Lorde God had shapen euery beast of the field," and that is the text the King James translators were given to revise; what they did with it is the next chapter's.[^nb43q] From 1540 to 1568 the Bible on the lectern, and from 1560 the Bible in the home, agreed with the Hebrew and the Greek that the animals were formed after the man.

The second is the man at the tree. The Hebrew of 3:6 says the woman gave also to her husband עִמָּהּ, "with her"; the Greek has μετ᾿ αὐτῆς; the Latin dropped it, *deditque viro suo, qui comedit*, and Coverdale, following the Latin, dropped it too, "and gaue vnto hir husbande also therof, and he ate."[^nb43r] The Great Bible put the man back beside the woman, and did so with emphasis: "and gaue vnto her husband beynge with her, which dyd eate also." The Bishops' kept "beyng with her," the Geneva and the 1611 "with her," and the man has stood at the tree in every English Bible since.[^nb43s] The reading of Part 3, that the man was present and heard the serpent and ate with knowledge of the commandment the woman had never been given, rests on a word that the English reader has had since 1540 and the Latin reader never had.

The third is the dust. Coverdale had followed the Latin into three words, "moulde" at 2:7, "earth" at 3:14, "earth" at 3:19; the Great Bible has "euen dust from of the grounde" at 2:7, "dust shalt thou eate" at 3:14, "thou art dust, & into dust shalt thou be turned agayne" at 3:19, one English word for the one Hebrew word, and the Geneva, the Bishops' and the 1611 kept it.[^nb43t] The Great Bible of 1540 is the first version in any language on this shelf, Greek, Latin or English, in which the man is made of dust, the serpent eats dust and the man returns to dust in a single word. The thread that binds the making to the two sentences, on which the reading of chapter 3.3 depends, was made visible to a reader who had no Hebrew for the first time in 1540, in English.

Two smaller recoveries stand with these. The Great Bible restored the doubled image of 1:27, "in his awne ymage: in the ymage of God," which Coverdale had not had at all, and every English Bible after it kept the doubling.[^nb43u] And the Geneva coined at 2:18 the phrase the English language would keep, "an helpe mete for him," with the Hebrew in its margin, "Ebr. before him": a help suitable for him, which is what the Hebrew says, and nothing about rank.[^nb43v]

Against the recoveries the same years made three losses, and the book has recorded them too. The Great Bible divided the one Hebrew phrase נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה into "lyuing creature" for the animals and "a lyuing soule" for the man, where Coverdale had "lyuynge soules" for the beasts of 1:24 and "a lyuynge soule" for the man, and every English Bible since has kept the division and put the Hebrew in the margin.[^nb43w] Every English Bible from Coverdale on has "in the coole of the day" at 3:8, a paraphrase in which the Hebrew's wind is neither wind nor Spirit, and only the Geneva's and the 1611's margins confess it.[^nb43x] And Coverdale's "subdue it, & haue domynion" of 1535, kept by the Great Bible, the Bishops' and the 1611, is the English the reader has heard the mandate in ever since, for a Hebrew whose first verb is the verb of bringing land under cultivation and whose second is the verb of a king's rule.[^nb43y] The English of these years gave the reader the Hebrew's order and its thread, and took from him its one name for what lives, and its wind.

### C. The Geneva: The Reader's Bible and Its Hand on the Text

The Geneva deserves a paragraph of its own, because it did more to the text than any of the four and told the reader more about the Hebrew than any of them. It kept the order at 2:19; it has the Hebrew's shape at 3:16, "Vnto the woman he said," with no connective, as the Hebrew has none; its margins give the Hebrew at 1:20, "Ebr. the soule of life," at 2:18, "Ebr. before him," at 2:23, "for in Ebr. Ish is man, and Ishah the woman," and at 3:8, "Or, winde."[^nb43z] But it also printed 2:9 and 3:20 in parentheses, "(For out of the grounde made the Lord God to growe euerie tre …)" and "(And the man called his wiues name Heuáh …)," turning two narrated acts into asides, a gentler harmonisation than Jerome's pluperfect but a harmonisation still, and the 1611 did not keep either.[^nb43aa] At 3:16 it supplied "shal be subiect" in the italic it used for the translators' own words, and sent the reader in the margin to 1 Corinthians 14:34.[^nb43ab] And its notes read the story throughout in the manner of its church: the plural of 1:26 as God taking counsel with his wisdom, the knowledge of good and evil as "miserable experience," the serpent as Satan's instrument, the seed of 3:15 as Christ, the man's eating as "ambicion," and the hiding as those who "soght not to God for remedie."[^nb43ac] The book has recorded the notes at their verses and taken one of them, on the knowledge as experience, as a witness. The Geneva is the Bible in which the English reader was given most of the Hebrew and most of the translators, on the same page, and its parentheses and its italics are the first places where an English translator's reading of the story entered the printed text of the story.

### D. The Heading of 1568

The Bishops' Bible went back, at 2:19 and at 3:1, on what the Great Bible and the Geneva had gained, and the book has said so at those verses. It is nonetheless the one of the four that matters most at the seam. Three of the four English Bibles gave each chapter a summary of its contents. The Great Bible's summary of the second chapter is a list: the hallowing of the Sabbath, the four floods of Paradise, the setting in of man, the tree forbidden, the naming, the creation of Eve, the institution of marriage. The Geneva's is a list: "God resteth the seuenth day, and sanctifieth it. 15 He setteth man in the garden. 22 He createth the womā. 29 Mariage is ordeined."[^nb43ad] The Bishops' wrote: "2 The day of rest is blessed. 4 A repeatyng of thynges foretolde in the first. 6 The cloude from the earth as a well to water it. 7 He rehearseth agayne the fashionyng of man. 8 Man in the paradise of pleasure …"[^nb43ae] Two of its eleven lines say what the other summaries do not: that verse 4 begins a repeating of things foretold in the first chapter, and that verse 7 rehearses again the fashioning of man.

The book does not claim that the Bishops' translators held the reading this book argues, or that "repeatyng" meant to them what "one narrative told twice" means here. It claims what the leaf shows: that the Bible of the English church stated in its own heading that the second chapter goes back over the first, and that the heading stood at the head of Genesis 2 in the Bishops' folios of 1568, 1574 and 1578 and in the edition of 1575, and was gone from the edition of 1577 and from the folio of 1602.[^nb43ai] The Hebrew of the seam is a gap in the codex and the formula אֵלֶּה תוֹלְדוֹת; the Bishops' told the reader what kind of heading that formula is. Its margins elsewhere show the same translators reading Hebrew as Hebrew: "Hebre. buylded" at 2:22, "Heb. As before hym" at 2:18, and at 3:22 the one note in any version that tells the reader God's sentence is unfinished, "You may supplie the sentence with these wordes: let vs cast hym foorth."[^nb43af] Its supplements it marked in brackets, "[he made] starres also" at 1:16, "[shalbe]" at 3:16, so that the reader could see what was the translators' and what was the text's.[^nb43ag] Its notes read the story as the Geneva's did, the plural of 1:26 as "One God, and three persons," the image as what man "by sinne he afterwarde corrupted," the seed as "Victorie promised by Christe."[^nb43ah] The King James translators were instructed to revise this Bible and no other, and the copy they revised was the folio of 1602, in which the heading had already given way to the Great Bible's list. What they kept of the Bishops' and what they changed is the subject of the chapter that follows; the heading they never saw.

### E. What the English Before 1611 Is to the Reading

The four Bibles of 1535 to 1568 are the years in which the Hebrew's second telling came into English whole. Coverdale brought it in the Latin's shape, with the animals formed before the man, the man absent from the tree and the dust dispersed into three words. The Great Bible, five years later and against the Hebrew, restored all three, and the Geneva kept the restoration and added the Hebrew in its margins. The Bishops' lost the order again at 2:19 and said in its heading what the order is for. Between them they marked the Hebrew's turns in a range of English the King James would narrow, they made the losses at the soul and the wind that the King James would keep, and they handed on, in the Bishops' text of 1568, a pluperfect to be struck, and a heading its own printers had already let go. The book takes from these years the recoveries of 1540, the Geneva's margins and its "help meet," and the Bishops' heading, and it reads the King James, in the next chapter, as what its translators made of them.
