## 4.4 The King James and After: The Levelling, the Margins, and the Modern Return

The King James Bible of 1611 is the English text of record in this book, and Parts 1 to 3 have quoted it at every verse from the facsimile of the first printing. This chapter reads it as a whole, and it reads it beside a second witness that the earlier chapters could not use: the copy of the Bishops' Bible of 1602 that one company of the King James translators marked up as they worked, now in the Bodleian Library, whose leaves for Genesis 1–4 are on the shelf and have been read at the image.[^nb44a] The marks are in a secretary hand, they are one company's and not the final text, and the author, who does not read the hand with ease, gives the readings below as the author's own and holds them for professional review; where the 1611 did what the mark proposes, the mark is cited, and where it did not, the difference is said.[^nb44b]

The chapter finds four things. The translators revised the Bishops' text toward the Hebrew at the verbs and the words, and the marked copy shows them doing it. They levelled the Hebrew's scene-clauses to "And" at five of the seven, further than any English Bible before them and further than either ancient version, while supplying at other verses a chain of consequence the Hebrew does not have. They confessed the Hebrew in their margin at every place where the text departed from it, so that the King James is two texts, and the reader of the margin has the Hebrew the reader of the text has not. And the English versions since 1611, read here as witnesses only, have returned to the Hebrew's shape at the scene-clauses by returning to the Greek's practice, and have returned to Jerome's order at 2:8 and 2:19 by returning to his pluperfect.

### A. The Revision: The Translators at Work

The instruction to the King James translators was to revise the Bishops' Bible, and the copy of 1602 shows the revision being made at the verses this book has read. At 2:19 the Bishops' "the Lord God had shapen euery beast of the fielde" is underlined and "formed" is written in the margin; the 1611 reads "the LORD God formed."[^nb44c] At 2:18 and 2:20 the Bishops' "an helpe like vnto him" is struck and "meet for him" written above, the Geneva's phrase; the 1611 has "an helpe meet for him" at both.[^nb44d] At 3:1 the Bishops' "AND the Serpent" has its "And" struck through and "Now" written above, again the Geneva's; the 1611 has "Now the serpent."[^nb44e] At 3:15 the Bishops' two verbs, "tread downe" and "tread vpon," are struck and "bruise" written for each, so that the Hebrew's one verb, used twice, is twice "bruise"; the 1611 has "it shall bruise thy head, and thou shalt bruise his heele."[^nb44f] At 3:16 the Bishops' "But vnto the woman" has its "But" struck, leaving the sentence without a connective as the Hebrew has it, and "subiect" is entered above the clause "thy desire shall be to thy husband"; the 1611 prints the bare "Unto the woman he said" and puts "Or, subiect to thy husband" in the margin.[^nb44g] At 3:12 the Bishops' "And Adam sayd" has "the man" written against it; the 1611 has "And the man said," where the Bishops' had "Adam" and where the 1611 itself keeps "Adam" at 3:17, 3:20 and 3:21.[^nb44h] At 3:21 "he put them on" is struck for "clothed them," at 3:23 "dresse" for "till," at 3:17 "Unto Adam hee said" is given the "And" the 1611 prints.[^nb44i] And at two verses the marked copy shows what the translators left alone: the "And" of 1:2, "And the earth was without forme, and voyd," where the hand brackets a second "was" and touches nothing else, and the "And" of 4:1, "AND Adam knew Heua his wife," where the hand underlines "who conceiuing, bare" and leaves the connective alone.[^nb44j]

What the copy shows is a company reading the Hebrew for its words and its verbs and taking from the Geneva what the Geneva had got right, and not reading the Hebrew for the shape of its clauses. The pluperfect at 2:19 went because the Hebrew has a sequence verb; "bruise … bruise" came in because the Hebrew has one verb; "the man" at 3:12 because the Hebrew has the article; the bare opening of 3:16 because the Hebrew has no connective. Where the Hebrew opens a scene with a noun and a vav, at 1:2 and 4:1, the copy shows no mark at the connective at all. The translators were not levelling by decision. They were not looking.

### B. The Levelling, and the Chain of Consequence

The result at the seven scene-clauses between 1:1 and 4:1 is the record the Method stated. The 1611 has "But there went vp a mist" at 2:6 and "Now the serpent" at 3:1, and "And" at the other five: "And the earth was" (1:2), "And euery plant" (2:5), "And a riuer went out" (2:10), "And vnto Adam he said" (3:17), "And Adam knew" (4:1); at the mid-verse turn of 2:20 it has the Geneva's "but for Adam."[^nb44k] Two of seven is fewer than the Geneva's four or Coverdale's three, fewer than the Latin's five and the Greek's six. At 1:2 the translators chose "And" over the Great Bible's bare "The earth was" and the Vulgate's *autem*, both of which were before them; at 4:1 they chose "And" over Coverdale's "Morouer" and the Geneva's "Afterwarde"; at 3:17 over the Great Bible's and the Bishops' bare "Unto Adam" and the Geneva's "Also."[^nb44l] The two they marked are the two the sense forces, the mist after the drought and the serpent after the two naked, and both marks the Geneva had made before them. Where the sense did not force the issue the King James wrote what the Hebrew's letter says and not what its clause does, and it did so at more verses than any English Bible before it.

The King James is nonetheless not a flat text, and the book has said so at each place. In the first chapter its "And … and … and" is the Hebrew's, and exact; the variety of Coverdale and the Geneva was English variety over a Hebrew that has none.[^nb44m] At 1:27 it has "So God created man," from the Bishops'; at 2:1 "Thus the heauens," from the Geneva and Coverdale; at 2:24 "Therefore shall a man leaue"; at 3:6 "And when the woman saw … she tooke," a subordination no other version made; and at the close of the third chapter "Therefore the LORD God sent him foorth" and "So he droue out the man," both from the Geneva.[^nb44n] The 1611 prints "and" some two hundred and ten times in Genesis 1–3 and opens sixty-three of the eighty verses with it, and where it departs from "And" it departs toward consequence, "So," "Thus," "Therefore," at verses where the Hebrew merely continues.[^nb44o] The pattern is the reverse of the Hebrew's. The Hebrew marks the turns and runs the sequence flat; the King James runs the turns flat and marks the sequence with consequence. The reader who follows the King James's connectives is told that the driving out followed from the sentence, which is true and which the Hebrew does not say, and is not told that the earth of 1:2 and the serpent's knowledge of 4:1 are scenes, which the Hebrew does say.

Where the Hebrew has no connective the King James has none. At 2:4, "These are the generations," and at 3:16, "Unto the woman he said," the 1611 begins its sentence as the Hebrew begins it, and the Geneva had done so before it; at 2:4 it adds a pilcrow.[^nb44p] The translators read the absence of the vav exactly. It is its presence before a noun that they did not read.

### C. The Margins

The 1611 is two texts, and the second is in its margin. Wherever the translators smoothed the Hebrew in the text they wrote the Hebrew beside it, and the margin of Genesis 1–3 is a record of their choices that no English Bible before it kept so fully and no English Bible since has printed. At 1:5, where the text has "the euening and the morning were the first day," the margin has "Hebr. and the euening was, and the morning was."[^nb44q] At 1:20 the text has "the mouing creature that hath life" and the margin "Or, creeping" and "Heb. soule"; at 1:28, "euery liuing thing that mooueth" and "Heb. creepeth"; at 1:29 "Hebr. seeding seed."[^nb44r] At 2:3, "which God created and made," the margin has "Heb. created to make."[^nb44s] At 2:6, "But there went vp a mist," it offers "Or, a mist which went vp from &c."[^nb44t] At 2:7, "the dust of the ground," it says "Heb. dust of the ground," to mark the phrase as the Hebrew's own.[^nb44u] At 2:15 and 2:19 it has "Or, Adam" against "the man" and "Or, the man" against "Adam."[^nb44v] At 2:16 and 2:17, "thou mayest freely eate" and "thou shalt surely die," it has "Hebr. eating thou shalt eate" and "Hebr. dying thou shalt die."[^nb44w] At 2:18, "an helpe meet for him," it has "Hebr. as before him"; at 2:22, "made hee a woman," "Hebr. builded."[^nb44x] At 3:1, "Yea, hath God said," it has "Heb. Yea, because, &c."; at 3:6, "pleasant to the eyes," "Heb. a desire"; at 3:7, "aprons," "Or, things to gird about."[^nb44y] At 3:8, "the coole of the day," it has "Heb. wind."[^nb44z] At 3:16, "thy desire shall be to thy husband," it has "Or, subiect to thy husband."[^nb44aa] At 3:20, "Eue," it has "Heb. Chauah."[^nb44ab]

Read together, the margins are the translators' account of where their English is not the Hebrew, and at three of these places the book's reading depends on the margin against the text: the wind of 3:8, which the text has made into weather; the idiom of 2:17, which the text has made into an adverb; and the subjection of 3:16, which the text does not have and the margin offers. At a fourth, the dust of 2:7, the margin confirms that the text's phrase is the Hebrew's own. The reader of the King James text has "the coole of the day" and "surely die" and "thy desire shall be to thy husband"; the reader of the King James margin has "wind" and "dying thou shalt die" and "subiect." The book has read the margin as part of the version, because the translators printed it as part of the version, and it has taken the margin's Hebrew over the text's English wherever the two differ.

The 1611 also kept the Bishops' practice of a summary at the head of each chapter, and wrote its own. The first chapter's runs from "The creation of Heauen and Earth" to "Also the appointment of food"; the second's, "1 The first Sabbath. 4 The maner of the creation. 8 The planting of the garden of Eden, 10 and the riuer thereof. 17 The tree of knowledge onely forbidden. 19. 20 The naming of the creatures. 21 The making of woman, and institution of Mariage"; the third's, "1 The serpent deceiueth Eue. 6 Mans shamefull fall. 9 God arraigneth them. 14 The serpent is cursed. 15 The promised Seed. 16 The punishment of Mankind. 21 Their first clothing. 22 Their casting out of Paradise."[^nb44ac] Chapter 1.3 has told what "The maner of the creation" replaced and what it does not say. The running heads over the leaves say the same in fewer words: "The creation of the world," "The creation of man," "The first Sabbath," "Mariage instituted," "The fall of man," "The promised seed."[^nb44ad]

### D. Two Centuries, and the Modern Return

The King James was the English Bible for some two hundred and seventy years before a revision of it was published, and its "And the earth was," "And euery plant," "And a riuer," "And vnto Adam," "And Adam knew" were the English reader's Genesis for the whole of that time.[^nb44ae] The book has not read the versions of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries at any page, and it does not treat them as witnesses to the Hebrew. It has read thirteen of them as edition text, in the public-domain files on the shelf, at the verses the Method marks, and it records what they show as a history of the English reader's connective after 1611, no more.[^nb44af]

At the scene-clauses the record is this. "Now the serpent" at 3:1 never left English after 1560: it is in the King James, the American King James, Webster's revision of 1833, the English Revised Version of 1885, the American Standard of 1901, the Jewish Publication Society's of 1917, the Douay-Rheims, and the two free modern versions on the shelf, the World English Bible and the Berean Standard Bible; only the literal school, Darby, Young and Julia Smith, has "And the serpent," and the Catholic Public Domain Version "However."[^nb44ag] "Now the earth" at 1:2 returns in the Jewish Publication Society's version of 1917 and in the World English and Berean Standard Bibles; the Catholic Public Domain Version has "But the earth"; every King James-lineage text and both English revisions keep "And the earth."[^nb44ah] At 2:5 the revisions of 1885 and 1901 and Young have "And no"; the Jewish version has "No shrub" and the World English "No plant," dropping the connective as the Great Bible did at 1:2; and the Berean has "Now no," the first English text on the shelf to write the particle at the verse where the Greek, the Latin and every English Bible before had missed it.[^nb44ai] At 4:1 the King James lineage keeps "And Adam knew," the revisions "And the man," and the World English alone drops the connective, "The man knew."[^nb44aj] The return of "now" to the noun-first clauses in the twentieth century is a return to what the Septuagint did in the third century before Christ and the Vulgate did at five of the seven, and the book has said in chapter 4.1 whose practice it is.

At the two verses where the order of the second telling is at stake the record runs the other way. Of the thirteen texts on the shelf the two Catholic versions carry Jerome: the Douay-Rheims has "the Lord God had planted a paradise of pleasure from the beginning" and "the Lord God having formed out of the ground all the beasts," the Catholic Public Domain Version "Now the Lord God had planted" and "Therefore, the Lord God, having formed"; Darby alone of the Protestant texts has "had formed" at 2:19; the other ten have the King James's simple past in sequence, "planted" and "formed," or Young's present and Smith's future, which keep the order as well.[^nb44ak] The "Now the LORD God had planted" and "Now the LORD God had formed" that the book's predecessor quoted as its text are the New International Version's, which is in copyright and not on the shelf, and the book cites them as the predecessor quoted them and as what Parts 2 and 3 have read them to be: Jerome's pluperfect of 2:8 and his ablative absolute of 2:19, come into English by way of a "now" that the Hebrew's clause does not have.[^nb44al] The two modern returns are therefore of opposite value to this book's reading. The return of "now" to 1:2 and 2:5 and 4:1 restores the Hebrew's shape. The return of "had" to 2:8 and 2:19 restores the Latin's order.

At the words the thirteen texts show what the book has said in Parts 1 to 3 becoming the English reader's settled text. "Dust" at 2:7 and 3:14 and 3:19 is in every text but the two Catholic versions, which have "slime" and "clay" at 2:7 and "earth" at 3:14 from the Latin.[^nb44am] "Living soul" at 2:7 holds in eleven texts and gives way to "living creature" in Young and "living being" in the Berean.[^nb44an] "Subdue" at 1:28 is in all thirteen.[^nb44ao] "Help meet" runs from the Geneva through the King James to the revisions of 1885 and 1901 and the Jewish version of 1917, and is dropped by every later text on the shelf, for "helper," "helpmate," "helper suitable for him."[^nb44ap] "As gods" at 3:5 holds in the King James lineage and the Douay; the revisions and the moderns have "as God" or "like God," singular again.[^nb44aq] "Subtil" holds in the King James lineage and the revisions; "crafty," which no version before the nineteenth century had, is Darby's, Julia Smith's, the Catholic Public Domain Version's and the Berean's.[^nb44ar] The Vulgate's *ipsa* survives in English only in the two Catholic texts, "she shall crush," and the Vulgate's *sub viri potestate* only in the same two, "under thy husband's power"; every other text has "it" or "he" at 3:15 and "desire" at 3:16.[^nb44as] The Latin's two readings that are its own have stayed the Latin's.

### E. What the King James Is to the Reading

The King James is this book's English because it is the English in which the Hebrew's order at 2:19 was restored against the Bishops', in which the Hebrew's one verb at 3:15 was rendered by one verb for the first time, in which the Hebrew's bare opening at 3:16 was kept and its subjection moved to the margin, and in which the Hebrew itself was confessed, at every departure, in a margin the reader may hold against the text. It is also the English in which the Hebrew's scene-clauses were levelled at five of seven, further than any version before it, by translators who read the Hebrew for its words and not for the shape of its clauses; the marked copy of 1602 shows them doing the one and not the other. The book reads the King James as its text and the King James's margin as its corrector, and where the two are silent together, at "And the earth was" and "And Adam knew," it reads the Hebrew's clause and says so. The versions' history closes where this book began: the versions divide at the places the Hebrew's clause-shape marks, and the version that levelled most at those places is the one that recorded the Hebrew most faithfully everywhere else.

# Conclusion

Genesis 1–3 is one narrative told twice. The first telling gives the frame, six days and a rest, and ends with a man and a woman made in the image of God and the whole judged very good. The second telling goes back over the sixth day and gives the detail: the man formed, then the garden and its trees, then the animals, then the woman; the commandment given to the man alone; the serpent's account of the knowledge, given to the woman who had not heard the commandment, and true; the eating, with the man present; the examination and the sentences in the order the examination set; and God's declaration that the man has become as one of us, to know good and evil, which is the image of the sixth day achieved. The rest of the seventh day follows, in the order of events, everything the second telling narrates. That is the reading, and the book has taken it from the shape of the Hebrew clauses, one clause at a time, and has set down at every clause what the versions did.

The versions divide into those that kept the order of the second telling and those that rearranged it, and the line between them does not run where a reader might expect. The Septuagint, the oldest of them, kept the order and twice added a word to insist on it. The Vulgate, the version of the West for eleven hundred years, moved the garden before the man and the animals into the background, and is the ancestor of every English version since that reads "had planted" and "had formed." The first English Bible followed the Latin; the Great Bible of 1540 restored the Hebrew's order, its man at the tree and its one word for dust; the Geneva kept the restoration; the Bishops' lost the order again at one verse and said in its heading what the order is for; the King James restored the verse and, by the time it was made, had never seen the heading. The modern versions have returned in two directions at once, to the Hebrew's shape at the scene-clauses and to the Latin's order at the two verses where the order matters most. No version kept everything. The Greek kept the order and lost the words; the Latin kept the words and lost the order; the English recovered the words in 1540 and levelled the shape in 1611.

A reader who follows the shape of each clause finds four things the tradition's readings of these chapters have not rested on. The first is that the second telling is told as sequence throughout, without one clause that lifts an event out of its place, and that its order is therefore the order of the events. The second is that the commandment was given to one person, in the singular, before the woman existed, and that the text never says she heard it from anyone but the serpent. The third is that the man was there, "with her," when she ate, and that the two versions which lost that word, the Latin and the first English Bible, are the two in which the man could be supposed elsewhere. The fourth is that God's own sentence at 3:22 says what the man has become, in the words of 1:26, and that the versions kept the sentence whole. None of these is an interpretation laid over the text. Each is a clause read for its shape and a word read at its place, and each was in the Hebrew before any version touched it.

What follows for the doctrine the book states in a paragraph and hands on. The teaching that these chapters record a fall from a finished perfection into sin, transmitted to every descendant, rests on readings this book has found to be the versions' and not the text's: on the Latin's order, in which the garden and its trees were made before the man and the man was set down in a finished world; on the Latin's *ipsa* at 3:15 and its *sub viri potestate* at 3:16, which the Hebrew and the Greek do not contain; on a "because" at 3:16 that no version supplies and the Hebrew does not have; and on a reading of 3:22 that hears irony where every version but one heard a declaration. The book does not answer the doctrine here. It has read the text, and the reading is that the knowledge of good and evil is what the image of God required and what the sixth day, told in full, was for. What that means for the teaching of the church, and for Augustine, who built the teaching on the Latin, is the subject of the companion volume, and the reader who has followed the clauses this far will know where that argument has to begin.

The book's last word is about its method, because the method is what it has to offer beyond the reading. Every claim in it about a version is a claim about a leaf, and the leaf is cited. Every reading of the Hebrew is a reading of the codex, made by an author who does not read the language and says so, and held for the review of those who do. The book has found the versions to be other than the author's own earlier essays took them to be: the Greek those essays said had flattened the Hebrew's connective marked its turns more faithfully than any English Bible before the twentieth century; the Latin those essays said lacked the Hebrew's flexibility read its clauses with care and moved its events; and the King James, which those essays set beside modern versions as though the moderns were the text, levelled the Hebrew's scene-clauses further than any version before it and confessed the Hebrew in its margin at every other place. The reader who wants to know what Genesis 1–3 says is sent, by this book, to the page: to the codex for the shape of the sentences, to the versions for what was made of them, and to the margin of the King James for the translators' own account of where their English is not the Hebrew. What the page shows is one narrative told twice, and a knowledge that was promised, taken, confirmed, and not called a fall by anyone in the text.

The tables below gather what the chapters have cited at their verses, for the reader who wants the witnesses side by side. The witnesses and their leaves are tabulated in Method § C and are not repeated here. A cell reads "—" where the book did not record that witness at that place; the book's readings are of the leaves cited in the chapters, and a blank here is a blank in the book, not in the witness. Early English spelling is as printed; "none" means the witness begins the clause with no connective.

### B. The Three Words for the Ground

אֶרֶץ (earth), אֲדָמָה (ground) and עָפָר (dust) at the verses the book reads. Where a verse has more than one of the words, the cells give them in the order of the Hebrew, separated by a slash.

| Verse | Hebrew | Septuagint | Vulgate | Coverdale | Great Bible | Geneva | Bishops' | 1611 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1:1–2 | אֶרֶץ | γῆ | terra | earth | earth | earth | earth | earth |
| 2:5 | אֶרֶץ / אֲדָמָה | γῆ / γῆ | terra / terram | earth / earth | earth / grounde | earth / grounde | earth / grounde | earth / ground |
| 2:6 | אֶרֶץ / אֲדָמָה | γῆ / γῆ | terra / terræ | — / — | earth / — | earth / — | earth / grounde | earth / ground |
| 2:7 | עָפָר / אֲדָמָה | χοῦν / γῆς | limo / terræ | moulde / earth | dust / grounde | dust / grounde | dust / grounde | dust / ground ("Heb. dust of the ground") |
| 2:9 | אֲדָמָה | γῆς | humo | earth | grounde | grounde | grounde | ground |
| 2:19 | אֲדָמָה | γῆς | humo | earth | grounde | earth | grounde | ground |
| 3:14 | עָפָר | γῆν | terram | earth | dust | dust | dust | dust |
| 3:17 | אֲדָמָה | γῆ | terra | earth | grounde | earth | grounde | ground |
| 3:19 | אֲדָמָה / עָפָר / עָפָר | γῆν / γῆ / γῆν | terram / puluis / puluerem | — / earth / earth | grounde / dust / dust | earth / dust / dust | ground / dust / dust | ground / dust / dust |
| 3:23 | אֲדָמָה | γῆν | terram | — | grounde | earth | grounde | ground |

In the Hebrew and in the English from 1540 the man is made of dust, the serpent eats dust and the man returns to dust in one word; in the Greek the three are one word with the ground; in the Latin they are three words; in Coverdale they are two. The Geneva, which keeps "grounde" in chapter 2, has "earth" for אֲדָמָה in chapter 3, and the 1611 restores "ground." Sources: chapters 2.1 § B, 2.2 §§ A–B, 2.3 § B, 3.3 §§ A, C, 3.4 § D, 4.1 § C, 4.2 § C, 4.3 § B; Bishops', leaf 56 (3:19, 3:23).

### E. Witnesses and Image Sources

Every reading in this book is cited at a leaf, and this table records where the leaves are. The copy is named as the shelf names it; the identifier is the one the holding library or archive assigns, so that a reader may reach the same image set.
